# Lemaire: text and notes

English translation of the notes in [ilias.md](ilias.md); see [README.md](README.md).

## p. 515

PDF page 525.

Title: XIV. / INCERTI AUCTORIS / (VULGO PINDARI THEBANI) / EPITOME ILIADOS HOMERI.

| Verse | Wernsdorf | Text | Printed |
|---|---|---|---|
| 1 | 1 | Iram pande mihi Pelidae, Diva, superbi, |  |
| 2 | 2 | Tristia quae miseris injecit funera Graiis , |  |
| 3 | 3 | Atque animas fortes heroum tradidit Orco, |  |
| 4 | 4 | Latrantumque dedit rostris volucrumque trahendos |  |
| 5 | 5 | Illorum exsangues inhumatis ossibus artus. | 5 |
| 6 | 6 | Confiebat enim summi sententia regis, |  |

### Notes

- **2** *Injecit funera* [inflicted death]. The older poets say *immittere* or *dare funera*. Virg. *Aen.* X, 13: « Quum fera Carthago Romanis arcibus olim Exitium magnum atque Alpes immittet apertas »; Val. Flaccus, III, 681: « nec enim solis dare funera Colchis Sit satis ». — Plautus, however, said *objicere funera*, *Amphitr.* I, 1, 35: « Qui multa Thebano populo objecit funera ». Ed.
- **4** *Volucrumque trahendos* [and to be torn by birds]. P. Bondam, *Var. Lect.* II, 4, observes that in these words the author imitated Ovid, *Ib.* 171: « Unguibus et rostro tardus trahet ilia vultur, Et scindent avidae perfida corda canes ».
- **5** *Exsangues inhumatis ossibus* [lifeless, with unburied bones]. Virgil, *Aen.* XI, 22: « socios inhumataque corpora terrae Mandemus ». Ovid, *Her.* XI, 123: « Ossa superstabunt volucres inhumata marinae ».
- **6** All the written and printed copies indeed have *Conficiebat enim*, but there is no doubt that a corruption is present, since a complete sense is not contained in the words, nor does it appear what the decree of Jove accomplished. Barth observed this, *Adv.* LVIII, 14, and believed that the defect lay in the word *enim*, but dared to change nothing, being destitute of the aid of manuscripts. But by a happy conjecture Jo. van der Dussen emended in *Prodr. novae Pind. Theb. ed.* p. 2: *Confiebat enim*, which emendation of his I myself follow. The copy-

## p. 516

PDF page 526.

| Verse | Wernsdorf | Text | Printed |
|---|---|---|---|
| 7 | 7 | Ex quo contulerant discordi pectore pugnas |  |
| 8 | 8 | Sceptriger Atrides, et bello clarus Achilles. |  |
| 9 | 9 | Quis Deus hos ira tristi contendere jussit? |  |
| 10 | 10 | Latonae et magni proles Jovis. Ille Pelasgum | 10 |
| 11 | 11 | Infestus regi pestem in praecordia misit, |  |
| 12 | 12 | Implicuitque gravi Danaorum corpora morbo. |  |
| 13 | 13 | Nam quondam Chryses solenni tempora vitta |  |
| 14 | 14 | Implicitus, raptae flevit solatia natae, |  |
| 15 | 15 | Invisosque dies invisaque tempora noctis | 15 |
| 16 | 16 | Egit, et assiduis implevit questibus auras. |  |

### Notes

- **(cont.)** [Copy-]ists in other places have often interchanged the verbs *confieri* and *conficere*. The verb *confiebat* expresses that Greek phrase of Homer, Διὸς δ᾽ ἐτελείετο βουλή, most excellently, and Virgil uses that verb in a similar way, *Aeneid* IV, 116: « nunc qua ratione, quod instat, Confieri possit, paucis, adverte, docebo ». Lucretius also uses it, III, 413: « Id quoque enim sine pernicie confiet eorum »; the same author, IV, 292: « quoniam res confit utroque ».
- **7** Cod. Guelf. 1 reads *Pertulerant ex quo discordia pectora pugnas*; Guelf. 2 *Ex quo pertulerant*. But without doubt what the other copies have is more correct: *Ex quo contulerant pugnas*. Thus Virg. *Aen.* X, 146: « Illi inter sese duri certamina belli Contulerant ».
- **9** Guelf. 1 and 2 read *Quis Deus hos ira tristi*, which I deservedly prefer to the reading of the rest, *fato tristi*. For if the author had wished to name fate here, he ought not to have asked *quis Deus* [what god]. For above he has already spoken of the will of Jove, which is fate itself. And Homer's words require this: ἔριδι ξυνέηκε μάχεσθαι.
- **10** G. 1 and 2 offer *Ille Pelasgum*; the old Leipzig edition and the Basel edition of Torinus have *iste Pelasgum*.
- **11** *Infestam regi* G. 1. *Infestam regis* Annaberg, Torinus, and the rest; but we must by all means read, as Dussen *loc. cit.* p. 4 correctly saw, *Infestus regi pestem in praecordia misit*. For this is what the words of Homer mean, v. 9: ὃ γὰρ βασιλῆϊ χολωθείς, and the author himself confirms it below, v. 55: « Haec ait: Infesti placemus numina Phoebi ». Guelf. 1 also supports this, which puts the dative *regi*. — *Pestem in praecordia misit* [sent a plague into their breasts]: namely wrath, which was to be pestilential to them. Thus in Ovid, *Metam.* VIII, 791, Ceres says of the hunger sent upon Erysichthon: « ea se in praecordia condat Sacrilegi scelerata jube ».
- **12** *Implicuit morbo* [entangled in disease]. Truly an inelegant expression, and it offends all the more because almost immediately in v. 14 *implicitus* recurs. Ed.
- **13** *Solenni tempora vitta Implicitus* [His temples bound with the ceremonial fillet]. He calls the fillet (*vitta*) the badge of priesthood bound to the head, which had to be solemnly used in the sacrifices and ministries of Apollo, just as the priest of Ceres in Ovid, *Met.* V, 110, is said to be « albenti velatus tempora vitta ». From this it is evident that what G. 1 writes, *solenni tempore*, is faulty.
- **15** *Invisosque dies invisaque* [both hated days and hated...]. G. 2 has *Infestosque dies*, incorrectly. That Chryses lamented days and nights

## p. 517

PDF page 527.

| Verse | Wernsdorf | Text | Printed |
|---|---|---|---|
| 17 | 17 | Postquam nulla dies animum moerore levabat, |  |
| 18 | 18 | Nullaque lenibant patrios solatia fletus, |  |
| 19 | 19 | Castra petit Danaum, genibusque adfusus Atridae, |  |
| 20 | 20 | Per Superos regnique decus miserabilis orat, | 20 |
| 21 | 21 | Ut sibi caussa suse reddatur nata salutis: |  |
| 22 | 22 | Dona simul profert : vincuntur fletibus ejus |  |
| 23 | 23 | Myrmidones , reddique patri Chryseida censent. |  |
| 24 | 24 | Sed negat Atrides, Chrysenque excedere castris |  |
| 25 | 25 | Despecta pietate jubet : ferus ossibus imis | 25 |
| 26 | 26 | Haeret amor, spernitque preces damnosa libido. |  |
| 27 | 27 | Contemptus repetit Phoebeia templa sacerdos,- |  |
| 28 | 28 | Squalidaque infestis moerens secat unguibus ora, |  |

### Notes

- **(cont.)** (cont.) the author signifies in this passage regarding Chryses, perhaps having imitated Virgil, who says of Orpheus, *Georg.* IV, v. 464: « Ipse cava solans aegrum testudine amorem, Te dulcis conjux, te solo in litore secum, Te veniente die, te decedente canebat ». Homer did not touch upon the duration and perpetuity of the grief; on the contrary, he depicts Chryses walking alone on the shore; what Virgil expressed in Orpheus, our author omitted. Poets, and especially Virgil, are accustomed to describe light or life as hateful (*invisam*) to mourners, as in *Aen.* IV, 631, XII, 177.
- **17** *Postquam nulla dies* [After no day...]. Barth, *Advers.* LVIII, 14, asserts that these following five verses were written with remarkable talent and judgment, and admires their sweetness. — The vulgate editions have *levavit*, but G. 1 and 2 *levabat*, which I prefer on account of the following *lenibant*. In the former manuscript, a later hand added in the margin: "Al. replevit", which is absurd.
- **19** *Genibus profusus Atridis* G. 1.
- **21** *Ut sibi caussa suae* [That to him as the cause of her own...]. In Ovid, *Met.* VI, 499, Pandion says of his daughter: « Per Superos oro ..... Et mihi sollicitae lenimen dulce senectae Quamprimum (omnis erit nobis mora longa) remittas ».
- **23** *Myrmidones*. Barth *loc. cit.* remarks, not circumspectly enough, that "Myrmidons" is used by this author for all Greeks, although Virgil uses Danai, Argives, Pelasgians for the same; but that this, because novel, cannot please; furthermore, that the case of the Myrmidons is different, since in their name they display an entirely peculiar tribal origin, to extend which to the rest gives the appearance of someone ignorant of history.
- **26** *Haeret amor, spernitque* [Love clings, and spurns]: so G. 1 and 2. *Sprevitque* Annaberg. *Haesit amor sprevitque* Leipzig, Torinus. To the renowned P. Bondam this seems expressed from Ovid, *Heroid.* IV, 70: « Acer in extremis ossibus haesit amor », who also writes in *Metam.* III, 395: « Sed tamen haeret amor ». — *Damnosa libido* [ruinous lust] is from Horace, *Epist.* II, 1, 107.
- **27** G. 2 reads *Contempto repetit*.
- **28** *Secat unguibus ora* [tears his face with nails]. Bondam thinks this is owed to Ovid, *Her.* V, 72: « Et secui madidas ungue rigente genas »; the same poet used the phrase *squalida ora*

## p. 518

PDF page 528.

| Verse | Wernsdorf | Text | Printed |
|---|---|---|---|
| 29 | 29 | Dilaceratque coinas, annosaque pectora plangit. |  |
| 30 | 30 | Mox ubi depositi gemitus, lacrymaeque quierunt, | 30 |
| 31 | 31 | Fatidici sacras compellat vocibus aras : |  |
| 32 | 32 | « Quid coluisse mihi tua numina, Delphice , prodest , |  |
| 33 | 33 | Aut castam vitam multos duxisse per annos? |  |
| 34 | 34 | Quidve juvat sacros posuisse altaribus ignes, |  |
| 35 | 35 | Si tuus externo jam spernor ab hoste sacerdos? | 35 |
| 36 | 36 | En haec desertae redduntur dona senectae. |  |

### Notes

- **(cont.)** (cont.) in *Trist.* IV, 2, 34. Below, v. 1012: « arat unguibus ora ».
- **29** *Annosaque tempora* [and his aged temples]: so indeed all the copies; yet Burmann on *Anthol. Lat.* IV, 224 rightly thinks we should read *annosaque pectora* [and his aged breast]. For to beat one's temples (*tempora plangere*) is certainly unusual. And thus Ovid, *Metam.* VI, 248: « laniataque pectora plangens », where *tangens* is corruptly read in cod. Leid., and *Amor.* III, 9, 10: « Pectoraque infesta tundat aperta manu ». Burmann noted on Val. Flacc. II, 324 that *tempora* and *pectora* are very frequently confused by scribes.
- **31** G. 2 faultily reads *Compellit vocibus*. Moreover, all copies exhibit *Vatidicis vocibus*, not well, since it is not fitting that the prayers of Chryses be called *vatidicae* [prophetic], and certainly the name of Apollo is required here. Therefore, in agreement with Dussen, I have most gladly emended here to *Fatidici*, that is, of Apollo. Our author is accustomed to designate his gods by the name of their office alone, as Jupiter by *rex*, Vulcan by *ignipotens*, Minerva by *armigera*. That the name *Fatidicus* belongs to Apollo because he issues oracles and speaks future things by foretelling is common knowledge. Ovid expressly calls Phoebus so, *Fast.* II, 262, and V, 626. And the title *augur*, which Horace, *Carm.* I, 2, 32, attributes to Apollo, points to the same thing. Furthermore, in place of *sacras aras*, which the vulgate editions have, upon the recommendation of the same Dussen (though made more diffidently), I would prefer to substitute *sacras aures* [sacred ears]. For to bear prayers to sacred ears, whether of Princes or of Gods, is a common formula, especially in a later age of Latinity. See what we noted on Calpurnius I, last verse; and ears inclined to suppliants are especially attributed to Apollo. Hence in prayers poured forth to Apollo the exclamation is particularly frequent: as here in Homer, Κλῦθί μευ, so among the Latins, *Audi Apollo*. See Horace, *Carm. Saec.* v. 34. Indeed, among the Spartans Apollo was called τετράωτος [four-eared], and his statue was fashioned with four ears, as Gyraldus noted, *Histor. Deor. Syntagm.* VII, near the beginning.
- **36** Dussen suggests reading *An haec?* with a question mark, which I personally do not consider better than the vulgate *En*. That other change is more necessary, namely that in place of the vulgate reading of the editions, *defectae*, we read *desertae senectae* [forsaken old age], as codd. G. 1 and 2 and the Annaberg edition offer. For this word is almost proper and most customary for old age, and especially suited to this passage, where there is discussion of the loss of a daughter. — Thus in *Epitaph. Luceii*, vol. II of this work, p. 263: « Me desolatum, me desertum ac spoliatum Clamarem ». See what we noted on this passage, and on Petronius' *Bellum civile*, vs. 286. Ed.

## p. 519

PDF page 529.

| Verse | Wernsdorf | Text | Printed |
|---|---|---|---|
| 37 | 37 | Si gratus tibi sum, sim te sub judice tutus, |  |
| 38 | 38 | Aut si quam ut luerem sub acerbo crimine poenam |  |
| 39 | 39 | Inscius admisi, cur o tua dextera cessat? |  |
| 40 | 40 | Posce sacros arcus ; in me tua dirige tela : | 40 |
| 41 | 41 | Auctor mortis eris certe Deus : ecce merentem |  |
| 42 | 42 | Fige patrem : cur nata luit peccata parentis, |  |
| 43 | 43 | Atque hostis duri patitur miseranda cubile?» |  |
| 44 | 44 | Dixerat. Ille sui vatis prece motus, acerbis |  |
| 45 | 45 | Luctibus infestat Danaos , pestemque per omnes | 45 |

### Notes

- **37** Guelf. 1 transposes *Si tibi sum gratus*. Dussen prefers *vindice* [avenger] instead of *judice* [judge], which would seem to me also more suitable and worthy of acceptance, if only it were supported by some manuscript authority.
- **38** The printed editions read *Aut si quam ut ..... poenam*. Both Guelf. manuscripts read *Aut si quid ut ..... poenas*. On their authority I would indeed venture to write: *Aut si quid, luerem ut sub acerbo crimine poenas Inscius admisi*; nor yet would I reject Dussen's correction, *Aut si qua, ut luerem*. Meanwhile, I retain the vulgate reading, since it too has means by which it can defend itself. As for the rest, what the author wrote in this and the following three verses, Barth, *Advers.* LVIII, 14, judges to be exceedingly beautiful and worthy of a better age, demonstrating the greatest felicity of talent. But it is also clear, as Bondam and Dussen have already observed, that in them he looked back to and imitated these complaints of Tellus to Jupiter during the Phaethontic conflagration, in Ovid, *Met.* II, 279: « Si placet hoc meruique, quid o tua fulmina cessant, Summe Deum? liceat periturae viribus ignis Igne perire tuo, clademque auctore levare ».
- **39** G. 2 reads *Cur jam tua*; but the vulgate reading *cur o tua* is more elegant, and is defended by the Ovidian imitation.
- **40** *Posce sacros arcus* [Demand your sacred bow]. A similar speech of Choroebus to Apollo is found in Stat. *Theb.* I, 651: « Quid meruere Argi? me me, Divum optime, solum Objecisse caput fatis praestabit »; and v. 658: « Proinde move pharetras, arcusque intende sonoros, Insignemque animam leto demitte ».
- **41** For *ecce merentem* [behold, one who deserves it], Dussen tries *atque merentem*, so that *atque* has the force of *statim* [immediately] and indicates a prompt willingness of mind. Soon, abandoning this, he substituted *hisce merentem*, namely by these weapons that preceded. To me the vulgate reading seems good enough, and by the word *ecce* the same sense seems to be conveyed as he intends by *atque*.
- **43** *Patitur miseranda cubile* [she, pitiable, endures his bed]. Thus *pati hymenaeos* Virg. *Georg.* III, 60; *pati concubitus* Ovid, *Art. Am.* III, 766; *pati servitium*, the same author, *Art. Am.* III, 486. — And our author perhaps wished to express Homer, who in *Iliad* XVIII, 433 has: καὶ ἔτλην ἀνέρος εὐνήν. Ed.
- **45** *Luctibus infestat Danaos* [He harries the Danai with griefs], that is, vexes, lays waste. Often *infestare* is also used for *infestum facere* [to make hostile / harass]; Sil. Ital. II, 277: « Ductorem infestans odiis ». Ed.

## p. 520

PDF page 530.

| Verse | Wernsdorf | Text | Printed |
|---|---|---|---|
| 46 | 46 | Immittit populos : vulgus ruit undique Graium, |  |
| 47 | 47 | Vixque rogis superest tellus, vix ignibus arbor: |  |
| 48 | 48 | Deerat ager tumulis : jam noctis sidera nonae |  |
| 49 | 49 | Transierant, decimusque dies patefecerat orbem; |  |
| 50 | 50 | Tunc Danaum proceres in coetum clarus Achilles | 50 |
| 51 | 51 | Convocat, et caussas hortatur pestis iniquae |  |
| 52 | 52 | Edere Thestoridem. Tunc Calchas numina Divum |  |
| 53 | 53 | Consulit,et caussas pariter finemque malorum |  |
| 54 | 54 | Invenit, effarique verens , ope tutus Achillis |  |
| 55 | 55 | Haec ait : « Infesti placetnus numina Phoebi , | 55 |
| 56 | 56 | Reddamusque pio castam Chryseida patri, |  |
| 57 | 57 | Si volumus Danai portus intrare salutis ». |  |
| 58 | 58 | Dixerat, exarsit subito violentia regis. |  |

### Notes

- **46** *Vulgus ruit undique* [The common throng collapses on all sides], that is, falls, perishes dying; as Seneca, *Oedipus* v. 53: « sed omnis aetas pariter et sexus ruit ». Barth *loc. cit.* admits that this description of the slaughter is poetic and vivid. But Bondam and Dussen, along with Barth himself, recognize that it is owed to Ovid. For so Ovid, *Metam.* VII, 611: « Qui lacryment, desunt, indefletaeque vagantur Natarum matrumque animae, juvenesque senesque. Nec locus in tumulos, nec sufficit arbor in ignes ». There may be added Manilius, speaking of the Attic plague, book I, 883 ff., and Seneca in *Oedipus* on the Theban carnage, act I, v. 37 ff., which is also very similar to the passage of Ovid.
- **47** *Vix ignibus aether* [Scarcely the air for the fires...]. G. 1 and 2 have *ignibus aer*, Annaberg *ager*; but corrected to *aer* or *aether*. But Schrader detected a corruption in these, *Observ.* book I, ch. 3, by reading *vix ignibus arbor*, an emendation which is not only manifestly confirmed by Ovid's words *nec sufficit arbor in ignes*, but was also particularly approved by Anton de Rooy, *Spicileg. Crit.* ch. 16, p. 96, and corroborated by several passages of the poets. Of these, this passage of Seneca in *Oed.* v. 68 is especially suited to the words of our author: « Pars quota in cineres abit? Deest terra tumulis: jam rogos silvae negant ».
- **49** *Patefecerat orbem* [had revealed the world]. And this, as Barth notes, he takes from Ovid, *Metam.* IX, 794: « Postera lux radiis latum patefecerat orbem ».
- **51** *Caussas hortatur*. G. 1 writes *casus hortatur*, but in the margin *causas* is read.
- **52** G. 1 has *Thestoriden*; G. 2 *Tum Calchas*; Annaberg has *Thestoridam, tunc Calchas*; Torinus has *Testoridem*.
- **54** G. 1 has *Invenit farique verens*; Annaberg has *Effari verens*.
- **57** *Portus intrare salutis* [to enter the harbors of safety]. On these words Barth writes: "It belongs to a certain corruption of the age that he said *portum salutis intrare* for being freed from the pestilence, in which passage a poet of the greater rank would certainly have written otherwise." "I for my part, whether the author [used] that

## p. 521

PDF page 531.

| Verse | Wernsdorf | Text | Printed |
|---|---|---|---|
| 59 | 59 | Thestoridem dictis primum compellat amaris, |  |
| 60 | 60 | Mendacemque vocat, magnumque incusat Achillem, | 60 |
| 61 | 61 | Inque vicem ducis invicti convicia sufFert. |  |
| 62 | 62 | Confremuere omnes : tandem clamore represso |  |
| 63 | 63 | Cogitur invitos aeger dimittere amores, |  |
| 64 | 64 | Intactamque pio reddit Chryseida patri , |  |
| 65 | 65 | Multaque dona super : quam cunctis notus Ulysses | 65 |
| 66 | 66 | Impositam puppi patrias devexit ad arces, |  |
| 67 | 67 | Atque iterum ad classes Danaum sua vela retorsit. |  |
| 68 | 68 | Protinus infesti placantur numina Phoebi, |  |

### Notes

- **(cont.)** [phrase suitably or not,] I shall not dispute; the phrase itself can be defended. That *intrare portum* [to enter port] is good enough and customary, no one will deny, and Virgil and Ovid have used it. And *portus salutis* [harbor of safety] is from the same Ovid, who in *Rem. amoris*, v. 610, has: « Inque suae portu poene salutis erat »."
- **59** *Primum compellat* [He first addresses]. G. 2 has *primo compellit*.
- **60** *Magnumque incusat* [And reproaches great...]: so the vulgate editions. G. 1 better has *te magnum incusat Achillem* [reproaches you, great Achilles].
- **61** *Convicia suffert* [He endures reproaches]. Compare below vv. 104 and 537.
- **62** *Confremuere omnes* [All murmured loudly]. The celebrated Bondam observes that this is a hemistich of Ovid, *Met.* I, 199.
- **63** G. 2 reads *Invitus*, and A. by correction; but *invitos amores* [unwilling love], which the vulgate editions have, is correct, because Chryseis was unwillingly with Agamemnon, and he is here said to release her sorrowfully (*aeger*). Propertius, III, 20, 30: « Atridae magno quum stetit alter amor ».
- **65** *Multaque dona super* [And many gifts besides]. For *super*, in the margin of the Basel or Torinus edition *simul* is written, and G. 2 displays *Meritaque dona simul*. Dussen approves of the word *simul*, as being familiar to our author and other poets. For above, in v. 22, he said *Dona simul profert*, and Ovid, *Met.* XI, 136: « Bacchus peccasse fatentem Restituit, factique simul data munera solvit ». But in this verse of Ovid my copy reads « pactamque fidem, data munera, solvit ». Nor do there seem to be reasons weighty enough to urge that the *simul* is to be preferred. — *Cunctis notus Ulysses* [Ulysses known to all]. But Dussen prefers *furtis notus* [known for tricks/wiles], ingeniously indeed, because Ulysses is much celebrated by the poets for his tricks, and is even called by our author *fraudis commentor* [contriver of deceit]. But I do not yet venture to change the vulgate reading. In Greek Ulysses is called πολύμητις. The author of the *Epitome* seems to have understood this word as if it were πολύμυθος or πολύμνητος, said of one who is on the lips of many, or of whom many tales are told, which he could thus interpret as *cunctis notus*.
- **66** *Devexit ad arces* [Carried down to the citadels]. G. 1 has *ad horas*, that is *oras* [shores], better, because the talk is of a sea voyage. Ed.
- **67** *Sua vela retorsit* [Turned back his sails]. Ovid, *Trist.* I, 1, 84: « Semper ab Euboicis vela retorquet aquis ».

## p. 522

PDF page 532.

| Verse | Wernsdorf | Text | Printed |
|---|---|---|---|
| 69 | 69 | Et prope consumptae vires redduntur Achivis. |  |
| 70 | 70 | Non tamen Atridae Chryseidis excidit ardor : | 70 |
| 71 | 71 | Moeret , et amissos deceptus luget amores. |  |
| 72 | 72 | Mox rapta magnum Briseide privat Achillem , |  |
| 73 | 73 | Solaturque suos alienis ignibus ignes. |  |
| 74 | 74 | At ferus Aeacides nudato protinus ense |  |
| 75 | 75 | Tendit in Atriden , cui , ni sibi reddat honestae | 75 |
| 76 | 76 | Munera militiae , letum crudele minatur. |  |
| 77 | 77 | Nec minus ille parat contra defendere sese, |  |
| 78 | 78 | Et nisi casta manu Pallas tenuisset Achillem, |  |
| 79 | 79 | Turpem caecus amor famam liquisset in aevum |  |
| 80 | 80 | Gentibus Argolicis : contentus voce minisque , | 80 |

### Notes

- **69** *Et prope consumptae* [And almost consumed]. G. 1 omits this verse in the text, but it is noted in the margin that this is added in other copies.
- **70** G. 1 has *Non tamen Atridi*.
- **73** *Ignibus ignes* [Fires with fires], as Ovid, *Trist.* IV, 3, 65: « compescuit ignibus ignes ». With similar thought Valerius Flaccus, II, 151, from the emendation of Heinsius: « Attamen hos aliis forsan solabere casus Tu thalamis ». Quintilian, *Declam.* II, 3: « Hoc juveni fuit consilium, ut pater, cui matrimonium filiumque abstulerat incendium, residua senectutis alia solaretur uxore ».
- **75** *Et ni sibi reddat* [And unless he restore to him]: so the Basel edition; G. 1 has *quod ni sibi*; A. and L. have *qui ni sibi*, and this last I have preferred. — But I have altered even this reading for the better, and written *cui*. Ed.
- **76** *Munera militiae* [Gifts of warfare]. He would have spoken more correctly had he said *praemia* [prizes/rewards]: for he means Briseis, who had fallen to Achilles as a reward of war, like booty and spoil.
- **78** *Et nisi casta manu* [And had not the chaste goddess with her hand...]. The author of the *Epitome* changes slightly the order and narration of events observed by Homer. Homer relates that Achilles wished to draw his sword against Agamemnon and was restrained by Minerva, before Briseis was taken away from Achilles. Our author speaks of that clash as if it occurred after Briseis was taken away.
- **79** *Turpem caecus amor famam* [Blind love... shameful renown]. This does not seem to be said according to the mind of Homer and the customs of the ancient heroes, who by no means considered it shameful for themselves to be driven into arms by the madness of love on account of a girl. But he seems to have sought the reason for this saying from the words of Achilles, v. 298, in which he asserts—evidently having been warned by Pallas—that he indeed will not fight with his hands for the sake of the girl: Χερσὶ μὲν οὔτι ἔγωγε μαχήσομαι εἵνεκα κούρης.
- **80** *Gentibus Argolicis* [To the Argive peoples]. G. 2 has *Fontibus Argolicis*, faultily. — The vulgate editions of Basel and Spondanus read *contempta voce*; codex A. and the Leipzig edition have *contemptus*, namely Achilles. From this I read *contentus voce minisque*, that is, Achilles, content to have contended with Agamemnon by voice, reproaches, and threats, not by hands and sword (for this Minerva had enjoined upon him), seeks vengeance from his mother for the wrong inflicted upon him.

### Notes

- **81** For *Invocat*, G. 2 has *Convocat*, erroneously; *numina matris* G. 2, A., L., and Sp.; the rest have *nomina*, less correctly.
- **82** *Ne se plus Thetis contra* [Lest Thetis further... against]. So all manuscript and printed copies; and yet the name of Thetis is neither suited to the place, nor to the meter, on account of the shortened syllable. Barth, *Advers.* p. 2753, notes on this verse that *plus* means *amplius* [further/more], as Plautus, Claudian, Sidonius, Apuleius, and others have it; about the latent fault he remarks nothing. Dussen, *loc. cit.* p. 14, attempts a remedy in various ways, of whose conjectures I consider this the most probable: *Ne se plus contra Atriden patiatur inultum*. For the rest, he judges that without the help of manuscripts these things are extremely difficult to heal. Long ago already a learned man's hand attempted to cure it, which in a copy of the old Leipzig edition preserved in the library of that Academy corrected: *Ne se diva Thetis*. But the name of Thetis seems unseasonable in this place, because it immediately recurs in the following verse.
- **86 (LL 87)** *Sparsis affusa capillis* [Falling prostrate with disheveled hair]. G. 2 and the Leipzig edition have *effusa*. Here our author clearly imitated Ovid, speaking of Ceres suppliant before Jove for her daughter Proserpina, *Met.* V, 513: « Ante Jovem passis stetit invidiosa capillis, Proque meo venio supplex tibi, Jupiter, inquit, Sanguine, proque tuo ».
- **87 (LL 88)** *Genitrix en ad tua* [Lo, mother, at your...]. I suspect that for *en*, *ait* [she said] ought to be put. For the speech requires this, or *inquit*, and Ovid did similarly in the passage which our author reproduced.
- **89 (LL 90)** G. 2 has *Corpus ab Atrida*. As Barth notes *loc. cit.*, Thetis calls her son Achilles her *corpus* [body/flesh], and I consider this a novel expression by the author, not easily to be found in other poets; for it is common for children to be called the *viscera* [entrails/flesh] or *sanguis* [blood] of their parents.
- **90 (LL 91)** For *violaret*, which is in the vulgate editions, G. 2 has *violarit*, which I think is more correct.
- **91 (LL 92)** *Turpiter occiderit* [Should shamefully fall]. G. 2 has *Turpiter occidet*, incorrectly. The author adds this thought from his own invention; in Homer Thetis speaks no such things. On the other hand, he omits what Thetis chiefly asked of Jove, and which the following expostulation of Juno looks back to, that he allow the Trojans to be victorious so long until the Greeks

- **92 (LL 93)** The Leipzig, Torinus, and Spondanus editions read *Jupiter huic dixit*; codex A. has *hinc dixit*; but G. 2 and the Basel edition in the margin have *Jupiter huic contra*, which is more elegant and very customary for poets. Dussen, p. 14, proves it by these passages: Ovid, *Met.* V, 30: « Ille nihil contra, sed et hunc, etc. »; Phaedrus, I, fab. 1, 6: « laniger contra timens, Qui, quaeso, possim facere, quod quereris, lupe? »
- **93 (LL 94)** *Mecum labor ipse manebit* [The task itself shall remain with me]. G. 2 has *labor ille*: that is, I shall take care of this matter diligently and constantly. Homer, v. 523: ἐμοὶ δέ κε ταῦτα μελήσεται, ὄφρα τελέσσω. — Our author has reproduced Virgil, *Aeneid* IV, 115: « Mecum erit iste labor »; and he seems at the same time to have looked to the words of Jove to Venus in Virg. *Aen.* I, 256: « Parce metu, Cytherea: manent immota tuorum Fata tibi »; or what Aeolus says to Juno, *Aen.* I, 76: « tuus, o regina, quid optes, Explorare labor, mihi jussa capessere fas est ». The opposite to our author's words is that of Virgil, *Aen.* II, 595: « quonam nostri tibi cura recessit ».
- **95 (LL 96)** As for what the edition of Torinus and Spondanus has, *et illa levis caeli*, Dussen, p. 15, thought it should be corrected to *illa leves ..... per auras*. But the old books present this very reading: G. 2 has *Dixerat, illa leves*; A. and L. have *Dixit, at illa leves ... dilapsa*.
- **97 (LL 98)** *Tantumque, ait* [And so much, she said]: so indeed the printed editions offer; but G. 2 has *Tantumne*, which is by no means inept.
- **98 (LL 99)** The Basel edition has *Dorida nata*; but that this is a printer's error de Rooy already observed, *loc. cit.* p. 96, who corrects it to *Doride nata* [daughter born of Doris], and the same is clearly read in G. 1. A. and L. corruptly present *Nata valet Doride tantumque debetur*. Propertius is cited by Anton de Rooy, book I, 18, 25: « At vos aequoreae formosa Doride natae, Candida felici solvite vela choro ». The daughters of Doris are the Oceanids, as they are called by Virgil, *Georg.* IV, 341. "Yet in this way," says Barth in *Adv.* p. 2753, "Juno foolishly calls Thetis by way of reproach, unmindful that she too is a daughter of Ocean, concerning whom Homer wrote in *Iliad*, book XXIII: Ὠκεανόν τε θεῶν γένεσιν καὶ μητέρα Τηθύν."
- **99 (LL 100)** *Ut mihi, quae conjux* [That to me, who am his wife...]. Similarly Juno in Virgil, *Aen.* I, 46: « Ast ego, quae Divum incedo regina, Jovisque Et soror et conjux ». — And with the same emphasis our eminent poet Racine, *Britann.* act I, sc. 2, v. 29: « Moi, fille, femme, soeur et mère de vos maîtres ». ### Notes

- **102 (LL 103)** *Haec ita dona refers nobis? sic diligor* [Do you return such gifts to us? Thus am I loved?]. Dussen prefers *Haeccine dona refers nobis?*, that is, such gifts. This is certainly clearer and more elegant; the vulgate reading is a little harsher. I however would believe that we come closer to the writing of the vulgate if we read *Haec tu dona refers nobis?* Furthermore, the form of this saying is clearly modeled upon the speech of Venus to Jove in Virgil, *Aen.* I, 253: « Hic pietatis honos? sic nos in sceptra reponis? »
- **103 (LL 104)** *Talibus incusat* [With such words she reproaches]: from Virgil, *Aen.* I, 410.
- **105 (LL 106)** *Interposito lis omnipotente* [The dispute, the Almighty intervening...] is read in all manuscript and printed books, and Barth, *Adv.* LVIII, 14, notes that *omnipotentem* beautifully designates Jove *kat' exochen* [par excellence]. But Dussen, *loc. cit.* p. 16, admirably observed that *ignipotente* [the Fire-Lord] should be read, and Vulcan is understood. For he intervened between Jove and Juno quarreling, and by encouragement and offering many cups appeased his parents and turned them to cheerfulness. So Hom. *Iliad* I, 571: Τοῖσιν δ᾽ Ἥφαιστος κλυτοτέχνης ἦρχ᾽ ἀγορεύειν. Moreover, Vulcan is called *Ignipotens* by our author and other poets. Below, v. 867: « Illic Ignipotens mundi caelaverat axem »; and Virgil, *Aen.* VIII, 414: « Haud secus Ignipotens, nec tempore segnior illo »; to which must be added that of *Aen.* X, 243: « clypeum cape quem dedit ipse Invictum Ignipotens, etc. » Ed.
- **106 (LL 107)** *Dimittit ab aula* [Dismisses from the hall]. For this *ab aula*, G. 1 erroneously reads *Olimpi*. That word moved into this verse from the following one by an error of the scribe.
- **107 (LL 108)** *Descendit Olympo* [Descends from Olympus] the vulgate editions read here, but the written copies G. 1 and 2 better have *decedit Olympo* [departs from Olympus]: for this is Virgilian, *Georg.* I, 450: « emenso quum jam decedet Olympo ».
- **108 (LL 109)** G. 1 and 2 have *Et dapibus Divi ... largis* [And the gods with lavish feasts...]. Virgil, *Aen.* III, 510: « passimque in litore sicco Corpora curamus ».
- **109 (LL 110)** *Dona quietis* [Gifts of rest]. Virg. *Aen.* II, 369: « quies ... dono Divum gratissima serpit ». Ovid, *Am.* II, 9, 40: « et somnos praemia magna vocat ». Stat. *Silv.* V, 4, 2: « donis ut solus egerem, Somne, tuis ».
- **110 (LL 111)** *Nox erat* [Night was falling / It was night]. Our author could have drawn this briefer description of the night from the more detailed ones which Virgil has, *Aen.* IV, 522 ff. and VIII, 26 f. — G. 1 reads *Sidera mundo*.

- **112 (LL 113)** The Basel and Spondanus editions have *Ac ita fatur*; G. 1 and 2 have *atque ita fatur*.

But here he had many passages of ancient poets to imitate, especially of Ovid on the dream sent to Alcyone, *Met.* XI, 586 ff., and of Statius, who imitated Ovid, *Theb.* X, 84 ff. — *Lenissime divum* [Gentlest of the gods]. Ovid, *Met.* XI, 623: « placidissime, Somne, Deorum ». Statius, *Silv.* V, 4, 1: « placidissime Divum », and *Theb.* X, 126: « mitissime Divum ».
- **115 (LL 116)** *Dumque tuo* [And while with your...]. This verse is omitted in G. 2, and placed after v. 123. — The author attributes weight (*pondus*) to sleep, just as poets elsewhere also say that people are weighed down (*gravari*), overwhelmed (*opprimi*), or succumb (*succumbere*) to sleep. See Virg. *Aen.* VI, 520; Ovid, *Her.* XII, 49.
- **118 (LL 119)** *Cogat in arma viros* [Let him summon the men to arms]. Virg. *Aen.* IX, 463: « Turnus in arma viros armis circumdatus ipse, Suscitat, aeratasque acies in praelia cogit ».
- **121 (LL 122)** *Corpus inundatum* [The body flooded / steeped]. Barth *loc. cit.* p. 2752 detects here some kind of affectation that is unworthy of the finest meter in this place. I consider the word *inundatum* to be thoroughly elegant, and formed after that custom of poets by which they elsewhere say *irrigare somnum* [to pour over with sleep] and *soporem irriguum* [refreshing sleep]. See Virg. *Aen.* III, 511; Lucret. IV, 906; Claudian, preface to *VI Cons. Hon.* v. 9.
- **122 (LL 123)** *Curarum operumque levator* [Reliever of cares and of labors]. He expresses more briefly what Naso states more expansively, *Met.* XI, 624: « Pax animi, quem cura fugit; qui corda diurnis Fessa ministeriis mulces, reparasque labori ».

- **125 (LL 126)** G. 2 has *Titan se emerserit*; A. has *undas*.

- **130 (LL 131)** *Attonitus jussis* [Astonished by the commands]. A. and L. read *attonitos*.

### Notes

- **(cont.)** [to designate his heroes,] following the example of Homer and others: such as in another place *Nestoris aetas* [the age of Nestor], for Nestor himself; *Menelai ardor* [the ardor of Menelaus]; *Ithaci solertia* [the skill of the Ithacan]; and *virtus Catonis* [the virtue of Cato] in Horace.
- **144 (LL 145)** *Compressam miti sedavit* [Calmed, checked with gentle...]. Perhaps taken from these words of Virgil, *Georg.* IV, 86: « Hi motus animorum atque haec certamina tanta Pulveris exigui jactu compressa quiescent ». And Virgil says *sedato pectore* [with calmed breast] in *Aen.* IX, 740.
- **145 (LL 146)** *Responsa recordans Temporis illius* [Recalling the prophecies of that time]. The distinguished Bondam, page 144, thinks this is fashioned after these words of Ovid, *Met.* [XIII,] 280: « quanto cogor meminisse dolore Temporis illius, quo Graium murus Achilles Procubuit ».
- **147 (LL 148)** *Consumpsit volucrum* [Consumed of the birds...]. There is scarcely any doubt that the author here had in mind Ovid, *Met.* XII, 15: « Nidus erat volucrum bis quatuor arbore summa, Quas simul et matrem circum sua damna volantem Corripuit serpens ». From this Dussen, p. 18, conjectures that the author wrote « volucrum bis quatuor arbore nidum », or *nidos*; and he notes that chicks and the offspring of birds are remarkably called *nidi* [nests] by poets and other writers. Although this is true, it is nevertheless not necessary, nor probable, to read *nidos* for *fetus* in our author. For *nidum* departs too far from the form of the word *fetus*, which all manuscripts preserve, and even if the author had Ovid in mind, he did not wish to copy him entirely. G. 2 has *bis quatuor in arbore*.
- **148 (LL 149)** All the vulgate editions have *Atque ipsam valido*. Dussen, p. 20, felt that one should write *invalido*, and I saw that very reading confirmed by codex G. 1, which offers « Atque ipsam invalido pugnantem corpore contra ». — We know that *pectore* and *corpore* are often and widely interchanged by copyists; in this passage, however, it is difficult to assert which is better.
- **150 (LL 151)** The vulgates have *Inquit deinde*; deeming this inept, I restored *Infit* from codex G. 1. In the remaining words there is much variation. G. 1 has *Infit deinde senex, moneo remoneboque Achivi*. G. 2 has *Tum sic inde senex, moneo removete Achivi*. Ann. and Lips. have *moneo remaneteque Achivi*. The editions of Torinus and Spondanus exhibit *maneo, remaneteque Achivi*: which reading I consider the best, and confirmed by Homer, *Iliad* II, 331, where Ulysses advises: Ἀλλ᾽ ἄγε, μίμνετε πάντες ἐϋκνήμιδες Ἀχαιοί. To be sure, in Homer it is a speech of Ulysses, which our author attributes to Nestor.

### Notes

- **(cont.)** [that is, in] the tenth year at last is the toil foretold and appointed for us, in which we may capture Ilion. Yet I would almost prefer to read *In decimum annum usque labor*, that is, we must toil. For he means that the war is to be delayed to that year, until Ilion is captured. Virgil is accustomed to speak thus, as in *Aen.* IX, 155: « decimum quos distulit Hector in annum »; and XI, 289: « Hectoris Aeneaeque manu victoria Graium Haesit, et in decimum vestigia rettulit annum ».
- **153 (LL 154)** *Nestoris aetas* [The age of Nestor], that is, aged prudence, or the aged Nestor.
- **155 (LL 156)** G. 2 reads *Rex jubet*; the same has *aptare*. Virg. *Aen.* X, 258: « sociis edicit, signa sequantur, Atque animos aptent armis: pugnaeque parent se ».
- **156 (LL 157)** G. 2 has *Tacitas ubi primum*.
- **157 (LL 158)** *Et nitidus Titan radiis patefecerat orbem* [And shining Titan had laid open the world with his rays]. Thus read the editions with codex A.; a verse of this kind already appeared at 49. But I preferred what MSS G. 1 and 2 offer: « Et nitidum Titan radiis caput extulit undis » [And Titan raised his shining head with rays from the waves].
- **159 (LL 160)** *Et petere* [And to make for...]. Verse 127 is repeated. In G. 2 it is missing here, and is placed after 167, absurdly.
- **160 (LL 161)** *Quid enim non ordine nostis?* [For what do you not know in order?]. Similarly Maro, *Aen.* VII, 645, and IX, 529: « Et meministis enim, Divae, et memorare potestis ».
- **165 (LL 166)** *Adspiretque libens operi* [And may she breathe favorably upon the work]. Virg. *Aen.* IX, 525: « Vos, o Calliope, precor, adspirate canenti ». Ovid, *Met.* I, 3: « Di coeptis ... adspirate meis ». — For the rest, this enumeration of the armies is practically customary for all epic poets since Homer. One should consult especially his happy imitator and rival, Virg. *Aen.* VII, 641; and among more recent poets, Torquato Tasso, *Gerus. lib.* c. I, st. 36. Ed.
- **166 (LL 167)** *Peneleus princeps, bello Laertius acer* [Peneleus leader, fierce Laertius in war]: thus the vulgate editions present it. But G. 1 has *et bello Lertius acer*, from which we should read *Leitus acer*, because by Homer in the Boeotia [Catalogue of Ships], verse 1, Πηνέλεως καὶ Λήϊτος are joined together. From this name the copyists erroneously made the better-known *Laertius*, that is, Ulysses.

### Notes

- **167 (LL 168)** *Arcesilaus atrox, etc.* [Fierce Arcesilaus, etc.]. Thus the names should be written according to Homer, and from the traces of the corrupt reading of the manuscripts. The vulgate editions have *Arcesilaus atrox, Clonius dux, atque Protenor*. Guelf. 1: *Archesilaus atrox, Protenorque Clonius atque*; and in the margin *Protenor Colono usque*. G. 2: *Protenorque Didonius atque*.
- **168 (LL 169)** The books have *Boetes decies*, but from Homer, *Iliad* II, 510, one should read *Boeotas* or *Boeotum*. For the vulgate *quinas duxere*, G. 1 and 2 have *egere*.
- **170 (LL 171)** *Finibus ortus* [Sprung from the territories]. G. 2, A., and L. have *moenibus*.
- **173 (LL 174)** A. and L. read *Et bis ter denis*. — *Menelai ardor* [The ardor of Menelaus], that is, the fiery Menelaus.
- **176 (LL 177)** *Gemina cum prole suorum* [With twin offspring of his own]. That he writes that Nestor arrived with two sons he does not have from Homer, but perhaps from Dictys of Crete, who in Book I, chap. 13 says: « Nestor cum Antilocho et Thrasymede, quos ex Anaxibia susceperat, supervenit ».
- **177 (LL 178)** Ann., Bas., and Sp. erroneously read *It cum ter denis munitus*. G. 1 has *Est ter tricenis*. G. 2 has *It ter tricenis*, which is correct: for Homer, *Iliad* II, 602, attributes ninety ships to him.
- **178 (LL 179)** The vulgate editions have *Ascadius virtute*. G. 1 and A. have *Ascedius ... Epistropus*; G. 2 has *Ascanius*, and L. *Episgrofus ingens*. All these are corrupt. One must read from Homer, *Il.* II, 517: *At Schedius virtute potens et Epistrophus*.
- **180 (LL 181)** Ann. and the vulgate editions have *Grandia ter denis pulsarunt*. But G. 1 has *Longaque ter denis*; whence I read *Longa quater denis*. For Homer *loc. cit.* sets down forty ships. From this verse codex Guelf. 1 is deficient down to verse 628.
- **181 (LL 182)** The vulgates read *At bis vigenas Polybetes atque Leontes*. G. 2 has *bis vicenas*; L. *atque Levittes*; A. *Lenotes*. But that one must read *Polypoetes atque Leonteus*

### Notes

- **(cont.)** [that one must read Polypoetes atque Leonteus] is clear from Homer, *Iliad* II, 740 and 745. And Bondam has already shown this, *Specim. Animadv.* chap. 13, p. 73, and Adriaan van Dorp, *Observat.* chap. 14, p. 133.
- **182 (LL 183)** *Ornatas milite* [Equipped with soldiery] is in the vulgates. G. 2 exhibits *Armatas, ornatas, oneratas* together.
- **183 (LL 184)** *Eurypylus* (or *Euripelus*, *Euripolus*) *Sthenelusque* is consistently read by the manuscripts and printed editions I have seen. G. 2 has *Sthenelusque duces*; A. has *Stenedusque*. Yet there is scarcely any doubt, as the distinguished Bondam proved in *Specim. Animadv.* p. 73 and *Var. Lect.* p. 146, that in place of *Eurypylus* one must put *Euryalus*, whom Homer himself joins with Sthenelus and Diomedes in *Iliad* II, 565. And Dares the Phrygian, chap. 14: « Diomedes, Euryalus, Sthenelus ex Argis », with eighty ships. On the other hand, Eurypylus, whom Homer (v. 736) numbers among the Greek leaders with forty ships, but whom our poetaster does not seem to have named, Bondam thinks was brought in at v. 189, which we ourselves shall note at that verse.
- **184 (LL 185)** G. 2 has *Remige fluctus*.
- **185 (LL 186)** A. and L. have *Hi quadragenas*; but Homer assigns them eighty ships, whence *bis* [twice] is necessary.
- **186 (LL 187)** *Et Ialmenus*: thus we have rewritten from Homer, *Iliad* II, 512, who places him next to Ascalaphus. A. and the vulgates have *et Telamus*. G. 2 has *potens Tolmenus*.
- **187 (LL 188)** G. 2 has *Complerunt remige naves*.
- **188 (LL 189)** G. 2 has *Bis vicenas fortes fortissimus*; completely erroneously.
- **189 (LL 190)** Editions L. and Tor. have *Totidemque Abantore natus*. But I find no son of Abantor in Homer's catalogue. The distinguished Bondam, *Var. lect.* p. 146, thinks Eurypylus, named among the leaders by Homer but not found in our author, is indicated in this verse, and therefore conjectures it was written thus: « Instruxit puppes, totidemque Evaemone natus ». For Eurypylus is called by Homer (v. 736) Εὐαίμονος ἀγλαὸς υἱός [glorious son of Euaemon], and is said to have led the same number of 40 ships. He is also called the son of Euaemon and Opis by Hyginus, *fab.* 94. Nor indeed do I find anyone else in the whole catalogue who could more fittingly be mentioned in this place; and the reading of codex Guelf. 2 somewhat approaches the similarity of his name, presenting *totidemque inthenore*, *enthenore*, *anthenore* together; and codex A. has *ex tenore*.
- **190 (LL 191)** Barth, *Advers.* p. 2754, explains *Graium ductor* [leader of the Greeks] as preeminent in war, supreme in bravery. But

| Verse | Wernsdorf | Text | Printed |

| 192 | 191 | Cum quinquaginta materna per aequora vectus. |  |
| 193 | 192 | Thessalici juvenes Phidippus et Antiphus ibant, |  |
| 194 | 193 | Altaque ter denis sulcarunt aequora proris: |  |
| 195 | 194 | Et tribus adsumptis ratibus secat aequora Nireus, |  |

| 197 | 196 | Eumelus sequitur, minus una nave profectus. |  |

### Notes

- **(cont.)** G. 2 reads *Graium durus comitator Ach.*; A. *durus comitatur*.
- **192 (LL 193)** I wrote *Phidippus et Antiphus* from Homer's *Iliad* II, 678, whereas the vulgate editions had *Pedippus et Antipus*.
- **193 (LL 194)** G. 2 has *Pulsarunt aequora*.
- **194 (LL 195)** *Ratibus secat aequora Teucer* [Teucer cuts the seas with ships]: thus the vulgates and manuscripts. But the most learned Bondam, *Var. Lect.* p. 147, contends that Nireus should be substituted in place of Teucer, whom Homer (*Il.* II, 671) relates to have brought only three ships, and whom our author, unless he named him here, must be judged to have omitted entirely. He further supports his opinion with these reasons: not only does Homer nowhere mention this Teucer in his catalogue, but he relates that his brother Telamonian Ajax led twelve ships to Troy, with which our author also agrees in verse 204; but other writers as well, such as Dictys, Book I, chaps. 13 and 17, make Ajax the leader of the ships, and say that his brother Teucer was his companion and ally. Dares the Phrygian clearly states, chap. 14: « Ajax Telamonius ex Salamine adduxit secum Teucrum fratrem ». Hyginus indeed, *fab.* 97, says that Telamonian Ajax brought 12 ships, and his brother Teucer just as many. But different from this is the number of ships which the vulgate reading attributes to Teucer in our author: he is said to have led only three ships; in Homer, however, three ships are attributed to Nireus alone, and he alone is passed over in silence by the epitomator, unless he is to be named in place of Teucer. And I see that these reasons carry such weight that I think one should write without hesitation: « Et tribus adsumptis ratibus secat aequora Nireus ». I also suspect that the word *adsumptis* is corrupt, and that one should write *Et tribus ex Syme ratibus*. For Dictys, I, 17 says: « Nireus ex Syme tres, Podarces et Protesilaus ex Phylaca naves XL ».
- **195 (LL 196)** All the books indeed gave *Triptolemusque novem Rhodius*, and G. 2 has *novem ratibus quos*. But that one must read *Tlepolemus*, Barth already saw, *loc. cit.* p. 2754; Homer (*Il.* II, 653) says that he brought nine ships from Rhodes. Dictys *loc. cit.*: *Tlepolemus ex Rhodo aliisque insulis, quae circa eam erant, octo navibus venit*. Dares the Phrygian, chap. 14: « Tlepolemus ex Rhodo navibus numero novem ».
- **196 (LL 197)** Bondam, *loc. cit.* p. 149, urged that the name of Eumelus—which the printed editions write most corruptly as *Emenelus*, *Emeneleus*, *En marelus*, and codex A. as *Eumarelus*—ought to be restored here on the authority of Homer, *Iliad* II, 714, where Εὔμηλος, the son of Admetus, is said to have commanded eleven ships from Pherae. Pherae is a city of Thessaly. Dictys *loc. cit.*: « Eumelus XI Pheris ». Whence Hyginus, who writes that Eumelus was from Perrhaebia, seems in need of correction. Our author, by *minus una nave*, says that he set out (profe-) with one ship fewer, i.e., eleven...

## p. 533

PDF page 543.

| Verse | Wernsdorf | Text | Printed |
|---|---|---|---|
| 198 | 197 | [Quam duxit Telamone satus Salaminius Ajax.] |  |
| 199 | 198 | At Prothous Magnes , Tenthredone natus , et una |  |
| 200 | 199 | Eubceae magnis Elephenor finibus ortus, |  |
| 201 | 200 | Dulichiusque Meges, auimisque insignis et armis | 200 |
| 202 | 201 | Aetola de gente Thoas Andraemone natus, |  |
| 203 | 202 | Hi quadragenas omnes duxere carinas : |  |
| 204 | 203 | Et bis sex Ithaci naves solertia duxit, |  |

### Notes

- **(cont.)** "[...profe-]ctum dicit [he says that he set out], because he counts together the ships of the preceding leaders, three of Nireus and nine of Tlepolemus."
- **197 (LL 198)** I judge that this verse ought to be rejected, and for that reason I have taken care to enclose it in brackets. It is a mere interpolation (*emblema*) by a schoolmaster who was expounding this poetaster in school. Since he did not see to what the words of the preceding verse, *minus una nave* [with one ship less], were to be referred, and knew that eleven ships were assigned to Eumelus, he thought that a commander of twelve ships should be subjoined, in comparison with whom Eumelus, having set out with one ship less, would be understood to have had eleven ships. It is manifest that verse 204 was transferred to this place for the sake of explaining this matter; moreover, it appears absurdly fabricated in G. 2, A., and the old Leipzig edition: *Quam duxit Telamone satus Telamonius Ajax*.
- **198 (LL 199)** The printed books read here *Ascropus, et magno Thestoride natus*. Guelf. 2 exhibits *Astropus*, *Astrophus*, and *Astraphius* together. The distinguished Bondam, who *loc. cit.* p. 149 purged this and the following verses of errors, and whom I gladly follow, considers that here one should read from Homer's *Iliad* II, 756: *At Prothous magno Tenthredone natus*. But the fact that the words *magno* and *magnis* occupied two successive verses already offended Barth, and in Homer no such praise of Tenthredon is read that he should be called *magnus* [great] by our poet. For that reason I preferred to write *At Prothous Magnes* [But Prothous the Magnesian]. For Homer calls Prothous the leader of the Magnetes, and Dictys in the catalogue of heroes who besieged Troy, Book I, 17: « Prothous, Magnes, XL naves ».
- **199 (LL 200)** *Elephenor*: thus it must be written from Homer, *Il.* II, 540. In the printed editions there was corruptly *Eubocca ac magnis Helpenor*. G. 2 has *Euboias ac*, and Leipzig *magnis Delphonor finibus*. A. *Delphenor*. Bondam, however, emended *Euboeae ex magnis*, but because the *ac* of the old books seems to have arisen from the final syllable of the word *Euboeae*, I judged that it should not be changed to *ex*, but rather expelled. So above our author, v. 170: *Mycenaeis Agamemnon finibus ortus*. Barth, *Adv.* p. 2754, because Homer relates that Elephenor sprang from the borders of the Abantes, refashions this verse quite boldly: *Euboea, Elpenorque e finibus ortus Abantum*. — In Ovid, *Trist.* III, perhaps the same Elpenor is mentioned, one of Ulysses' companions, who, when he had been turned into a swine by Circe, and then restored to human shape, while hastening to Ulysses in careless eagerness to flee, fell from a high place and perished. Ed.
- **201 (LL 202)** The vulgate editions have *Et clara de gente Thoas Andremone* or *Andromene*, which Bondam rightly emends to *Andraemone*. I moreover thought that *Aetola de gente* [of the Aetolian race] should be written, from Homer's *Il.* II, 638: and cod. G. 2 clearly offers: *Ethola de gente Thoas*.

### Notes

- **204 (LL 205)** *Quam sequitur* [Whom follows]. This verse is omitted in the old Leipzig edition; which I think happened because the editor saw Telamonian Ajax already mentioned in verse 197. He saw that one of these two verses ought to be rejected, but did not understand which was spurious and which genuine.
- **205 (LL 206)** Guneus led twenty-two ships from Cyphus: Homer, *Il.* II, 648 [748]. Hitherto the printed editions had *simul ordine Phineus*. G. 2 has *simul horrida Phineus*.
- **209 (LL 210)** Menestheus led fifty ships from Athens: Hom. *Il.* II, 552. Dictys and Dares call him Mnestheus, and assign as many ships to him. The old editions together with cod. A. had *clara de gente Menetes*, or *Menetis*.
- **210 (LL 211)** *Athenaeus* [the Athenian], because he was the leader of the Athenians. G. 2 has *Duxit Atheleus*; erroneously. — The editions have *Quos viribus*, but *quot* should be read, so that it may refer to the preceding *totidem* [as many]. For he means that Menestheus had as many ships as Achilles was leading, namely fifty; see verse 191. Moreover, for *ambit Achilles*, I think we should read *addit viribus*, that is, adds to the forces, i.e., to the army or the entire fleet. After this verse cod. Annab. inserts two verses, which are also corrupt in every way: « In totidem ratibus armatus milite plenis Amphonias venerat, cum remis aequora pulsans ».
- **211 (LL 212)** *Et Thalpius* [and Thalpius] I have restored from Hom. *Il.* II, 620. The old editions had *Alpinus et Elide nati*. Cod. Annab. *Amphimachusque ferox et Alpibus*.
- **212 (LL 213)** The old editions have *Polixeus atque Diares*. More correctly, G. 2 has *Et clara virtute Polyxenus atque Diores*. Homer joins these men with Amphimachus and Thalpius from Elis, as does Dictys, I, 17.
- **214 (LL 215)** Podarces and Protesilaus brought 40 ships from Phylace and the other places over which they ruled: Homer, *Il.* II, 704, and Dictys *loc. cit.* Old editions corruptly *Podartes*.
- **215 (LL 216)** *Instructas* [Equipped]. This verse too seems to be spurious. For why should Oïlean Ajax have led the ships of Protesilaus, when he was already present in verse 188? But if perhaps you should wish to read *quot duxit* instead of *quas*, then indeed this verse will declare the number of the ships of Protesilaus, which was as grea-

### Notes

- **(cont.)** [-t / -tus fuit] as that of Ajax, but nevertheless it will be superfluous. For the *totidem* [just as many] of verse 214 in itself refers to the *bis vicenas* [twice twenty] of the preceding verse; and the words *Instructas puppes* [equipped sterns] repeated from verse 189 betray an interpolator. For the rest, G. 2 writes here *quas rexit Doricus Ajax*; Leipzig *Eolus Ajax*; and A. *duxit Eolius*.
- **216 (LL 217)** *At septem Paeante satus* [But he that was born of Poeas with seven]. Bondam showed that it should be read thus, *Var. Lect.* p. 146, that is Philoctetes, whom Homer, *Il.* II, 719, writes was in command of seven ships. And in this name the books vary greatly. Basel ed. *Vetonte satus*. G. 2 *Et septem Pheronte satus*. Leipzig *Ac septem Blutone satus*. Annaberg *Plutone satus*.
- **217 (LL 218)** *Polidarius atque Mathaon* G. 2. *Polidonius atque Methaon* A. — Both were physicians, both sons of Aesculapius. And Machaon is mentioned in Virg. *Aen.* II, 263. Ed.
- **218 (LL 219)** *Altaque ter denis* [And the deep (seas) with thirty]. The same verse as above, 193. Our author loves to compose the same or similar verses about the same subject, following the example of Homer himself, whom he compiles.
- **220 (LL 221)** *Quam mille ducentae* [than twelve hundred], that is, 1186 ships [1200 minus twice seven]: it is Homer's own number. As Barth observes *loc. cit.*, Dictys puts the number of ships coming to Troy at 1117, Dares at 1148. Anne Dacier from Dictys counts the ships of the Greeks as 1153, from Dares 1140; she says that the scholiast on Euripides' *Orestes* counts 1155, Cedrenus 1148, Thucydides and Chrysostom 1200.
- **221 (LL 222)** *Jamque citae* [And now swift] G. 2. The vulgate editions have *cito*, less elegantly.
- **222 (LL 223)** *Tum pater* [Then the father] G. 2.
- **224 (LL 225)** *Petit arma* [seeks arms] G. 2.
- **225 (LL 226)** *Totamque in praelia pubem* [And all the youth into battle]. Virgil, *Aen.* VII, 429: « armari pubem, portisque moveri Laetus in arma para ».
- **226 (LL 227)** *Portis ... agmen apertis* [the column through the open gates]. Virg. *Aen.* XII, 121: « pilataque plenis Agmina se fundunt portis ».

### Notes

- **231 (LL 232)** *Quales decet Hectoris esse* [Such as befit Hector's]. An Ovidian formula, *Met.* II, 14: « facies non omnibus una, Non diversa tamen: qualem decet esse sororum ». Barth *loc. cit.* judges that this and the following verses concerning Paris should be commended as outstanding, weighty, and briefly encompassing everything necessary.
- **234 (LL 235)** The vulgate editions have *Fortisque Poletes*. But Dorp, *Observ.* c. 14, p. 116, thinks one should read *Polites*, as in Homer, *Il.* II, 791 [or 701 in Wernsdorf's citation]: and G. 2 clearly offers this very reading.
- **235 (LL 236)** *Gratissima proles* [most pleasing offspring] G. 2, which I would almost prefer.
- **236 (LL 237)** *Acamasque* [and Acamas]. Hom. *Il.* II, 823; old editions *Athamasque*.
- **238 (LL 239)** *Glaucus in armis* [Glaucus in arms]. Homer, at the end of *Il.* II, joins him with Sarpedon, leader of the Lycians, concerning whom our author writes below, v. 248.
- **239 (LL 240)** I write *Amphiusque Adrastus* from Hom. *Il.* II, 830; and *Asius*, *ibid.* 838: and the old Leipzig edition along with A. seems to recognize this, writing *Adrastus Kasius atque*; the remaining editions have *Adrastus et Austius atque Phialtes*. Since neither Homer's catalogue nor Dictys of Crete, II, 35, has this Phialtes, I have brought Pylaeus here in his place, whom Homer joins with Hippothous from Larissa, *Il.* II, 842, and whom I have not seen mentioned by our author anywhere else. Bondam says (*Var. Lect.* p. 150) that he emended this and the following verses in *Specim. Animadv.* p. 77, which book, however, I was unable to consult.
- **240 (LL 241)** Amphimachus and Nastes from Caria are joined by Homer, *Il.* II, 870. The printed editions read *Amphimachus Mariusque*. A. *Maciusque*.
- **241 (LL 242)** Hodius and Epistrophus, sons of Minos king of the Halizones: so Dictys, II, 35, and Homer joins the same, v. 856; the printed editions have *Rhodius et Epistrophus*.
- **242 (LL 243)** Bondam *loc. cit.* p. 151 emended this and the following verses, whom I follow in most instances. Euphemus, leader of the Cicones, and Pyraechmes, of the Paeonians, are mentioned by Homer, vv. 846 and 848; and the reading of the old Leipzig edition, *Emphimusque ferox*, alludes to the name of Euphemus, whereas the rest more corruptly read *Euphoriusque*. A. *Eriphimusque*.

## p. 537

PDF page 547.

| Verse | Wernsdorf | Text | Printed |
|---|---|---|---|
| 244 | 243 | Cum quibus etMesthlesatque Antiphus, et bonusarmis |  |
| 245 | 244 | Hippdthus atque Acamas venere, et Pirous una, |  |
| 246 | 245 | Alcinoque sati, Chromiusque ac Ennomus, ambo | 245 |
| 247 | 246 | Florentes aetate viri , quos Phorcis et ingens |  |
| 248 | 247 | Ascanius sequitur, simul et Jovis inciyta proles |  |
| 249 | 248 | Sarpedon, claraque satus tellure Coroebus. |  |
| 250 | 249 | His se defendit ducibus Meptunia Troja, |  |

### Notes

- **243 (LL 244)** *Et Mesthles atque Antiphus*, Maeonians: Hom. v. 864. The printed copies have *Cum quibus Enesteus ac Amphius*; but the manuscript G. 2 has *Enesteus atque Antiphus*. A. and L. have *Cum quibus Monostes ac Amphius*.
- **244 (LL 245)** *Hippothus*. The celebrated Bondam reads this verse differently: *Pirous atque Acamas venere, Pylaeus et una*. But our author seems neither to have omitted Hippothous, whom Homer names among the foremost leaders and immediately before Acamas, nor to have placed him anywhere other than in this verse; and this is confirmed by the reading of codex Guelf. 2: *Ippodomus atque Athamas una cum fortibus armis*; the printed editions, however, have *Ipothus atque Othamas*. Since *Hippothous* was metrically inadmissible, I suspect that the author contracted it into *Hippothus*. In the remainder of the verse the printed editions have *venere Pigerius una*, from which I preferred to form *Pirous* rather than *Pylaeus*, because Homer joins Peiros or Peirous with Acamas at v. 844, and Pylaeus has already found a place above.
- **245 (LL 246)** *Alcinoque sati* [And the sons born of Alcinus]. The vulgate editions have *Ixioneque sati*; who these are, Bondam confesses he does not know. And certainly this reading offered by the vulgates is corrupt, nor yet is there any doubt that the name of the father of Chromius and Ennomus, who are named next, lies hidden in it. That most learned scholar himself cites Ausonius, *Epitaphia heroum*, epigr. 22, where the father of Ennomus and Chromius is called Alcinus. Therefore I write *Alcinoque sati*. This certainly does not depart far from the corrupt reading, and is even closer to another reading of codex G. 2: *Axinonoque sati*. In Homer the father does not appear, where he simply says that Chromis and Ennomus commanded the Mysians, v. 858. But our author is accustomed to draw many things from other sources.
- **246 (LL 247)** *Quos Phorcys* [Whom Phorcys]. I have written thus on the authority of Homer, who at v. 862 joins Phorcys and Ascanius as commanders of the Phrygians. The printed books corruptly read *quos fortis*. This Ascanius is to be distinguished from the son of Aeneas, who was at that time still a mere boy.
- **247 (LL 248)** *Jovis inclyta proles* [illustrious offspring of Jove]. Ovid, *Met.* IX, 229.
- **248 (LL 249)** *Coroebus*. Erroneously the Basel ed. has *Choroebus*; Leipzig *Achorebus*; A. *Achoebus*. Homer does not name this Coroebus, but Virgil calls him the son of Mygdon, and relates that he came to the aid of the Trojans and was killed by Peneleus, *Aen.* II, 341 and 425. Quintus Smyrnaeus (*Calaber*), XIII, 168 seqq., has him slain by Diomedes. In contrast, Homer names Pylaemenes, leader of the Paphlagonians, among the allies of the Trojans, whom our author, unless I am mistaken, has omitted. For more on Coroebus, see the notes by the distinguished C. G. Heyne, Excursus X to *Aen.* II. — Another Coroebus is mentioned by Statius, *Theb.* I, 650. Ed.
- **249 (LL 250)** *Neptunia Troja* [Neptunian Troy]: from Virgil's *Aen.* II, 625, and III, 3.

|---|---|---|---|
| 251 | 250 | Vicissetque doios Danauin , nisi fata vetassent. | 250 |
| 252 | 251 | III. Jamque duae stabant acies fulgentibus armisy |  |
| 253 | 252 | Quum Paris, exitium Trojae funestaque flamma, |  |
| 254 | 253 | t Armatum adverso Menelaum ex agmine vidit, |  |
| 255 | 254 | Seque velut viso perterritus angue recepit |  |
| 256 | 255 | Ad socios amens : quem postquam turpiter Hector | 255 |
| 257 | 256 | Confusum terrore videt, «Proh! dedeciis, inquit, |  |
| 258 | 257 | Aeternum patriae, generisque infamia nostri, |  |
| 259 | 258 | Terga refers? atnon dubitabas hospitis olim |  |
| 260 | 259 | Expugnare toros, cujus nunc defugis arma, |  |

### Notes

- **250 (LL 251)** The old editions read *Vicissentque dolo Danaum*, reading which Barth thought that *Danaum* was used in the accusative case for the army of the Danaans. But Bondam, *Var. Lect.* p. 153, saw that one must read *dolos Danaum*, and I now find this confirmed by the manuscript Guelf. 2, which has: *Vicissetque dolos Danaum, ni fata fuissent*; and the sheets of the old Leipzig edition have *dolus Danaum*.
- **252 (LL 253)** The editions read *Trojae et funesta ruina*, but because this was already present above, v. 233, I preferred *funestaque flamma*, which is the reading of cod. G. 2. Barth observes on this passage, *Advers.* LIX, ch. 1, that it is the custom of the best writers to call the authors of deadly disasters the disasters themselves. Juvenal of Domitian: « si peste et clade sub illa Saevitiam damnare, et honestum afferre liceret Consilium ». The author of the declamation against Catiline: « vigent enim in illa clade res diversissimae pariter, continentia et libido ». Lampridius in *Elagabalus*: « mirum fortasse cuipiam videatur, quod haec clades, quam retuli, loco principis fuerit ». Seneca of Medea: « Abolere ferro pessimam propera luem ». Claudian of Rufinus, Book I: « quo tanta lues eruperit ortu ». Barth. — And *flamma* [flame] is especially to be preserved for this reason, that it agrees more aptly with the myth of Hecuba, the mother of Paris, dreaming that she had given birth to a firebrand, by which the Troad and all Asia would be laid waste by fire. Ed.
- **253 (LL 254)** *Ex agmine cernit* [sees from the battle-line] G. 2.
- **254 (LL 255)** *Seque velut viso perterritus angue* [And as if terrified by the sight of a snake]. G. 2 has *Sicque velut*. The simile made by Homer, *Il.* III, 33 seqq., which our author here expresses very briefly, Virgil, *Aen.* II, 378 seqq., employed of Androgeos: « Obstupuit, retroque pedem cum voce repressit. Improvisum aspris veluti qui sentibus anguem Pressit humi nitens, trepidusque repente refugit ». More briefly Ovid, *Fast.* II, 341: « Attonitusque metu rediit: ceu saepe viator Turbatum viso rettulit angue pedem ». Juvenal, I, 43: « Palleat, ut nudis pressit qui calcibus anguem ».
- **256 (LL 257)** *Perfusum terrore videt, o dedecus* [sees him drenched in terror: 'O disgrace!'] G. 2.
- **257 (LL 258)** *Generisque infamia nostri* [And the infamy of our race]. Ovid, *Metam.* VIII, 97: « o nostri infamia saecli ».
- **259 (LL 260)** *Hospitis expugnare toros* [To storm the bed of one's host], that is, to capture and corrupt a wife by solicitation, just as they are said to be stormed [ex- / -pugnari]...

### Notes

- **(cont.)** [...ex]pugnari [to be taken by storm]: they are said to be taken by storm who, unwilling and resisting, are overcome by entreaties; in which sense Suetonius used it, Caes. 1, Tiber. 21. In Ovid, Her. XVII, 3, Helen to Paris: « Ausus es, hospitii temeratis, advena, sacris, Legitimam nuptae sollicitare fidem ». Propertius, III, 13, 9: « Haec etiam clausas expugnant arma pudicas ». — Rutilius, Itin. I, 359: « Aurea legitimas expugnant munera taedas, Virgineosque sinus aureus imber emit ». Ed. — On the use of the verb expugnare, Barth has noted more on Statius, Theb. IV, 187.
- **262 (LL 263)** *Hic animos ostende* [Show your courage here]. Virgil, Aen. VI, 261: « Nunc animis opus, Aenea, nunc pectore firmo ». — G. 2 reads *nil adjuvat armis*.
- **263 (LL 264)** *Duro Mars milite gaudet* [Mars rejoices in a hardy soldier]. Helen to Paris in the passage cited above [Ovid, Her. XVII], v. 253: « Apta magis Veneri, quam sint tua corpora Marti ».
- **264 (LL 265)** *Dum jaceas in amore* [While you lie in love], that is, broken by lust you languish and idle, unfit for warfare. Such is the passage in Virgil, Catal. V, 1: « Jacere me, quod alta non possim, putas, Ut ante, vectari freta, Nec ferre durum frigus, aut aestum pati, Neque arma victoris sequi ».
- **265 (LL 266)** *Et nostrum fundemus* [And we shall pour out our own blood]: so Guelf. 2, which I have preferred to the vulgate reading *ut nostrum fundamus*. — *In hoste* [against the enemy] is opposed to the preceding *in amore tuo* [in your love]. Moreover, in these verses the author seems to have had in mind Virgil, Aen. XI, 371, where Drances says to Turnus: « Scilicet, ut Turno contingat regia conjux, Nos, animae viles, inhumata infletaque turba, Sternamur campis ». And the author of the speech of Achilles in the Parthenon, which we gave above, v. 57: « Scilicet, ut conjux viduo reddatur Atridi, Procumbat vilis Teucrorum victima Achilles ».
- **266 (LL 267)** Here begins the Helmstedt MS.
- **268 (LL 269)** *Populus* [the people], for *exercitus* [the army], as Barth observed, Adv. LIX, 1. Similarly below, vv. 280 and 342.
- **269 (LL 270)** *Conferte manus* [Join battle / Engage hand-to-hand]. Virg. Aen. X, 876; XI, 283.
- **270 (LL —)** *Vestrum nunc*. This verse is missing in G. 2.
- **271** *Quae contra*. The Leipzig edition has *quem contra*, as does also A.

| 288 | 288 | Ille virum forti venientem reppulit ictu , |  |

### Notes

- **273** *Nam ut mihi conjux*, G. 2.
- **274** *Pronaque luxuria* [and prone to lust], that is, easy and inclined toward Venus. The reading of codex G. 2 and A., *Parvaque luxuria*, seems almost more suitable, from which one might derive *Pravaque*. What follows, *potior virtutis honore* [I obtain the honor of virtue], I have transferred from the Helmstedt MS and G. 2, since the vulgate editions had *fungor virtutis amore*, which both gives an unsuitable sense and does not cohere with the preceding *nec mihi*.
- **275** *Dextramque viri tentare* [And to test the right hand of the hero], to experience by fighting what the strength and right hand of the hero can do. Virg. Aen. II, 334: « vix primi praelia tentant ». Codex G. 2 transposes the words: *Nec vires tentare viri dextramque recuso*. — Tacitus, Germ. XXXIV: « Ipsum quin etiam Oceanum tentavimus ». Ovid, Epist. VII, 121: « bellis peregrina et femina tentor ». Thus *tentare* is used of undertaking and attempt, as *tentare aequor*, *vias*, and the like. See Burmann's note on Val. Flacc. Argon. I, 529. Ed.
- **279** G. 2 has *Post hoc descendit ut*, wrongly. Virgil, Aen. XII, 696, treating of the similar single combat of Turnus and Aeneas: « Discessere omnes medii, spatiumque dedere ». Drakenborch on Silius Italicus, XVII, 1, observed that *discedere* and *descendere* are frequently confused by copyists.
- **280** *Depositis populus telis* [The army, laying down their weapons], the Helmstedt MS. So Ovid, Met. XII, 147: « positis pars utraque substitit armis ». Virg. Aen. XII, 707: « Armaque deposuere humeris »; and in verse 710: « ut vacuo patuerunt aequore campi ».
- **281** *Toto procedit ab agmine* [He steps forth from the whole battle-line]: so I have written from H. and G. 2. The vulgate editions wrongly read *discedit ab agmine*.
- **285** *Nec longum laetabere* [Nor shall you rejoice for long]. Virgil, Aen. X, 740. But this whole phrase is drawn from Ovid, Metam. V, 64: « arcus Arripit, et, Mecum tibi sint certamina, dixit, Nec longum pueri fato laetabere ».
- **286** *Quae te Mox rapuit regem* [Who soon snatched you as king].

## p. 541

PDF page 551.

| Verse | Wernsdorf | Text | Printed |
|---|---|---|---|
| 289 | 289 | Seque gradu celeri recipit, longeque frementem |  |
| 290 | 290 | Hastam deinde jacit, quam devitavit Atrides, | 290 |
| 291 | 291 | Inque vicemi misso fixisset pectora telo |  |
| 292 | 292 | Praedonis Phrygii, nisi vastum ferrea corpus |  |
| 293 | 293 | Texisset lorica viri septemplice tergo. |  |
| 294 | 294 | Insequitur juxta clamor, tuni adversus uterque |  |
| 295 | 295 | Constitit, et galea galeam terit, et pede plantam | 295 |

### Notes

- **(cont.)** [...Quae te Mox rapuit regem.] "I do not understand that *te rapuit regem*. In Homer the originator, it is that the victor will have everything. I would read *quae me Mox rapiet regem*, who will of her own accord snatch me into her embraces, as you snatched her. Yet it is more likely that something else lies hidden, nor does this writer always adhere strictly to Homer, nor is there anything of the kind in him; but Menelaus addresses Jove himself as he enters upon the fight or duel." Barth, Advers. LIX, 1. I too do not understand the pointed meaning (*acumen*) that ought to reside in these words; nor does Barth's conjecture quite satisfy me. Perhaps it is better: *quae me Mox capiet regem* [who will soon take me as her king].
- **289** *Frementem hastam* [A roaring spear], which, once hurled, makes a sound as it cleaves the air. Virgil is accustomed to say *stridentem* [whirring], as in Aen. IX, 418; X, 645, 776. Yet I do not know whether we should rather read here *trementem* [quivering], which the same poet used, Aen. XII, 94.
- **290** A. and L. read *Quamquam devitat*. According to Homer, Il. III, 360, Paris avoided the spear of the son of Atreus, not the son of Atreus that of Paris. And our author narrates several particulars of this combat differently.
- **291** *Fixisset corpora ferro*, G. 2.
- **292** *Praedonis Phrygii* [Of the Phrygian robber]. The insult *praedo* [robber/ravisher] is frequent against adulterers and abductors of girls, specifically against Paris on account of the abduction of Helen, as in Stat. Achill. I, 45, and against Aeneas on account of Lavinia, in Virg. Aeneid VII, 362: « Perfidus alta petens, abducta virgine, praedo »; and Aen. XI, 484, where he is called « Phrygius praedo ». Pluto is so called on account of the rape of Proserpina in Ovid, Met. V, 521; Fast. IV, 591. Instead of *ferrea corpus*, H. and G. 2 read *ferrea pectus*.
- **293** *Septemplice tergo* [With a sevenfold hide], which was covered and protected with seven layers of hide. Elsewhere sevenfold shields are usually attributed to heroes. Virg. Aen. XII, 925; Ovid, Met. XIII, 2 and 347.
- **294** G. 2 has *Dum adversus*. Anton de Rooy, Spicileg. crit. p. 97, considers that *arrectus* might perhaps be substituted for *adversus*, from Virg. Aen. V, 426: « Constitit in digitos extemplo arrectus uterque ». But he himself advises that nothing be changed. And indeed *arrectus* [poised on tiptoe] is suitable for those fighting with the boxing-glove (*caestus*), as in Virgil; not for those attacking with the sword, as here. So in Statius, Theb. I, 412: « tum vero erectus uterque Exsertare humeros, nudamque lacessere pugnam ».
- **295** *Constitit, et galea galeam ferit* [He stood firm, and helmet strikes helmet]. The distinguished Bondam, loc. cit. p. 153, thinks that these words of Ovid were before the mind of the author when writing this, Met. IX, 43: « eratque Cum pede pes junctus: totoque ego pectore pronus, Et digitos digitis, et frontem fronte premebam ». And indeed here, as in other places, an opportunity grasped by the author for imitating Ovid is apparent, since the narrative of Homer itself did not suggest such things.

| 296 | 296 | Conjungit, stridet mucro mucrone corusco, |  |
| 297 | 297 | Corpus collectum tegitur fulgentibus armis. |  |
| 298 | 298 | !Non aliter fortes nitida pro conjuge tauri |  |

| 301 | 301 | Tunc memor Atrides raptae sibi conjugis instat, |  |
