| 303 | 303 | Cedentemque retro dum desuper adpetit hostem , |  |
| 304 | 304 | Splendidus extremas galeae percussus ad oras |  |
| 305 | 305 | Dissiluit mucro : gemuerunt agmina Graium. | 305 |
| 306 | 306 | Tunc vero ardescit, quamvis manus ense careret, |  |
| 307 | 307 | Et juvenem arrepta prosternit casside victor, |  |

### Notes

- **296** The Helmstedt MS transposes the words: *mucro stridet mucrone*. Guelf. 2 reads differently: *Constringit, stridetque mucro mucrone corusco*.
- **297** *Corpus collectum* [His body drawn together], that is, drawn back and enclosed within the shield. Virg. Aen. XII, 491: « Substitit Aeneas, et se collegit in arma »; and X, 412: « seque in sua colligit arma ». In place of *tegitur*, the Helmstedt MS has *petitur fulgentibus*, erroneously. — Cf. Cuper, Observ. book I, ch. 12, p. 90. Ed.
- **298** *Non aliter fortes* [Not otherwise the brave...]. This simile is plainly borrowed from Ovid, who in Met. IX, 46, after the words cited above, continues: « Non aliter fortes vidi concurrere tauros, Quum pretium pugnae, toto nitidissima saltu Expetitur conjux ».
- **300** *Atque diu rigido*: so the printed editions, but *Jamque diu*, A., which I have preferred. — *Captabant pectora* [They were aiming at the breasts], that is, an opportune place for wounding the body. — So in Virgil, Aen. XII, 920: « telum Aeneas fatale coruscat, Sortitus fortunam oculis ». Ed. — The vulgate editions read *Corpora telo*, but I considered *ferro*, which H. and G. 2 have, to be more suitable.
- **304** A. gives *Percussit ad oras* along with the old editions, but the distinguished Dorp, Observ. p. 116, from the reading of a manuscript codex of the Leiden Library emends to *percussus ad oras Dissiluit*, which is also the reading of G. 2. Moreover, Barth, Adv. LVIII, 14, in vain referred *ad oras* to the faults of our writer, because he read *percussit*.
- **305** A. reads *Desiluit mucro* along with the vulgates, but Barth, p. 2770, already noted that this is corrupt, and Dorp loc. cit. and Dussen in Prodr. p. 21 judged that *dissiluit* should be read. Codex G. 2 prefers the same reading, and our author himself repeats it below in a complete verse, 968, and has it from an imitation of Virgil, who, recording the same misfortune of Turnus, says in Aen. XII, 741: « Mortalis mucro, glacies seu futilis, ictu Dissiluit »; and Ovid, Metam. V, 171 ff.: « Non circumspectis exactum viribus ensem Fregit, et extrema percussae parte columnae Lamina dissiluit; dominique in gutture fixa est ».
- **306** The editions have *Ense carebat*; H. has *careret*, which I think should be preferred.
- **307** *Juvenem rapta*, G. 2; which is erroneous.

## p. 543

PDF page 553.

| Verse | Wernsdorf | Text | Printed |
|---|---|---|---|
| 308 | 308 | Ad socios traheretque , nisi caligine csca |  |
| 309 | 309 | Texisset Cytherea virum , subjectaque mento |  |
| 310 | 310 | Fortia rupisset iaxatis vincula nodis, | 310 |
| 311 | 311 | Ultimus ilie dies Paridi foret : abstrahit auro |  |
| 312 | 312 | Fuigentem galeam secum Mehelaus, et ardens |  |
| 313 | 313 | In medios mittit proceres, rursumque recurrit, |  |
| 314 | 314 | Et magnam validis contorsit viribus hastam |  |
| 315 | 315 | In ciadem Phrygii; sua quem Yenus eripit hosti, | 315 |
| 316 | 316 | Et secum in thalamos defert testudine cultos : |  |

### Notes

- **308** G. 2 reads *Traheret, nisi tum caligine*; A. has *Traheret, nisi caligine*. These words and what follow do not cohere well with verse 311. And hence, taking into account the manuscripts that I have cited, I think we should write: « Et juvenem arrepta prosternens casside victor Ad socios trahit, et nisi tum caligine caeca ... Ultimus ille dies Paridi foret ».
- **313** *Rursusque recurrit*, G. 2.
- **314** *Contorsit viribus hastam* [Hurled the spear with all his might], are the words of Virgil, Aen. II, 50.
- **315** *Sua quem Venus* [Whom his own Venus...]. For Alexander was her devoted worshipper, whence he called her *sua* [his own]. So he says in v. 877 that its own deities were added to the sea: « Addideratque freto sua numina, Nerea magnum ». Moreover, Venus herself commends Paris, devoted to her, to Helen in Homer, after she rescued him from the hands of Menelaus. Barth, Adv. LIX, 1. That the pronoun *suus* often signifies favorable, appropriate, and advantageous escapes no one versed in the reading of Roman writers, so that it is superfluous to demonstrate this with more examples. Horace, Epod. 9, 30: « Cretam ventis iturus non suis ».
- **316** A. and the old editions have as a written reading *testudine structos*. Barth observes from this passage that our writer is not accustomed, as other poets do, to lengthen a preceding syllable on account of the following letters *sc* or *st*, and adduces another example from our author, v. 791: *Promachum quoque sternit atrocem*. But I consider that this is done very rarely by good poets, except in the middle of a word. You have an example in Virg. Aen. VI, 687, where however the reading also varies. And in our author, the vulgate editions indeed have *testudine structos*, but G. 2 exhibits the other words *cultos*, *tectos*, *comptos* together; of which I think *cultos* is the true reading on account of our author's customary imitation of Ovid, who says in Met. II, 737: « Pars secreta domus ebore et testudine cultos Tres habuit thalamos ». Moreover, our author spoke of tortoise-shell bedchambers (*thalamos testudineos*) not according to the mind of Homer and the custom of the heroic age, but according to more recent luxury. Nor in this passage of Homer, III, 390, where the bedchamber is also mentioned, is any material indicated. But Pliny, book IX, sect. 13, records that « cutting the shells of tortoises into veneers, and overlaying couches and cabinets with them » was first introduced at Rome by Cornelius Pollio, who lived around the times of Sulla. Before these times, to speak with Juvenal, XI, 93: « Nemo inter curas et seria duxit habendum, Qualis in Oceani fluctu testudo na-

- **(cont.)** (cont.) [-taret, Clarum Trojugenis factura ac nobile fulcrum ».] Virgil says *varios pulchra testudine postes* [doorposts adorned with beautiful tortoise-shell], Georg. II, 463. — We have seen the tortoise-shell couch (*testudineum lectum*) in vol. II, p. 460 of this work, to which passage Martial, XII, 66 [67], can be added: « Gemmantes prima fulgent testudine lecti ». Ed.
- **321** *Vidi puduitque videre* [I saw, and was ashamed to see]. Bondam, p. 154, also points out that this half-verse is from Ovid, Met. XIII, 223, where Ulysses says to Ajax: « Vidi, puduitque videre, Quum tu terga dares ». And anyone who examines and compares these things more carefully will easily see that our author, in writing the *Epitome*, especially looked to this book—in which the contest of Ulysses and Ajax over the arms of Achilles and the principal events of the Trojan War are summarily set forth—and borrowed very many expressions from it.
- **323** *Pulvere crines* [Hair with dust]. Virg. Aeneid XII, 99; Ovid, Metam. VIII, 529.
- **325** *Discuteret* [Should dispel / shatter], just as Frontinus, IV, 7, 31, said « consilia hostium discutere » [to shatter the plans of the enemy]; and Silius Italicus, VII, 153, from the correction of Drakenborch, said « discutere dolos » [to shatter stratagems]. — Anton de Rooy, loc. cit. p. 97, prefers *Mente relictae* [faint of mind / abandoned by her senses], because we are said to be *relinqui mente*, just as *linqui animo* [to swoon], *deseri viribus* [to be deserted by strength], *defici annis* [to fail through age], and similar expressions.
- **326** *Fugerat ore color* [Color had fled from her face]. This half-verse is also from Ovid, Her. XI, 27. G. 2 reads *Ore calor* [warmth from her face]; compare also Ovid, Her. XIV, 37: « Sanguis abit, mentemque calor corpusque relinquit ».
- **328** *An nondum*, G. 2. *An non vaga fama*, L. — *Pervenit ad aures* [Reached the ears]. So Ovid, Met. V, 256: « Fama novi fontis nostras pervenit ad aures »; and Virg. Aen. II, 81: « Fando aliquid, si forte tuas pervenit ad aures ». Ed.
- **331** *Dixit, tum largis*, G. 2 and A.
- **332** *Tristis Alexander*, L. and T.

- **(cont.)** In place of which A. [the Annaberg edition] has *Ad quam Priamides, non me*.
- **333** The Helmstedt codex reads *Cautae Palladis*, which is perhaps better.
- **336** *Post haec amplexu* [After this, in an embrace]. G. 2 has *Post hoc*, and A. has *per mutua corpora victis*. There is no doubt that in these verses the writer looked to the well-known passage of Maro, *Aeneid* VIII, 405: « Optatos dedit amplexus, placidumque petivit Conjugis infusus gremio per membra soporem ».
- **337** *Incubuit* [lay down] G. 2, A., and L., which I preferred to the vulgate *Incumbit*. For *Cygneidos*, H. corruptly puts *Etneidos*. Now Helen is called *Cygneis* because she was believed to be the daughter of Jove transformed into a swan; I do not know whether any other poet has used that name, although the Tyndarid [Helen] is called « cygneo edita Tyndaris ovo » by Virgil, *Eleg. ad Messal.* 27, and Helen herself says in Ovid, *Her.* XVII, 55: « Dat mihi Leda Jovem, cygno decepta, parentem ».
- **338** *Flammas Trojaeque suasque* [Both Troy's flames and his own]. Witty regarding Paris, whose love for Helen was destined to be the ruin of Troy. This expression also seems to rely on that passage of Paris's letter in Ovid, *Heroides* XVI, 45 sq., where Paris interprets the dream of his mother—who before the day of giving birth had seemed to bring forth a burning torch—as referring to the ardour of his own passion that was fatal to Troy: « Arsuram Paridis vates canit Ilion igni; Pectoris, ut nunc est, fax fuit illa mei ».
- **339** *In agmine Troum* [In the battle-line of the Trojans]. Helmst. reads *ab agmine*.
- **341** *Frater* [brother], Agamemnon. — H. reads *Acuens in praelia dextras* [Whetting right hands for battle], but *in bella catervas* [crowds for war] has been added by another hand.
- **342** The manuscript A. and the printed editions have *Populos Phrygas*; and Barth, *Adv.* page 2770, asserts that this is an elegant expression, but that only the army or soldiers are understood: in which he is quite mistaken; for without doubt this reading is corrupt, and on the authority of codex H. and G. 2 we must read *pulsos Phrygas* [the routed Phrygians].
- **343** *Servarique jubet* [and orders to be observed]: so H., G. 2, and A.; L. has *servareque jubet*; the remaining editions have *Et servare jubet*, which is less suitable.
- **344** *Certamen haberent* [should hold a contest]. The phrase

### Notes

- **(cont.)** [The phrase] does not seem worthy enough of poetic style, and what occurs in the following verse, *concilium habuit* [held a council], is an unpleasant repetition. Nor does it seem sufficiently defended by the verse of Naso [Ovid], *Met.* XIII, 159: « Ergo operum quoniam nudum certamen habetur ».
- **346** *Foedera turbavit* [broke the treaty], for which G. 2 erroneously gives *turpavit* [defiled]. Virgil used the verb *confundere*, expressing the Homeric συγχέειν more suitably, *Aen.* V, 496: « Pandare, qui quondam jussus confundere foedus, In medios telum torsisti primus Achivos ».
- **347** *Volatile telum* [winged missile] from Virg. *Aen.* IV, 71, and Ovid, *Metam.* VII, 841. H. and G. 2 prefer *Volatile ferrum* [winged iron].
- **349** H. and G. 2 have *Dissecat* [cuts through], which I preferred to the vulgate *Desecat* [cuts away]. The verb *dissecare* in this connection is less commonly used by other poets, who are accustomed to employ *rumpere* and *lacerare*. See Virg. *Aeneid* XII, 98; Ovid, *Met.* XII, 117.
- **350** H. reads *Et sua castra petit* [and seeks his own camp], which is perhaps better.
- **351** Guelf. 2 gives *Podalirius simul et Chirurgicus*, which is a scribe's gloss. — *Paeoniis herbis* [with Paeonian herbs] is from Virgil, *Aen.* VII, 769. Our author here names Podalirius as treating the wound of Menelaus, but Homer names Machaon, IV, 193. — See the mention of Podalirius above, v. 217. Ed.
- **352** *Atque iterum in caedes ..... mittit* [And again sends ... into slaughter]. Virgil of Aeneas, after his wound was healed, *Aen.* XII, 429: « atque opera ad majora remittit ». G. 2 corruptly has *Atque iterum incedens*: unless one wishes to correct from it: *Atque iterum incedentem horrenda in praelia mittit*; but this is almost barbarous.
- **354** *Et dolor in pugnam* [And grief (drives them) into battle]. So Virg. *Aen.* VIII, 500: « quos justus in hostem Fert dolor, et merita incendit Mezentius ira ».
- **355** G. 2 gives *Multumque cruoris utrimque* [and much blood on both sides].
- **356** *Sternuntur corpora campis* [Bodies are laid low across the plains]. Virg. *Aen.* II, 364: « Plurima perque vias sternuntur inertia passim Corpora ».

### Notes

- **359** *Volat cunctis de partibus imber* [A shower flies from all sides]: so G. 2, which I preferred to the vulgate *volant ex cunctis partibus imbres*. Virg. *Aen.* XII, 283: « it toto turbida caelo Tempestas telorum, ac ferreus ingruit imber ». — By an inverse metaphor, Avianus spoke of *pharetratos imbres seu nubes* [quivered showers or clouds], *fab.* XLI, v. 16. Cf. our author, v. 746. Ed.
- **360** In the vulgate editions is *Antilochus rigido demersus in umbras*. Codex A. has *magnas demersus in umbras*. Guelf. 2: *Stygias dimissus ad umbras*. Whence I published *demissus*, which is Virgilian, *Aen.* II, 398. Furthermore, *Antilochus* must necessarily be corrupt, because Antilochus is one of the Greeks, and in this passage the slaughter of Trojans is being discussed. Correctly therefore did Bondam judge, emending on p. 154: « Occidit Antilochi rigido demersus in umbra Ense Thalysiades »; namely Echepolus, from Homer's *Iliad* IV, 458. And this is confirmed by codex H., which writes *Ense Thalacides*. G. 2 has *Chalestiades*.
- **361** The vulgate editions have *Ense Calestiadis*. See the preceding note. G. 2 has *Lumina mittit*.
- **363** The vulgate and manuscript copies had *Amphione satum* [sprung from Amphion], which Bondam corrected from *Il.* IV, 473, to *Anthemione satum* [sprung from Anthemion].
- **364** I restored *Transfigit pectora* [Pierces the breast] from Helmst. and G. 2, especially since Homer says this, IV, 480. The vulgate editions, however, together with A., gave *transfixit corpora*. Homer says nothing of a hardened weapon (*praedurato telo*), but the author perhaps alluded to that Virgilian phrase, *Aeneid* VII, 524: « Stipitibus duris agitur sudibusve praeustis ». Ovid, *Met.* XII, 299: « sude figit obusta ».
- **365** *Purpuream vomit*, etc. [Vomits purple (life)]. The verse is Virgil, *Aen.* IX, 349. — And in Anth. Lat. Burm. vol. I, p. 45, Epith. Incertae, v. 8: « Nunc animam quoque tu purpuream vomeres ». Ed.
- **366** Barth, *Advers.* LIX, 1, thinks we should read *Ora riget moriens*, which is not pleasing.
- **367** Codex A. and editions L. and T. read *Viribus adversis*, but Helmst. and G. 2 read *adversam*; whence I correct to *adversum*, and refer it to Ajax. — Moreover, *Viribus* belongs with *maximus*. Thus Ovid speaks, *Met.* XII, 116: « hastam Misit in adversum Lycia de plebe Menoeten ». For *conatus*, I suspect we should read *connixus corpore toto* [straining with his whole body] from Virgil, *Aen.* V, 642, and X, 127. G. 2 gives both *Pectore* and *corpore* together.
- **368** For *in Ajacem*, Helmstedt reads *in Aeacidem*.

|---|---|---|---|
| 369 | 369 | Inque liostem cecidit; nam flxit in inguine Leucon. |  |
| 370 | 370 | Concidit infelix prostratus vulnere forti, | 370 |
| 371 | 371 | Et carpit virides moribundus dentibus berbas. |  |
| 372 | 372 | linpiger Atrides casu commotus amici |  |
| 373 | 373 | Democoonta petit , teloque adversa trabali |  |

### Notes

- **369** *Inque hostem cecidit* [And fell upon an enemy]: supply another [enemy], namely Leucos, whom he presently names. The edition of Torinus reads here: *Inque hostem cadit, ictus in inguine Leucon*, and the manuscript A., along with the Leipzig edition: *Inque hostem cecidit, ictus in inguine Leucon*. Barth, in the cited place, sees that a word is missing in the verse, yet lacks the means to restore it, being destitute of the aid of another manuscript. At length he fills it out: *Inque hostem cadit, adverso ictus in inguine Leucon*. Dorp, however, *Observ.* p. 117, from the margin of a Leiden manuscript emends: *cecidit, namque ictus in inguine*, or even substitutes *tunc* for *namque* from another manuscript of P. Burman. Dussen feels the same, *loc. cit.* p. 23. To me, codex G. 2 seems to come to the rescue best, which presents: *cecidit, transfixit in imagine Leuchon*. Whence I emend: *Inque hostem cecidit; nam fixit in inguine Leucon*. To be sure, one could also read *transfixus in inguine*, but another participle presently follows, *prostratus vulnere forti*, and the fourth case [accusative] *Leucon*, which all books preserve, requires a verb *fixit* or *transfixit*. For the man who is spoken of here is called Leucos in Homer, IV, 491, not Leucon.
- **370** For *Concidit*, Helmst. has *Decidit infelix*.
- **371** *Et carpit virides moribundus* [And dying he grazes the green (grasses)]. Here I find an unusual phrase for the slain and dying who fall forward to the earth. The Homeric formula is ὀδὰξ ἑλεῖν οὖδας (see *Il.* II, 418; XI, 748), which the Latin poets, at least the ancients, imitate by saying *mordere* and *mandere humum* [bite and chew the earth], or *petere, adpetere ore, morsu, terram* [strike the earth with mouth or bite]. Virg. *Aen.* XI, 418: « Procubuit moriens et humum semel ore momordit »; and ibid. v. 669: « cruentam Mandit humum, moriensque suo se in vulnere versat ». Sil. Ital. IX, 383: « Volvitur ille ruens, atque arva hostilia morsu Adpetit ». But as for anyone who, in that sense, said *carpere dentibus herbas* [to pluck the grasses with teeth] instead of *mordere* or *mandere humum*—which is used by poets of the grazing of cattle—I have indeed hitherto found no one. Yet a similar verse is cited by the grammarian Diomedes, book I, p. 336, from Book XX of Cn. Matius's *Iliad*: « Ille hietans herbam moribundo tenuit ore ».
- **372–373** *Impiger Atrides* [The energetic Atrides]. In that reading, although received in all the books, there is either an error of the scribes or an oversight (παρόραμα) of the author himself. The one who is said here to be moved by the fall of his friend, namely Leucos, in Homer is not Atrides, but Ulysses. There Leucos is called Ὀδυσσέος ἐσθλὸς ἑταῖρος, *Il.* IV, 491, and Ulysses, greatly angered at his slaying, advanced into the battle-line and struck Democoon, who confronted him. Hence the distinguished Bondam, *loc. cit.* p. 155, rightly thinks the verse should be read in this or another similar way: *Hinc Laertiades casu commotus amici Democoonta petit*. The vulgate editions have *Demophoonta*, which is a more famous name among heroes; but closer to the truth is *Demononta* in G. 2. — *Teloque trabali*, that is, with a great spear. Virg. *Aen.* XII, 294.

### Notes

- **374** *Transadigit* [thrusts through], which H. and G. 2 have, seemed to me more fitting than the vulgate *transfixit*. G. 2 at the same time adds *transegit*. — *Vagina ensem eripit* [draws the sword from its sheath] is a Virgilian phrase, *Aen.* IV, 579, but hardly suitable for this place; for no other fight is being prepared.
- **376** G. 2 has *Conscidit* for *Concidit*. The vulgate editions have *Occidit*. Here I should prefer to read with Dussen, p. 24, *moribundo vertice* [with dying head], because he seems to have expressed Ovid, who in *Met.* V, 84 says: « Et resupinus humum moribundo vertice pulsat »; and in the same work, XII, 118: « Quo plangente gravem moribundo vertice terram ».
- **377** In the vulgate editions these words are extremely faulty: *Post haec Aegaeum saxi dejecerat ictu Impiger Umbrasides*. Helmst. gives *dejecit ab ictu*, not badly. But if we compare the narrative sequence in Homer, IV, 517, in this place Peiros, son of Imbrasus, struck Diores, son of Amarynceus, with a stone, and the surviving name *Umbrasides*, thoroughly like the truth, shows that our author is speaking of him. Since, however, Homer (II, 615) says of Diores, as also of Amphimachus, Thalpius, and Polyxenus, that they inhabited noble Elis, the distinguished Bondam (p. 157) considers that Diores could have been called *Eleus* [the Elean] by our author, and hence corrects: *Post haec Eleum saxi dejecerat ictu Impiger Imbrasides*. But to me this method of emendation seems too far-fetched, and the remedy brought forward by the most learned Dorp (*Obs.* p. 117) pleases me more, when from Homer's very verses (IV, 517 and 520) he established this reading: *Jamque Amaryncidem saxi dejecerat ictu Piros Imbrasides*. Codex G. 2 supports this emendation, which, although in an absurd reading, preserves the ruins of the true one and writes *Inque mare Egeum saxi*, in the same way that Dorp affirms it is read in two other manuscripts. Moreover, the vulgate *Impiger* could easily have arisen from the genuine *Piros*; although I do not deny that *Impiger* can remain, and that it suffices for Peiros to be called here by his patronymic alone, as was done with Diores.
- **379** The vulgate editions read *Tuncque avidus*. G. 2 has *Dumque avidus*. A. has *Dumque avidus praedo*. But I preferred to write with Helmst. *Dum cupidus*.
- **381** H. falsely reads *Annosaque tempora*. A. and L. have *annosaque pectora*; Tor. and Sp. have *animosaque pectora*. As for the word *scapulas*, which our author uses rather often, no epic poet, so far as I know, has used it; I think because it was considered vulgar and plebeian, since it is especially found among the Comic writers. Our author either knowingly neglected that consideration,

### Notes

- **(cont.)** [either knowingly neglected that consideration,] or did not know it. — Ovid, however, used it in a lighter subject to designate properly that part of the body, *Art. Am.* III, 273: « Conveniunt tenues scapulis analectides altis ». And Claudian also used this word. Ed.
- **383** *Stratusque super sua palpitat arma* [And stretched out he throbs upon his arms]. Comparable is Virgil, *Aen.* X, 488: « Corruit in vulnus: sonitum super arma dedere ».
- **385** *Pugnabat* [was fighting]; for which L. wrongly presents *pugnatur*.
- **389** The vulgate editions have *Hic postquam*. But H. and G. 2 have *Sed postquam*, which I preferred. A. has *longe tendentia*.
- **390** *Tumidumque increscere Martem* [And that swelling war was growing]: perhaps modeled on this passage of Maro, *Aen.* IX, 687: « Tum magis increscunt animis discordibus irae ».
- **391** The vulgate editions read *eminet hostis*; Dussen (p. 24) by conjecture, and the distinguished Dorp (p. 119) from the Leiden MS restored the sound reading *imminet*, which is supported by codex Helmst. and edition L.
- **392** The editions have *Adversas prosternit*. I substituted *et versas*, which is both more suitable and is read in the Leiden MS, as Dorp testifies.
- **393** Torinus and Spondanus gave *Huc illuc volat ense ferox*. H. and G. 2 have *Huc illuc ensemque ferox*; A. and L. have *enseque ferox hastaque*; G. 2 wrongly has *hastamque retorsit*.
- **394** *Bellica Pallas adest* [Warlike Pallas is at hand]. These words are taken from Ovid, *Met.* V, 47: « Bellica Pallas adest, et protegit aegide fratrem, Datque animos ». — He gives *flagrantia ignibus arma* [arms blazing with fires] to Diomedes after Homer, V, 4, who says that Pallas caused an unwearied fire to radiate from his helmet and shield: Δαῖέ οἱ ἐκ κόρυθός τε καὶ ἀσπίδος ἀκάματον πῦρ. And below, v. 467, our author says of Diomedes: *flagrantibus irruit armis*.
- **396** *Saeva leaena* [Fierce lioness]. Virgil rather frequently uses the comparison of a lion rushing upon flocks, as in *Aen.* IX, 339 sq. and X, 723 sqq. Homer indeed compares the onset of Diomedes to a tor-

### Notes

- **(cont.)** [-rent, sweeping away bridges, fences, and crops], but our author more often abandons Homer's model and, wherever there is an opportunity, prefers to use the resources supplied by Virgil and Ovid.
- **397** The Helmstedt MS has *Agmina circum*.
- **400** *Virginis armigerae* [Of the armor-bearing maiden]: Pallas. I wonder whether we should read *armiferae* [weapon-bearing]. For she is often called that by Ovid, as in *Met.* XIV, 475; *Fast.* III, 681; *Eleg. ad Liv.* 22. She is called *armipotens* [mighty in arms] by Virgil, *Aen.* II, 425, and XII, 483.
- **402** The vulgates have *Et exstinctos* [and extinguished / slain]; but Dussen pointed out, p. 25, that we should read *exstructos* [piled up], and the Helmstedt MS displays this very reading; and that it was borrowed from Naso [Ovid] is apparent from *Met.* V, 88: « Sternit, et exstructos morientum calcat acervos »; in which passage Heinsius also noted that many of the ancient manuscripts read *exstinctos*, while Burmann put *adstructos* from certain others.
- **403** *Dumque furit* [And while he rages]. Dussen wants *ferit* [strikes], which I do not think necessary; on the contrary, *furit* is all the better because Horace employs it as if characteristic of Diomedes, *Carm.* I, 15, 27: « Ecce furit te reperire atrox Tydides, melior patre ». — The printed editions with cod. A read *Videt ecce Thoantis* [behold, he sees (the sons) of Thoas]. But the celebrated Bondam, p. 157, proves that we must read *Daretis* [of Dares], whom Homer names as the father of Phegeus and Idaeus, V, 10; and this is confirmed by the Helmstedt MS, in which *ecce Danetis* occurs. Likewise in the following verse that MS presents *Phegeum Idaeumque*, just as Homer names them.
- **405** The vulgates offer *Aegeum Idaeumque*; but see the note to verse 403. G. 2 has *Cuspide praepes*.
- **406** *Occupat ille* [He forestalls / strikes]: thus I have written on the authority of codex H. The vulgates, along with A, have *Occupat ante*.
- **407** *Vibratumque solo* [And quivering in the ground]. The Helmstedt MS reads *Vitatumque* [and avoided], which I wonder whether it ought to be preferred. — *Stetit* [stood] is from Virgil, *Aen.* II, 52, on the spear of Laocoon: « stetit illa tremens ». Ed.
- **408** *Ingentem torquet*, etc. [Hurls the huge (spear)], are the very words of Virgil in the cited passage of the *Aeneid*.
- **409** A. and T. have *Transfoditque* [and pierced through]. H. has *Transadiitque*, from which we have given *Transadigit* [and drives through]. Virgil, *Aen.* IX, 544: « pectora duro Transfossi ligno ».

### Notes

- **410** *Eminet, et prodit*, etc. [Stands out and comes forth]. Thus Ovid, *Metam.* IX, 127: « terga sagitta Trajicit: exstabat ferrum de pectore aduncum »; and book V, 138: « Torquet in hunc hastam, media quae nare recepta Cervice exacta est, in partesque eminet ambas ». In this sense, a discharged missile that exits through the other side is spoken of by Grattius in *Halieut.* v. 62. — See vol. I, part 1, page 224 of this work. Ed.
- **411** *Calidum de pectore flumen* [A warm river from his chest]. Virg. *Aen.* IX, 414: « Volvitur ille, vomens calidum de pectore flumen »; and book XI, 668: « Sanguinis ille vomens rivos cadit ».
- **413** *Stricto celer advolat* [Quickly flies up with drawn (sword)]. The Helmstedt MS has *celer extracto advolat*.
- **414** *Cupit exsistere vindex* [Desires to stand forth as an avenger]. This phrase seems to Barth to smack of barbarism, *Advers.* LVIII, 14, and LIX, 1, p. 2770; and he proves from a certain passage of Alboin the presbyter that medieval writers used the verb *subsistere* in the same way. And indeed that expression makes for a sluggish verse, from which poets of the ancient age seem universally to have refrained. Yet I would not on that account assign it to barbarisms, and perhaps *exsistere* in this passage is put for *exstare*, *eminere*, *conspicuum esse* [to stand out, to be prominent, to be conspicuous]. Concerning the verb *subsistere*, it is more certain that it belongs to the barbarous Latinity of the scholastics. — But *exsistere* in the sense of *fieri* [to become] or *esse* [to be] seems to have been introduced by writers of a lower age. For Julius Exsuperantius writes thus: « Facile enim poterant exsistere proditores; quia egestas haud facile habetur sine damno ». And Lactantius Placidus in *Argum. Metam. Ovid.* lib. VI, fab. 3: « Latona questa cum filiis, quod suarum injuriarum ultores non exsisterent ». Ed.
- **415** A. has *Neque vim fortis nec fortia*; G. 2 has *nec fortis sustinet*.
- **416** *Quem tum contra defendere*: thus G. 2 seems to read.
- **417** H. and A. have *Decepta sui*; so too G. 2, and at the same time *defecta*. Barth, p. 2770, corrects to *discerpta* [torn to pieces], with Dussen agreeing, p. 20. I preferred, following the lead of the Helmstedt MS, *derepta* [snatched away], namely from the nest of its mother.
- **419** G. 2 has *Anxia fato*.
- **420** G. 2 has *Quodque potest levius*.

### Notes

- **424** L. incorrectly has *Nec minus intentus armis*; A. has *in Troicos armis*.
- **425** H. has *Funera miscet*.
- **426** A. and T. have *Fatis ductus occurrit iniquis*.
- **427** *Infelix Rhodius* [Unhappy Rhodian] is the common reading. G. 2 and A. have *Rodius*; but we must read *Hodius* from Homer, *Il.* V, 39.
- **428** G. 2 has *Diverberat hasta*.
- **429** *Hinc petit Idomeum de adversa parte ruentem Merione satum*: thus A. and the vulgates. H. has *Idomeneum*, and G. 2 omits *de*. Bondam, p. 158, attempted to correct this utterly corrupt reading thus: *Hinc petit Idomeneus de adversa parte ruentem Meione natum*. More felicitously, Dorp, p. 119: *Hinc petit Idomeneus adversa parte ruentem Maeoniden Phaestum*. And this expresses more fully the verse of Homer, V, 43: Ἰδομενεὺς δ᾽ ἄρα Φαῖστον ἐνήρατο Μῄονος υἱόν. And *Maeoniden* seems to me to lie hidden in the reading of the Helmstedt MS and G., which present *Melidione* and *Meridione*.
- **430** *Cujus post funera laetus*: thus the vulgates; but the renowned Dorp, p. 120, emended to *cujus post funera Atrides*. And I profess that I have printed the following three heavily corrupted verses shaped from his emendation, even though they depart not a little from the vulgate reading, because I saw, having compared Homer's verses, *Iliad* V, 49–71, that they could scarcely be constituted otherwise, and were in part also confirmed by the reading of my manuscripts.
- **431** *Et Strophio genitum* [And the son of Strophius]. The vulgate editions have *Astropio*. — H. has *Demisit ad undas*, whence I wanted to write *demittit*. — Similarly above, v. 360, *demissus ad umbras*. Ed.
- **432** *Meriones Phereclum*. The old editions had most defectively *Merionis puerum*, and A. has *Megeonis puerum*; following which reading, Barth, *Advers.* LVIII, 14, hawked it as an idiom of the author, on the grounds that he used *puer* [boy] for a slave. — *Vibrata* [brandished / quivering]. By its repetition, the reading of verse 407 is confirmed. Ed.
- **433** *Pedaeumque Meges*. The older editors falsely published *Pergellumque necat*, and A. has *Pregeumque necat*. G. 2 has *Phregestumque necat fastis*. The Helmstedt MS favors the emendation made: *Phregeumque Megesten vastisque horridus armis*. Burmann's codex agrees with H., as Dorp testifies *loc. cit.*, and the Leiden codex reads *Praegeumque Megeus*.

For *perturbat* is less suitable and falls short of the force of a weapon, to which it is attributed; but *proturbat* is the same as *propulsat* [repels] or *prosternit* [lays low]: and Virgil used it similarly of weapons, *Aen.* X, 801: « Telaque conjiciunt, proturbantque eminus hostem Missilibus », in which passage Heyne also noted that *perturbant* is read in certain manuscripts. So Livy, V, 47, spoke of driving back (*proturbare*) the enemy with weapons and thrown stones; and Lucilius in *Aetna*, v. 62: « validos tum Jupiter ignes Increpat, et jacto (sic ibi scripsi) proturbat fulmine montes »; and I myself noted that *perturbat* was incorrectly substituted in this passage: and the same in several passages

### Notes

- **(cont.)** [of our author], occurring below, will have to be noted. — A. has *Ad Tartara ducit*.
- **449** *Prostratus Pindare*; as above, here G. 2 has *Pandare*.
- **451** *Qua naris fronti conjungitur imae* [Where the nostril joins the lowest part of the forehead]. This seems to have been borrowed from Ovid, *Met.* XII, 315: « inter duo lumina ferrum, Qua naris fronti committitur, accipit, imae ».
- **453** The editions with A. read *Ossaque confossa spargit* [and scatters the pierced bones], and Barth, *Adv.* p. 2770 end, notes that it is a mark of erudition that the author made the syllable long in *confossa* before the letters *sp*; but Dorp and Dussen, in the places cited, saw that *confossi* [of the pierced man] should rather be read, and they testify that the same exists in Burmann's manuscript codex, to which I can add the Helmstedt MS. G. 2, however, differs: *Ossaque cum galea spargit Thitydeius* or *Thitieus ensis*.
- **454** *Jamque manum Aeneas simul et Calydonius* [And now Aeneas and at the same time the Calydonian (joined combat)]. Thus I have written, guided by the Helmstedt MS and G. 2. For the edition of Torinus, along with cod. A., presents a rough verse: *Jamque manus Aeneas et Calydonius heros*. The Leipzig edition also defectively has *Jamque Aeneas simul manus et Calydonius heros*.
- **456** *Undique rimabant* [From all sides they probed]. Thus all the manuscripts and printed copies that I have seen. Yet I hardly think this verb is found in the active voice in a nobler poet. But this is a mark of a later age, in which those verbal forms that had long been obsolete were accustomed to be recalled into use. — We have seen another example, *contemplaverit*, in the fragment of Nemesianus *De aucupio*, v. 3; vol. I, p. 182 of this work. Ed. — Moreover, the sense of the words *rimabant pectora ferro* [they probed their chests with the sword] is: they were watching and testing on what part or in what place they might inflict a wound on the adversary; and verse 458 makes this clearer, and then in verse 593 the same formula is repeated more fully. — But perhaps we should read *limabant* [they grazed], that is, *stringebant* [touched lightly], *terebant* [rubbed], or *libabant* [grazed], which occurs in verse 610. Ed. — Hence it is apparent that the words with which the Helmstedt MS props up this verse are foolish and proceed from interpolation: *Undique rimabant inimicaque corpora fundunt*; but G. 2 reads *corpora ferro*. Similarly Saleius Bassus in *Carm. ad Pisonem*, v. 171: « Et nunc vivaci scrutaris pectora dextra, Nunc latus adversum nec opino percutis ictu ». — Silius indeed seems to describe this act of probing and observing more brilliantly, XIII, 163: « At non idem animus Rutulo; spectatur, et omni Corpore perlustrat, qua sit certissima ferro In vulnus via, nunc vibrat, nunc comprimit hastam, etc. » Ed.

### Notes

- **458** G. 2 and L. have *Postquam uterque diu steterat*.
- **459** A. has *Qua dederat infesto*.
- **461** The vulgate editions display *Tellure levabant*. But that we should read *levarent* [might lift] is required both by grammatical construction and confirmed by the authority of Burmann's codex, which Dorp rightly adduces. Of my manuscripts, the Helmstedt MS has *tellure moverent*; G. 2 has *movebant*. For the rest, our author adapted almost this entire passage [ῥῆσιν] to his own use from Virgil, *Aen.* XII, 896 ff.: « Nec plura effatus, saxum circumspicit ingens, Saxum antiquum, ingens, campo quod forte jacebat: Vix illud lecti bis sex cervice subirent ». Homer speaks much more modestly of the size of the stone that Diomedes was throwing, V, 303, saying that it was a stone that two men of the present age could not carry.
- **463** H. has *Prostratus humi*; the rest have *humo*.
- **464** L. and A. have *Dilapsa*.
- **465** H. has *Caligine texit*; G. 2 has *condit* and *texit*.
- **466** L. has *Non tulit Eonides*; A. has *Enoides*. Barth, *Adv.* p. 2770 end, judges that the following verses are to be praised as most elegant.
- **467** *Flagrantibus armis* [With blazing arms]. See the note to verse 394.
- **468** G. 2 has *Ferro petit*; A. has *Ferro secat*. Without doubt we must read *petat*.
- **469** I have written *Vulnerat hasta* [wounds with a spear] instead of the vulgate *ense* [with a sword], because Homer relates it thus, *Il.* V, 337, as do two of my codices, although G. 2 at the same time sets down *ense* as a variant. Diomedes confesses the same thing about himself in Virgil, *Aeneid* XI, v. 276: « quum ferro caelestia corpora demens Adpetii, et Veneris violavi vulnere dextram ». And in Ovid, *Met.* XV, 769, Venus says of herself: « Quam modo Tydidae Calydonia vulneret hasta ».
- **471** The manuscript and printed copies offer *Sidereo queritur sua vulnera Marti* [complains of her wounds to starry Mars], but at the suggestion of the celebrated Dorp, p. 122, I have gladly restored *sidereae matri* [to her starry mother]. Homer also commands the same; he indeed relates that Venus, having been wounded, from her bro-

### Notes

- **(cont.)** (cont.) [brother] Mars for a chariot and horses with which to be carried to heaven, but there complained to her mother Dione, *Iliad* V, 370: Ἡ δ᾽ ἐν γούνασι πῖπτε Διώνης δῖ᾽ Ἀφροδίτη Μητρὸς ἑῆς. That the names of *mater* and *Mars* are quite often confused by scribes is exemplified by Virgil, *Aen.* IX, 584, where the common editions display *Martis luco*, but Macrobius, V, 18, reads *matris*, by which name I indeed think Thalia, mother of the Palici, who was worshipped there, is to be understood. The scribes were perhaps led astray by the fact that in other poets mention was made of Mars addressed by Venus, but not likewise of her mother Dione, as in Ovid, *Rem. Am.* 5: « Non ego Tydides, a quo tua saucia mater In liquidum rediit aethera Martis equis ».
- **473** I restored *Reducit* from the Helmstedt MS, whereas the vulgate editions have *reduxit*.
- **475** *Horrendumque sonat* G. 2.
- **476** *Hinc alius* G. 2.
- **477** *Conteritur* is in the printed editions; I preferred *Proteritur*, which H. and G. 2 have. Similarly Virgil, *Aen.* XII, 329: « Semineces volvit multos, aut agmina curru Proterit ».
- **479** *Quadrupedis tergo* [from the back of his four-footed beast]. If the author understands a rider falling from his horse on account of a wound, he wrote contrary to the sense of Homer and contrary to the custom of the writers on the Trojan War, who know no horsemen in battle, but present heroes fighting from chariots. A similar error also occurs below in verse 496, where Agamemnon *sublimis equo volat agmina circum* [high on horseback flies around the ranks]. To be sure, a passage of Homer, *Il.* X, 513, can be cited in favor of horse-riding; but because he says ἵππων ἐπεβήσατο, horses yoked to a chariot can comfortably be understood. Wernsdorf in the Addenda.
- **481** *Hic jacet exsanguis* G. 2. Similarly Virg. *Aen.* IX, 753: « Collapsos artus atque arma cruenta cerebro Sternit humi moriens ».
- **485** *Terga metit gladio* [mows backs with the sword]. Dussen in *Prodr.* p. 27 prefers *petit gladio* [strikes backs with the sword]. And indeed this seems more fitting, seeing that our author also below, v. 594, wro-

### Notes

- **(cont.)** (cont.) -te: *Et modo terga petunt*. Yet I do not wish to decide, since our author does not everywhere maintain a proper regard for his own words, and perhaps there hovered before his mind that half-verse of Virgil, *Aen.* X, 513: « Proxima quaeque metit gladio ».
- **486** *Spes una Phrygum* [the sole hope of the Phrygians], a frequent eulogy of Hector. Pentadius in the Tomb of Hector: *Occubuere simul spesque salusque Phrygum*; and below, our author, v. 944: « Unus, tota salus in quo Trojana manebat, Hector adest ». — And especially Virgil, *Aen.* II, 281: « O lux Dardaniae! spes o fidissima Teucrum! » Ed. — Moreover, Barth, *Adv.* p. 2771, declares that these and the following lines were admirably written.
- **488** *Pecudes quum cernit* vulgate edd.; *pecudes dum vidit* H.; *quum vidit* G. 2, and this seemed best to me. This description of a wolf rushing upon flocks is altogether neat and vivid (ἐνεργὴς), especially on account of the concluding words. I would have preferred, however, that he had put a lion instead of a wolf. For the latter's force is not so fearless and heedless of guards as is described here, as is that of a lion, and Virgil skillfully describes the wolf as treacherous rather than brave, *Aen.* XI, 810 ff. In Barth's judgment, p. 2771, the author took considerable pleasure in this comparison, so that a little later he repeated a similar one of a lion. In this he might seem to have acted wrongly and indulged in too much luxuriance, were it not that he did this in imitation of Homer himself, who in two passages not far apart compares Diomedes to a lion rushing upon cattle, *Iliad* V, 136 and 161. That comparison is frequent in Virgil, as in Homer himself; see *Aen.* IX, 339 ff. and X, 723 f. Cf. the tigress in Ovid, *Metam.* V, 164 f.
- **490** *Premit esuries* was corrected by an ancient hand in a copy in the Leipzig library. But all the books have *esuriens*, and it coheres better with the sense. — Moreover, Valerius Flaccus speaks similarly to this, VI, 615: « nec caede moratur in una Turbidus, inque omnes pariter furit ». Ed.
- **492** *Ense cruento* H., G. 2, and A., which I preferred to the vulgate *ense corusco*.
- **494** *Adtolluntque animos* [and lift their spirits] is from Virgil, like the preceding *Deficiunt* [they fail], *Aen.* XII, 2, 4. — *Geminat victoria vires* [victory doubles their strength], a weighty expression, which he repeats in verse 768.
- **495** *Ut vidit socios*. This line is absent from Codex Guelferbytanus 2.

- **505** Here too I thought *proturbat* should be written instead of the vulgate *perturbat*, as I remarked on verse 448. G. 2 has *turbabat cuspide*.
- **507** *Et spes exacuit* [and hope sharpens]. Virg. *Aen.* X, 263: « spes addita suscitat iras: Tela manu jaciunt ».
- **509** Here G. 2 reads *Hic tandem Aeneam misso contendere*; but I deservedly retained the vulgate reading *immisso*, which MS A also preserves, though it puts *tendere* for *contendere*. Virg. *Aeneid* XI, 889: « immissis pars caeca et concita frenis Arietat in portas ». Ovid, *Met.* I, 280: « Fluminibus vestris totas immittite habenas ».
- **510** *Stricto concurrere ferro Hinc parat* G. 2. *Occurrere ferro Tenuit et jaculum* A.
- **511** *Quantum furor* [as far as fury...]. I would prefer to read here *quantas furor ipse movebat*, scil. *vires* [as great a force as fury itself stirred up]. Thus Virgil, *Aen.* V, 454: « vim suscitat ira, Tum pudor incendit vires et conscia virtus ». — And Grattius in *Halieut.* 56: « et viribus addidit iram ». See vol. I, part 1, page 223 of this work. Ed. — For *movebat*, G. 2 reads *manebat*.
- **512** *Depulit error* [a miss deflected it]. Ovid, *Metam.* XII, 83: « quamquam certa nullus fuit error in hasta ».
- **513** *Stomachoque infigitur* [and is fixed in the stomach]. Virgil, *Aen.* IX, 698: « volat Itala cornus Aera per tenerum, stomachoque infixa sub altum Pectus abit ».

### Notes

- **514** *Media inter lora* [amidst the reins]. Virg. *Aen.* XII, 468: « Aurigam Turni media inter lora Metiscum Excutit ».
- **515** H. presents *Claro cum sanguine*; but this is inept, and *calido* is confirmed by Virg. *Aen.* IX, 414: « vomens calidum de pectore flumen »; and v. 422: « calido mihi sanguine poenas Persolves ».
- **517** The vulgate editions read *et valido Cretheona*; G. 2 *valido Credonia*. From Hom. *Il.* V, 542, one should read *Crethona*, but since the metrical scansion rejected this, I restored the Latin case form *Crethonem*.
- **518** Homer *loc. cit.* requires *Orsilochum*; the vulgate editions have *Tersilochum*. At the end of the verse the printed copies read *post funera victor*, which is deservedly suspected. Barth, *loc. cit.* p. 2771, thinks that under that word lies hidden the name of the leader of the Paphlagonians, which however—namely *Pylaemenis*—the meter does not admit; nor can any other name be brought here from Homer, *Il.* V, 576. The celebrated Bondam, *loc. cit.* p. 162, conjectures that it should be changed to *victus*, which I follow, because nothing better occurs to me. The ridiculous spellings with which the manuscripts and printed books corrupt the word *Paphlagonum* here I do not repeat.
- **520** *Antilochique Mydon*: Bondam demonstrated from Homer that this is the reading. The printed editions have *Antilochumque Mison*; A. *Amphilogumque*; H. and G. 2 *Milon*.
- **521** *Sarpedon sequitur*. I embraced the reading of the Helmstedt MS as the clearest; the readings of the remaining books clearly appear corrupted by interpolation. The Torinus edition: *Sarpedon bello funesto praelia miscet*. Codex G. 2: *Sarpedon in bella subit funestaque miscet*. A. and Leipzig: *Sarpedon bella funestaque praelia miscet*.
- **523** That we must read *Tlepolemus* from Hom. *Il.* V, 628 is evident, as Bondam also saw. Otherwise the written and printed books have *Triptolemus*.
- **525** *Tenuemque daret de corpore vitam* [and yielded the faint breath of life from his body], that is, gave up the ghost. He calls it *tenuem vitam* according to the mind of those whom Virgil praises in *Georg.* IV, 223, who said that each soul of living beings is a particle of the world soul, and returns thither through death. « Hence, he says *loc. cit.*, each one for itself at birth draws a sli-

### Notes

- **(cont.)** (cont.) -ght breath of life [« Quemque sibi tenues nascentem arcessere vitas »] ». And *Aen.* IV, 705: « omnis et una Dilapsus calor, atque in ventos vita recessit ».
- **527** Ulysses is called *fraudis commentor* [contriver of deceit], as if this were his craft, says Barth, *Adv.* LIX, 15, of the kind constantly attributed to him by Homer and other ancients. Virgil, *Aen.* II, 164: « scelerumque inventor Ulysses »; and our author repeats the same below, v. 579. Similarly Ovid regarding him, *Met.* XIII, 31: « quid sanguine cretus Sisyphio, furtisque et fraude simillimus illi », whose fabrications (*commenta*) he also notes presently in v. 38. Hence also, as Dussen notes on verse 65 of our author, Ulysses is called φὼρ [thief] in the second Altar of Dosiadas, as Salmasius shows at greater length in his notes, p. 156.
- **528** *Et septem juvenum* [And of seven youths], the Lycians whom Homer enumerates, *Il.* V, 677: Coeranus, Alastor, Chromius, Alcander, Halius, Noemon, and Prytanis.
- **529** Guelf. 2 arranges the words differently: *Hinc patriae culmen pugnat*. Dussen, p. 29, prefers to read *columen* [pillar/support], which is indeed exceedingly fitting for Hector and most appropriate to that other eulogy which our author attributed to him above, v. 486, *spes una Phrygum* [the sole hope of the Phrygians]. Similarly Seneca in *Troad.* v. 126, of Hector: « Columen patriae, mora fatorum, Tu praesidium Phrygibus fessis, Tu murus eras ». — « Graium murus Achilles », Ovid, *Met.* XIII, 281. Lucan, VI, 201, of Scaeva: « stat non fragilis pro Caesare murus, Pompeiumque tenet ». Moreover, what Seneca adds *loc. cit.*, « Tecum cecidit, summusque dies Hectoris idem patriaeque fuit », our author seems to have imitated below, v. 1061. See what was noted by us on Pentadius' Tomb of Hector, vol. II, p. 329 of this work. Ed. — Nevertheless, I would not wish to alter the word in the text itself, since *culmen* [summit/peak] is by no means unsuitable for Hector, indicating at least his supreme dignity. In precisely this way Cornelius Severus calls Cicero « Egregium semper patriae caput », above on page 211 of this volume.
- **530** *Illic Thitides* G. 2.
- **533** G. 2 reads *Clypeo quem sacra virago*. *Virago* is frequent and almost proper regarding Pallas. She is called *flava virago* by Ovid, *Met.* VI, 130; *sancta virago*, in the judgment of Barth, *loc. cit.* p. 2806, suggests both chaste and Martial, that is, warlike.
- **534** All the books here indeed read *Egit, et extrema*. Dussen, however, p. 29, thinks we should read *Fregit* [he broke], understanding it of the shield that preceded, and that this is confirmed by the passage of Ovid which our author imitated, *Met.* V, 172: « Non circumspectis exactum viribus ensem Fregit ». But to me

|---|---|---|---|
| 535 | 535 | Attonitumque simul caelum petere ipsa coegit. | 535 |
| 536 | 536 | Hic ille aethereo queritur sua vulnera regi |  |

| 538 | 538 | VI. Interea magnis Acamantem viribus Ajax |  |
| 539 | 539 | Interimit, vastumque capit Menelaus Adrastum, |  |
| 540 | 540 | Et rapit ad classes manibus post terga revinctis, | 540 |
| 541 | 541 | Ut vivo ducat laetos ex hoste triumphos. |  |
| 542 | 542 | Incumbunt Danai, cedit Trojana juventus, |  |

### Notes

- **(cont.)** (cont.) there is no doubt that we should read: *Aegide et extrema percussum cuspide caedit*. For Pallas must be considered to have opposed her aegis to the shield of Mars, which the author named before, and in subduing Mars it was necessary to use the aegis, in which the greatest power of Pallas was believed to reside. Whence also Jupiter is said to have put the Achaeans to flight with the aegis in Homer, *Iliad* XVII, 593. How easily the words *Aegide et* could have been corrupted into *Egit et* by scribes is known to those who understand the manner of writing customary in the Middle Ages. And although Homer, who narrates these events, *Iliad* V, 841, makes no mention of the aegis, our author, who rarely adheres to Homer and often follows the Latin poets, perhaps had in view in this passage Horace, who says that Pallas used the aegis in this very contest, *Carm.* I, 15, 11: « Jam galeam Pallas et aegida Currusque et rabiem parat ». — And Ovid, *Met.* V, 47: « Bellica Pallas adest, et protegit aegide fratrem ». Ed.
- **536** *Illic aethereo* G. 2. He repeats the verse that he used above on a similar matter, v. 471.
- **537** *Jurg. suffert* [endures the rebukes]. See v. 61 and 104.
- **538** Bondam and Dussen *locc. citt.* read *Acamantem* from Homer, VI, 8. The vulgate editions have *Atamantem*.
- **539** The vulgate editions read here *magnumque petit Menelaus Adrastum*. Dussen in *Prodr.* page 31 bids us rewrite *miserumque petit*, on account of the account of Homer, *Iliad* VI, 37 ff., who relates that Adrastus was thrown from his chariot when his horses were panic-stricken, and having been captured alive by Menelaus standing close by, suppliantly begged him to spare his life; and having at first obtained this, was soon severely wounded by Agamemnon, who rushed up and rebuked Menelaus. Therefore, for Adrastus undergoing such a fate, the epithet *magnum* [great] is less fitting, and *miserum* [wretched] is more true. But this emendation is rendered superfluous by the sound reading of codices H. and G. 2, which we have printed: *vastumque capit Menelaus Adrastum* [and Menelaus captures the huge Adrastus]. From this you may understand that the author did not have in view here the wretched entreaties of Adrastus, but merely wished to express his fall and capture. — I would prefer *lapsumque* [fallen] from Homer. Ed.
- **540** I chose to put *Et rapit* from H. and G. 2 instead of the vulgate *trahit*.
- **541** We have printed the genuine reading of codices H. and G. 2. The editions wrongly show *Ex hinc deducit laetos*. Furthermore, the author here truncated Homer's narrative through excessive brevity. For he relates only that Adrastus was captured and ordered to be taken to the ships; but he passed over in silence what was most important, that Adrastus was killed by Agamemnon, who reproached Menelaus' clemency.

- **543** *Tergaque nuda tegit* [And covers their bare backs], with shields, I suppose, lest they be slain with impunity. Virg. *Aen.* XI, 630: « Bis rejecti armis respectant terga tegentes » [Twice driven back, they look behind them, covering their backs with weapons].
- **546** *Hecubamque vocare* G. 2.
- **548** I do not know whether *Armatas* [armed] is a sufficiently fitting word, yet nothing else is to be sought from the reading of the manuscripts. — *Innuptae Minervae* [of unwed Minerva], Virgil, *Aen.* II, 31.
- **549** *Altaria sertis* [altars with garlands]. Namely, to appease the divine powers, says Barth in the cited passage. The ancients employed not only sacrificial victims, but also garlands for encircling altars, and, I might add, shrines as well. Virg. *Aen.* II, 249: « Nos delubra Deum ... festa velamus fronde per urbem »; and IV, 202: « variis florentia limina sertis »; also *Georg.* IV, 276: « Saepe Deum nexis ornatae torquibus arae ».
- **550** The vulgate editions have *Ex more bidentes*; H. has *de more*, which is more correct, and is Virgil's phrase, *Aen.* IV, 57; V, 96. — G. 2 has *Sacras ad templa bidentes*, which without doubt migrated here by mistake from the following verse.
- **553** *Decernere ferro* G. 2.
- **555** *Et unde ferat* [and whence he bears], namely arms: thus I gave from H. and G. 2. The vulgate editions have *Quis sit et unde foret*.
- **557** *Te, scelerate* [You, villain!]: this is indeed harsher than accords with the courteous speech of Diomedes in Homer, *Iliad* VI, 123 ff.; nor does the reading of MS H., *te celerare furentem*, help at all. — But Barth, *Adv.* XXV, 15, observes that *sceleratus* is often used for troublesome/grievous and impious; as indeed Virgil said « sceleratum frigus » [*Georg.* II, 256], and Rutilius: « Obruerint citius scelerata oblivia solem » [*De reditu suo* I, 137]. ### Notes

- **(cont.)** (cont.) -*tram* all [authorities] give. Barth, *Advers.* p. 2807, teaches that the word *dextram* [right hand] is to be repeated here in this manner: *Vides dextram, quae Veneris dextram vulneravit* [You see the right hand, which wounded Venus's right hand]; and that this is a figure common among poets, by which, when a word in the same construction can apply to two things, they put it only once, yet in such a way that it must be applied in both places. Thus he thinks our poet in verse 964 omitted the particle *quam*, when he should have doubled it: *Quam post elapsam*, that is, *Quam postquam elapsam vitavit*. But it is better in this passage to read *qui vulnere*, so that it may more simply refer to the preceding *hospitis*.
- **560** For *Perculit*, G. 2 incorrectly writes *Protulit*. Furthermore, H. has *pupugit certamine*; perhaps *retudit* would be more fitting. — But this word, like others similarly compounded, *repulit*, *recidit*, is usually employed by good poets with the first syllable long. And J. Schrader, in *Praefat. ad lib. Emend.* p. 42, emends more plausibly: « summo pepulit certamine Martem ». Ed.
- **562** *Post hoc* G. 2 and ed. L.
- **565** *Ad pectora natum tenet* [holds her son to her breast]. Virg. *Aen.* VII, 318: « Et trepidae matres pressere ad pectora natos ».
- **569** *Cristamque micantem* [And the glittering/quivering crest]. Thus the editions and MS H. give, from which G. 2, which has *minantem*, differs little. The celebrated Dorp, *Observ.* p. 123, thinks it much more fitting to read *comantem* [plumed], as *cristae comantes* often occurs in Virgil. He says that correction is confirmed by Burman's codex and by this passage of Statius, *Theb.* IX, 107: « Lycus excelso terrore comantem Perstrinxit galeam ». I, however, believe that *micantem* should not be disturbed, nor that it was used by the author without good reason. For to poets *micare* is often the same as to be vibrated and to tremble. The crest of the helmet terrified the infant most of all because it fluttered and nodded vigorously before his face. We have seen *manus jactare micantes* [to toss fluttering hands] in Calpurnius, *Ecl.* II, 26. Ovid, *Her.* V, 37: « Attoniti micuere sinus »; the same author, *Met.* IX, 37: « Et modo cervicem, modo crura micantia captat » [And now he aims at the neck, now at the shifting legs].
- **570** *Juvenis* [warrior/young man] is said of any warrior; here of Hector. — *Posito aere*, having laid aside his bronze helmet.

| 580 | 580 | Et ferus Idomeneus, et notus gente paterna | 580 |
| 581 | 581 | Meriones, Graiumque simul dux acer Atrides, |  |
| 582 | 582 | Ajacesque duo clari , speciosus in armis |  |
| 583 | 583 | Eurypylus, magnoque Thoas Andraemone natus, |  |
| 584 | 584 | Quique manum Veneris violavit vulnere tristi, |  |
| 585 | 585 | Procedunt : aberat nam Troum terror Achilles, | 585 |
| 586 | 586 | Et dulci cithara dirum lenibat amorem. |  |

### Notes

- **573** *Tua munera natus* G. 2.
- **574** In place of *imitetur* [let him imitate], Anton de Rooy, *Spicil. crit.* p. 97, conjectures *miretur* [let him admire], from an imitation of Virgil, *Aen.* VIII, 517: « tua cernere facta Adsuescat, primis et te miretur ab annis » [Let him become accustomed to seeing your deeds, and admire you from his earliest years]. I, however, consider the word *imitetur* more fitting to the character of Hector the father speaking of his son than if he had said *miretur*. That word is too boastful when a father is speaking of himself. But that a son should imitate his father is an admonition worthy of a father. Moreover, the word *imitetur* is necessary because he adds *patrias virtutes* [his father's virtues]. It is fitting to imitate these, whereas one admires rather the person on account of virtues. The writer might rather have had in mind that Virgilian saying by which Aeneas exhorts his son, *Aen.* XII, 435: « Disce, puer, virtutem ex me, verumque laborem, Fortunam ex aliis » [Learn, boy, virtue and true toil from me, fortune from others]. — Besides, Schrader, *Emendat.* ch. 7, p. 141, observes that Virgil and others frequently use *mirari* for *imitari*, and so scribes often change *mirari* into *imitari*, as in Propertius, II, 23, 117: « Quod si tu Graias, sive es imitata Latinas », where he states that four manuscripts have *tuque es mirata Latinas*. Ed.
- **576** I gave *Ad certamina* from H.; the editions have *in certamina*.
- **579** *Fraudis commentor* [contriver of deceit]. He repeated this from verse 527 in the Homeric manner.
- **582** *Claris speciosus* G. 2 and A., with the comma deleted.
- **583** *Magnusque Thoas* G. 2; *Andromone* A. L.
- **586** The manuscripts and printed editions read *Et dulci cithara Divum lenibat amores* [And with sweet lyre soothed the loves of the gods], which everyone realizes cannot stand, and will readily agree with Dussen, who on page 32 emends *dirum lenibat amorem* [soothed his dreadful love], or *diros amores*. For our author himself above, v. 25, said *ferum amorem*, and below, v. 641, uses almost the same diction: *Praedaque, quae duros Menelai mulceat ignes*. Here indeed he seems to have imitated Virgil, who in *Georg.* IV, 464, says of Orpheus: « Ipse cava solans aegrum testudine amorem » [He himself consoling his lovesick heart with the hollow tortoise shell].

| Verse | Wernsdorf | Text | Printed |
|---|---|---|---|
| — | 587 | [Sortes miserunt , quis eonim in bella valeret] |  |
| 587 | 588 | Ergo ubi dejectis auratam regis Atridae |  |
| 588 | 589 | Sortibus in galeam , magnus processerat Ajax , |  |
| — | 590 | Concurrunt armis Ajax crudelis et Hector. | 590 |
| 589 | 591 | Principio jactis committunt praelia telis, |  |
| 590 | 592 | Mox rigidos stringunt enses , et fortibus armis |  |
| 591 | 593 | Decernunt, partesque oculis rimantur apertas; |  |
| 592 | 594 | Et modo terga petunt, duros modo fortibus ictus |  |
| 593 | 595 | Depellunt clypeis : ingens ad sidera clamor | 595 |
| 594 | 596 | Tollitur, et vastis impletur vocibus aether. |  |
| 597 | 597 | [Alterni vibrant gladios, et vulnera miscent] |  |

### Notes

- **587 (LL —)** The vulgate editions write *In bella valeret*, in place of which I would prefer *in bella veniret*. In the Helmstedt MS this verse is missing, and in G. 2 it is read altered after v. 588: *Sortes miserunt, quis bella valeret inire*. For that reason, and because as far as the sense is concerned it is entirely redundant, it seems more correct that the whole verse should be excluded.
- **588 (LL 587)** *Ergo ubi dejectis* [Therefore when having cast...]. He took this from Virgil, *Aen.* V, 490: « Convenere viri, dejectamque aerea sortem Accepit galea » [The men assembled, and the bronze helmet received the cast lot].
- **590 (LL —)** *Concurrunt* [They clash]. This verse also is absent from H., nor does it seem clearly necessary here.
- **593 (LL 591)** I restored *Decernunt* [They decide/fight] from H. and G. 2; the vulgate editions have *Decertant*. In the remainder of the verse the editions read *partesque oculis rimantur apertis* [and probe parts with open eyes]. Barth, *Adv.* p. 2807, scents an error and bids us write *partesque oculis rimantur opertas* [and probe covered parts with their eyes], adding: to write of probing open parts is not the mark of sound judgment, since things that are exposed have no need of probing. But Dussen, page 33, more felicitously emends *rimantur apertas*, that is, with sword or spear they seek those parts that are open to their eyes. Our author himself makes clear the meaning of this line below, v. 605: « quaque patebat Nuda viri cervix, fulgentem dirigit ensem ». With similar meaning Virgil, *Aeneid* XII, 920: « telum Aeneas fatale coruscat, Sortitus fortunam oculis ». — Cicero refers to this custom of fighters in *Verr.* V (VII), 71: « Si ullum locum aperuerimus suspicioni aut crimini, accipiendum est statim vulnus ». Ed.
- **596 (LL 594)** *Impletur vocibus aether* [The upper air is filled with cries]. The passage of the following verses up to 604 is wonderfully disturbed, altered, and interpolated in the manuscripts, so that almost every single one presents it differently. It was necessary for me to correct and establish it as seemed best. A more complete remedy must be expected from a more intact manuscript. And Wolfenbüttel 1 (G. 1), from which it could most certainly be sought, is deficient here.
- **597** *Alterni vibrant gladios* [By turns they brandish swords]; where A., L., and T. read *Alternos fibrant gladios*. I restored this verse in the place where the Helmstedt MS shows it, which nevertheless reads *librant gladios*. The printed editions, as also Guelferbytanus 2, place it after the words *modo dentibus uncis* in v. 599. Hence Barth, *Adv.* p. 2807, thinks the author spoke of the "swords of teeth" in reference to boars, as Apuleius spoke of "spears of teeth." But that this verse is a gloss

## p. 567

PDF page 577.

| Verse | Wernsdorf | Text | Printed |
|---|---|---|---|
| 595 | 598 | Non sic setigeri exacuunt fervoribus iras, |  |
| 596 | 599 | Pectoribusque premunt vastis, modo dentibus uncis |  |
| 598 | 600 | Fortia terga petunt, spumantque per ora vicissim : | 600 |
| 599 | 601 | Fumiferse nubes, concretaque fulgura, et ignes |  |
| 600 | 602 | Jactantur , magnoque implentur murmure silvae : |  |

### Notes

- **(cont.)** (cont.) is apparent from the transposition itself. In the place where the Helmstedt codex puts it, it seems indeed more fitting, where the discourse is still about Hector and Ajax having clashed, and *gladios* [swords] can be taken in the proper sense; but nevertheless it is both superfluous in itself and ill-inserted, because it mentions wounds inflicted, which the author intended to treat only after the appended comparison. Therefore, as suspect, I set it apart and enclosed it in brackets.
- **598 (LL 595)** *Exacuunt fervoribus iras* [They whet their rage with heat]. Thus I wrote from the vulgate editions. MS Helmst. alters this verse and expands it by another: « Non sic setigeri rapidis fervoribus iras Exacuunt, silvas complentes murmure vasto ». But I reject the second, because the same thing is said shortly thereafter, almost without even changing the words, in verse 602.
- **599 (LL 596)** Several manuscripts indeed have *Pectoribusque petunt*, but I thought *premunt* should be brought here from the following verse, because it is read there in the vulgate editions, and yet is less fitting there than here with *pectoribus*.
- **600 (LL 598)** The printed editions have *Fortia terga premunt*; where he said *fortia terga* [strong backs], just as a little before he said *fortes clypeos* [strong shields]. The Helmstedt MS, however, has *duraque terga petunt*; where *dura* [hard] can also be defended by poetic usage, unless perhaps the redundant conjunction *-que* indicates that it is less genuine. For why the boar's back is called *dura*, Virgil explains, *Georg.* III, 256: « fricat arbore costas, Atque hinc atque illinc humeros ad vulnera durat ». Hence by Olympius [Nemesianus], *Laud. Hercul.* 110, the boar is called « duratus armos scopulis » [having hardened its shoulders against crags].
- **601 (LL 599)** The printed editions have *Fumigerae*; I gave *fumiferae* from manuscript H., because this word is customary among other authors, whereas that one is unknown. Virgil calls them « Fumiferos ignes » in *Aen.* IX, 522; Ovid has « fumificos mugitus » in *Metam.* VII, 114. Above also in our poet *armigera Pallas* was said instead of *armifera*. In the rest of the verse there is much variation among the scribes. In G. 2 there is written above *et crebri fulminis ignes*, below *ignes et fulgura crebra*. In Helmst., similar things are written over erased letters, which are read with difficulty. On that account I did not alter the vulgate reading. But Barth in the cited passage notes that this was crudely expressed by the author, when he says lightning and fires are breathed out by boars. Fires are indeed accustomed to be attributed by poets to horses and bulls (see Nemesianus, *Cyneg.* 255, and Petronius's poem on the witch, v. 11), and even lightning to the boar, yet they do this more moderately than our author. Ovid, *Met.* VIII, 289, of the Calydonian boar: « Fulmen ab ore venit, frondes adflatibus ardent » [Lightning comes from its mouth, foliage burns with its blasts]; and X, 550: « Fulmen habent acres in aduncis dentibus apri » [Fierce boars have lightning in their curved tusks].
- **602 (LL 600)** *Jactantur* [Are tossed]. Here too the Helmstedt MS is interpolated or corrupt, which writes *Implent* (and above *Jactantur*) *implentur vicinae murmure silvae*.

| 611 | 613 | Sed magno saxi jactu petit : at ferus Ajax |  |

| 613 | 615 | Et juvenem saxo percussum sternit codem. | 615 |

| 615 | 617 | Integratque animum : jam rursus ad arma coibant, |  |

### Notes

- **603 (LL 601)** *Talis Priamides* [Such was the son of Priam]. This verse also must be expunged, having been added by a schoolmaster who thought that the preceding simile required this application. But the comparison instituted in v. 598 is formed in such a way that it refers back to the preceding matters, and demands no apodosis. An indication of a spurious verse is also its defective and abrupt sense, and the fact that it is presented differently and tainted with fault in the Helmstedt codex: *Talis Priamides fortisque Aeacides armis*. And you might indeed subjoin here verse 597, which was redundant above; but nevertheless both can be omitted without detriment to the discourse.
- **604 (LL 602)** *Animis telisque* H.; *animis teloque* G. 2.
- **605 (LL 603)** *Quaque petiti* H., badly.
- **608 (LL 606)** Here I preferred to follow the reading of codex H. rather than that of the editions and A., which have *submittit, et umbone repellit*. After this verse another absurd line is thrust into G. 2: *Cedebat juveni paullum Mavortius heros*, which surely displays nothing other than an incongruous interpretation of the preceding words *tergaque submisit*.
- **609 (LL 607)** H. writes *Perlabitur oras*, which I think is more correct than what the printed editions have, *dilabitur*.
- **611 (LL 609)** H. has *Acrius in pugnam*; G. 2 has *Actior in pugnam*; A. has *Acrius in pugna*. Hence perhaps one should write: « Acrius impugnans, rursus consurgit in hostem » [More fiercely attacking, he rises again against the enemy].
- **614 (LL 612)** MS H. has *Ingentis clypei* incorrectly; G. 2 has *depulit ictum*; the vulgates have *reppulit*. *Clypeo septemplice* [with sevenfold shield] is according to Ovid, who says in *Met.* XIII, 2: « clypei dominus septemplicis Ajax »; and in v. 346 of the same book: « frustra Telamone creatus Gestasset laeva taurorum tergora septem ». Virgil, *Aen.* XII, 925, of Turnus: « orasque recludit Loricae, et clypei extremos septemplicis orbes » [and lays open the edges of the corselet, and the outermost circles of the sevenfold shield].
- **616 (LL 614)** *Quem levat exceptum* G. 2 and Annab. The vulgate editions have *exemptum*.
- **617 (LL 615)** *Integrat ac animum* G. 2.

### Notes

- **(cont.)** (cont.) *Integrare* [to renew], as Barth notes, *Adv.* p. 2807, admirably means to restore to its whole state (*in integrum*), to renew; just as he said above, v. 101: « Et Troum renovare velis in praelia vires ». Statius, *Theb.* VIII, 657: « bellum integrabat Enyo ». Seneca, *Medea*, v. 672: « semet dolor Accendit ipse, vimque praeteritam integrat ».
- **618 (LL 616)** *In undis* G. 2.
- **622 (LL 620)** *Tum bello* G. 2; *Tunc* A. and T.
- **623 (LL 621)** *Quae te terra viris* A., erroneously. Virg. *Aen.* I, 606: « qui tanti talem genuere parentes ».
- **625 (LL 623)** *Contra haec dicta*, thus H. Then *sic contra referre* G. 2. *At contra referre parat* A. and the editions.
- **627 (LL 625)** *Nobilis haec domus et fama* G. 2.
- **628 (LL 626)** The editions of Torinus and Spondanus read *Hector et Hesionae ... recordans*. Dussen, page 34, advises reading *Hector at Hesionae*. But the Helmstedt MS has *Hector ut Esionae nomen casusque recordat*. So also Guelferbytanus 1, which in this verse continues, along with A. and the old Leipzig edition: *Hector ut Esione ... recordans*. On their authority I prefer to embrace *ut* and *recordat*. For the former well supports the connection of the sentence, and *recordat* is worthy of this poetaster, whom we know from many examples to use archaic verb forms now and then. — See what was noted above at verse 456, and on the fragment of Nemesianus *De Aucupio*. Ed. — Moreover, Hesione, who is mentioned here, was the daughter of Laomedon, king of Troy, whom Hercules freed when she was exposed to a sea-monster; afterwards, when Troy was taken, he gave her as wife to Telamon, who had been the first to scale the wall. Ovid, *Metam.* XI, 216 ff.
- **629 (LL 627)** *Sanguis communis utrique* G. 1 and 2. The vulgate editions add *est*.
- **630 (LL 628)** *Et prior Ajacem* H., G. 1, and L.
- **632 (LL 630)** *Accipit insignem*. This is the reading of MSS H. and G. 2, certainly better than that of the rest: « Accepit balteum vario caelamine sculptum »

### Notes

- **(cont.)** (cont.) Guelferbytanus 1 displays *Concipit insignem*. He made *balteum* a disyllable after the example of Virgil, *Aen.* X, 496: « rapiens immania pondera baltei ». Ovid speaks of *caelamina clypei* [carvings of the shield], *Met.* XIII, 291.
- **636 (LL 634)** I restored *Atque avidi* from G. 1, 2, A., and L., whereas Torinus' edition had *avide*.
- **637 (LL 635)** *Postera quum primum* [When the next (morning) first...]. Bondam thinks that Ovid was likewise imitated by the author in this verse, *Met.* XV, 665: « Postera sidereos Aurora fugaverat ignes »; and *Met.* IV, 81: « Postera nocturnos Aurora removerat ignes ». — And the passage of Virgil, *Aeneid* III, 521, could also be cited: « Jamque rubescebat stellis Aurora fugatis ». Ed. — But I rather see here adopted that passage of Virgil, *Aen.* V, 42: « Postera quum primo stellas Oriente fugarat Clara dies, socios in coetum litore ab omni Advocat Aeneas ». The old Leipzig edition gave *fugaret*, less correctly.
- **638 (LL 636)** *Tum maximus* G. 1 and 2; the rest have *tunc*.
- **639 (LL 637)** *Funera caedis* [the dead of slaughter] is the reading of my three manuscripts, to which A. and L. allude in reading *funera necis*. I therefore restored this with the best right, since the vulgate reading *funera noctis* is in itself inept and alien to the context. For during the past night no slaughter had taken place.
- **641 (LL 639)** *Volnerat ignes* H.; *moverat* G. 1: neither reading is preferable to the vulgate.
- **642 (LL 640)** The editions falsely display *cum saevo* here: *tum* is necessary, which G. 1 and 2, and the old Leipzig edition have.
- **644 (LL 642)** This verse exists in a very corrupt form in the editions of Torinus and Spondanus: *neque ille Aut animum precibus, nec dictis porrigit aures*. The others vary. The old Leipzig edition: *Aut animum prece, aut dictis porrigit aures*. The Helmstedt MS: *praedae aut dictis commodat aures*. Guelferbytanus 1: *praedae vel dictis commodat aurem*. G. 2: *praedae vel dictis accommodat*. A.: *praedae aut dictis porrigit*. From each of them the true reading could be gathered, which I have given.
- **645 (LL 643)** *Ultro de castris* G. 2.
- **646 (LL 644)** *His monitis* G. 1, A., and L.

### Notes

- **647 (LL 645)** *Contemptum duro se narrat ab hoste* [He reports that he was spurned by the harsh enemy], thus have A. and the printed editions L., T., Sp. But H., G. 1, and 2 better have *duro se reddit ab hoste* [he returns from the harsh enemy]. For Homer does not report any actual speech of Idaeus in which he relates that he was spurned. Nor ought *se reddit* to cause offence on the grounds that he was already present, having turned back again. For such a pleonasm is frequent in good writers. Suetonius, *Jul.* ch. 2: « intra paucos rursus dies repetita Bithynia ». Plautus, in the Prologue to the *Poenulus*, 79: « Revertor rursus denuo Carthaginem ».
- **648 (LL 646)** *Confusi a sede* H., wrongly. Writers of later Latinity say *confundi* for *perturbari animo* [to be troubled in mind]. Thus Juvenal, *Sat.* III, v. 1: « Quamvis digressu veteris confusus amici ». Pliny, *Epist.* V, 5, 1: « qui nuntius gravi me dolore confudit ». The same, *Paneg.* ch. 86: « Quam ego audio confusionem tuam fuisse, quum digredientem prosequereris ». Below, v. 681, our poet says: « Danai turbati caede suorum ».
- **650 (LL 648)** Instead of *tradiderant*, which is in the vulgate editions, Dussen, page 34, thinks that *tradiderunt* should be restored, and rightly so. For it is well known that such forms were often altered by scribes ignorant of the laws of meter and unfamiliar with the usage of the poets. A. erroneously has *Fortia tenderunt*.
- **651 (LL 649)** The vulgate editions have *Tunc removent fossas*, which Barth long ago, p. 2807, judged should be changed to *renovant*, that is, they repair and renew. But what he attained by conjecture, the ancient books freely offer. H., G. 2, and L. read *Tunc renovant*. G. 1 has *Tum renovant vires*. For *robore cingunt*, G. 1 has *robore firmant*. *Robur*, as Barth notes, designates a wooden fence, an oak palisade. And our poet often mentions such an oak rampart, as in vv. 683 and 764.
- **652 (LL 650)** *Jam nitidum* H. Bondam, *loc. cit.*, notes that this verse was fashioned from this line of Ovid, *Met.* IX, 796: « Postera lux radiis totum patefecerat orbem ». So also Virg. *Aen.* IV, 118: « ubi ortus Extulerit Titan radiisque retexerit orbem ».
- **653 (LL 651)** *Superos Jovis* H., A., and L.; *Superos pater* T., but *Jovis* seems sufficiently supported by authority. — *Et monet armis* H.; *et monet orbem* G. 1.
- **654 (LL 652)** *Sua dicta* H., G. 1, and 2. The vulgates have *Sua fata*.
- **655 (LL 653)** *Dilabitur* G. 2, A., and L.; *horas* G. 2.
- **656 (LL 654)** *Consedit montibus* G. 1 and 2, A., L.; *concedit* the editions of Torinus and Spondanus. — For

| 667 | 669 | Hic illi occurrit fatis Agelaus iniquis |  |
| 668 | 670 | Telum immane manu quatiens, quem maximus heros, | 670 |
| 669 | 671 | Occupat , et duro medium transverberat ense. |  |
| 670 | 672 | Hinc Phrygas Ajacis vastis protectus in armis |  |

### Notes

- **(cont.)** (cont.) *simul*, it seems that *super* should be read, just as this was interchanged with *simul* above, v. 65. Ed.
- **657 (LL 655)** *Inde videns aciem Iliacam* H.; *Tum videt acies Iliacas* G. 2.
- **659 (LL 657)** *Fataque dira* H.
- **661 (LL 659)** *Conditus ira* G. 2, erroneously.
- **662 (LL 660)** The editions read *Totis super eminet armis*, ineptly; more correctly, H. and G. 1 have *totis gravis imminet*, and G. 2 has *et totis imminet*.
- **664 (LL 662)** *Ingenti explentur* A. and L.
- **667 (LL 665)** *Ardentibus armis*, as above, v. 394, he said *flagrantia arma*. Ed.
- **668 (LL 666)** *Cum magno turbine* H.
- **669 (LL 667)** *Hinc illi* L. The spurious name *Aegaeus* is read here in all manuscripts and printed editions, as above, vv. 377 and 405. As the celebrated Bondam notes, *loc. cit.* p. 164, Agelaus son of Phradmon should be placed here, whom Homer records as pierced through the back and chest with a spear by Diomedes, *Iliad* VIII, 258.
- **671 (LL 669)** *Ac duro medium* G. 1, and *medium diro* H. Instead of *ense* [sword], *hasta* [spear] should have been said here according to Homer, and perhaps that is found in another codex. For in other passages of our author too, we have seen sword and spear interchanged by the scribes.
- **672 (LL 670)** The printed books read *Hinc Phrygias acies vastis protectus*. In those words no one would easily notice a fault, unless good manuscripts indicated it. But the Helmstedt MS and G. 1 clearly reveal *Hinc Phryges Ajacis*. Homer shows this to be true, recounting that Teucer, protected by Ajax's shield, killed many Trojans with arrows, *Iliad* VIII, v. 266: Τεῦκρος δ᾽ εἴνατος ἦλθε, παλίντονα τόξα τιταίνων. Στῆ δ᾽ ἄρ᾽ ὑπ᾽ Αἴαντος σάκεϊ Τελαμωνιάδαο· and v. 272: ὁ δέ μιν σάκεϊ κρύπτασκε φαεινῷ. I should think that the manuscript's *Phryges* should be changed to *Phrygas*, and instead of *in armis*, perhaps *ab armis* would be better.
