### Notes

- **674 (LL 672)** Instead of *Corisiona*, which the vulgate editions have, *Gorgythiona* should be read from Hom. *Iliad* VIII, 302, as the celebrated Bondam noted, *loc. cit.*; and G. 1 and 2 come close, reading *Coriphiona*. H. has *Cretionaque satum*, and A. has *Carphiona ... fudit*.
- **675 (LL 673)** *Mox alias acies petit* [Presently he seeks other battle lines]. Dussen, p. 36, wished to recast this as *Mox alios ardens petit* [Presently burning he seeks others]. The celebrated De Rooy, *Spic. crit.* p. 98, prefers *Mox alios acie petit*, which I for my part would prefer, if I believed that *acie aliquem petere* could be said of arrows, which are to be understood in this place. I consider the vulgate reading rather tolerable.
- **676 (LL 674)** *Quem*, namely Teucer. Ed.
- **677 (LL 675)** *Excussumque externo proterit* G. 1; *extemplo proterit arcu* G. 2; but the vulgate reading prevails, and by *excusso arcu* [his bow having been struck from him] it is signified that the bow was knocked from Teucer's hands by the blow of the stone hurled by Hector.
- **678 (LL 676)** *Rapiunt a caede* H. Ovid has that phrase, *Her.* VI, 135: « Prodidit illa patrem: rapui de caede Thoanta ».
- **680 (LL 678)** *Invisa cuspide* H.; *inversa* G. 1 and 2. The vulgate's *Infesta* is better.
- **682 (LL 680)** *Convertunt, iterumque* G. 1 and 2; *ad castra* G. 2.
- **683 (LL 681)** *Objecto robore* [with the oak barrier thrown across], that is, with an oak bolt thrown across. The whole verse recurs below at v. 935. Verse 762 may also be compared.
- **684 (LL 682)** *At Phryges obsident* L.; *Inclusos occidunt aggere* H.
- **685 (LL 683)** *Excubituque premunt muros flammisque coronant* H. and G. 1. From these I adopted *coronant* into the text in place of *coruscant*, which is in the vulgates. For Virgil, *Aen.* IX, 160: « Interea vigilum excubiis obsidere portas Cura datur Messapo, et moenia cingere flammis », namely of watch-fires; and ibid. 380: « omnemque aditum custode coronant ».
- **687 (LL 685)** *Curas animosque* [cares and spirits]. It seems that one should read *curis animosque*, or *curas animisque resolvunt*.

### Notes

- **690 (LL 688)** *Mox hoste repulso*. Thus the manuscripts and printed editions read, but it seems it should be corrected to *mox hoste repulsi*. See the first Excursus at the end of this poem.
- **691 (LL 689)** *Dextramque hortantur Achillis* [And they urge the right hand of Achilles]. Barth, p. 2807, observes that *dextram* in this place designates pledge of faith (*fides*); he further proves, by adducing many passages of the ancients, that the right hand was mutually given as a sign of friendship, favor, and clemency; likewise that by offering the right hand mutual promises were made, peace settled, and treaties struck. It would take too long to repeat all these well-known matters here. I cite one or two. Valerius Flaccus, II, 639, on Cyzicus: « Ut videt, ipse ultro primus procurrit ad undas, Miraturque viros, dextraque amplexus et haerens Incipit ». The same author in Book III, 14: « manibusque datis junxere nepotes », that is, they established a perpetual alliance for all posterity (though Gronovius there prefers *penates*). Prosper in the book *De Ingratis*: « An dextram, pacis palmam, dare te pudet hosti ». In this passage of our Homerizer, in my opinion at least, *dextra* denotes not only the faith and friendship of Achilles, but also his bravery and warlike might.
- **692 (LL 690)** *Auxilium Danais* G. 2, undoubtedly from a gloss. *Theteius heros* Torinus' edition; others even more corruptly. Dussen, page 36, saw that *Thetideius*, namely the son of Thetis, should be read, and that is correctly placed in other passages of our author, as in vv. 897, 943, and 962. And in this place G. 1 has *Thitideius*.
- **694 (LL 692)** *Ulla ferus cepit* H. I restored *Nec enim illum redditus* from G. 2, whereas the vulgate editions have *non illum redditus*.
- **695 (LL 693)** *Briseis de corpore movit* L.
- **697 (LL 695)** I gave *Hinc dapibus* from G. 2; others read *Et dapibus curant*. To this verse G. 2 subjoins another: « Per conversa poli quum tertia pars superesset »: that this is an ill-conceived addition and gloss can be seen from verse 699.
- **698 (LL 696)** This verse and the following one exist in a very corrupt state in the books. The vulgate editions read *Alterius noctis tarde labentibus astris*. Guelferbytanus 1: *Ulterius tenebrae tarde*. The Helmstedt MS and G. 2: *Alterius tenebrae*; the same G. 2 and L. have *tardis labentibus*, and A. has *armis* for *astris*. Since here, of a new action, enacted not indeed on another, but on the same night, as is clear from Homer, the nar-

| 706 | 708 | Perspicerel, sensusque ducum plebisque referret. |  |
| 707 | 709 | Quem procul ut vidit socius Diomedis Ulysses, |  |

### Notes

- **(cont.)** (cont.) -ration begins, and in the following verse *pars tertia noctis* [the third part of the night] is added, *alterius noctis* [of another night]—which the vulgate editions seem to connect with the preceding *lenique sopore*—cannot have a place here; rather, the author intended to signify the second part of that night in which the events mentioned above had occurred, and to which he soon subjoins the third. Therefore I think we should read: *Altera transibat*, scil. *pars noctis*, or, if you prefer this, *Altera transierat*. This reading is elicited without difficulty from the traces of the reading of the manuscripts, *Alterius tenebrae*.
- **699 (LL 697)** The printed editions indeed read *pars altera noctis*, but this is a manifest interpolation. All my manuscripts have *pars tertia noctis*, and this is confirmed by Homer's lines from which these were translated, *Iliad* X, 252: Ἄστρα δὲ δὴ προβέβηκε· παρῴχηκεν δὲ πλέων νὺξ Τῶν δύο μοιράων, τριτάτη δ᾽ ἔτι μοῖρα λέλειπται.
- **700 (LL 698)** The printed editions have *Tunc Danaum*, and G. 2 *Tum Danaum*; but I judged that *Quum Danaum* should be restored from the Helmstedt MS and G. 1, since this must be referred to the preceding indication of time, and the lines of Homer from which I said it was taken are spoken by Ulysses when about to embark with Diomedes upon this very journey of which we are treating here. For *Aetolius*, A. has *Idomeneius heros*.
- **702 (LL 700)** *Secum tacite* G. 1, 2, A., and L.; but *tacito* belongs to the following *studio*. Virg. *Aen.* IX, 373: « sublustri noctis in umbra ».
- **704 (LL 702)** *Quid vel agunt, quantasve parent* G. 2, erroneously.
- **705 (LL 703)** The vulgate editions read *loca per montana*, to which I preferred *secreta*, which G. 1 has; H. and G. 2 have *per nocturna paventes*; A. has *per ignota paventes*. G. 1 also has *paventes*, which I think is foreign to the poet's intention.
- **708 (LL 706)** The vulgate editions prefer *Aspiceret* along with the Helmstedt MS. G. 1 has *Perspiceret*; G. 2 *Conspiceret*. — *Sensus*, as Barth notes, p. 2808, are counsels, judgment. So frequently in Claudian, as *de III Cons. Honor.* 187: « non corrumpentia sensus Dona valent ». The same author, *de IV Cons. Hon.* 300: « nec sic inflectere sensus Humanos edicta valent, ut vita regentis ». The same author, *de Laud. Stilich.* III, 10: « magnanimum pectus, quo frena reguntur Imperii, cujus libratur sensibus orbis ».
- **709 (LL 707)** *Procul ubi videt* A., corruptly.

### Notes

- **710 (LL 708)** The vulgate editions have *Abdiderant*, but we must read *Abdiderunt*.
- **711 (LL 709)** I wrote *Post densos frutices* according to the Helmstedt MS and G. 2; the printed editions have *In densos*; G. 1 has *Per densos*: I considered *dum spe*, which G. 1 and H. have, more correct than the vulgate *ut spe*. Furthermore, G. 1 reads *spe perculsus*, H. *spe frustratus*; I regard both as unsuitable. Most fitting is what stands in the vulgate editions and in G. 2, *spe percussus inani* [struck by vain hope]. Similarly Cicero, *Tusc.* V, 11: « quodcumque nostros animos probabilitate percussit, id dicimus ». — But to me it seems that hardly anyone can be said to be struck by hope (*spe percuti*). Ed.
- **712 (LL 710)** The vulgate editions read *Trojanus juvenis cursu*, but all my manuscripts have *Troius Eumedides*, which designates Dolon, son of Eumedes, and is taken both from Hom. *Il.* X, 314, and from Virg. *Aen.* XII, 346. From this it is clear that *Eumedides* must be written; and because *Troius Eumedides* is contrary to the meter, the reading in the manuscripts should be corrected to *Tros Eumedides*, which I have adopted. *Trojanus juvenis* comes from a gloss.
- **714 (LL 712)** *Fidens animo* [confident in spirit], a Virgilian expression, *Aen.* II, 61.
- **718 (LL 716)** *Perstatis in ira* [you persist in anger]. Ovid, *Pont.* I, 4, 44: « Perstiterit laesi si gravis ira Dei ».
- **719 (LL 717)** *Munera laudis* G. 2.
- **720 (LL 718)** *Aut si* H., G. 2, and L.
- **721 (LL 719)** *Currum promittit Achillis* G. 1. Dolon himself relates this to Ulysses in Homer, *Iliad* X, 392. Virgil, *Aen.* XII, 349, says of Dolon: « Qui quondam, castra ut Danaum speculator adiret, Ausus Pelidae pretium sibi poscere currus ». Ovid, in *Ib.* 629: « Qualis equos pacto, quos fortis agebat Achilles, Acta Phrygi timido est, nox tibi talis eat ». An epigram on Dolon in *Anthol. Lat.* I, 95: « Praemia magna Dolon, currum dum poscit Achillis, Prodidit ipse cadens munera magna Dolon ».
- **722 (LL 720)** *Si vestras cepisset opes* [if he had captured your resources]. The celebrated Antony de Rooy, p. 98, considers this corrupt and emends it to *Si vestras rescisset opes* [if he had learned of your resources], because Dolon had been sent to spy out the strength and resources of the Greeks. See above, v. 707.

### Notes

- **725 (LL 723)** *Per Ditis fluctus* [by the waves of Dis], by the Styx, or by the infernal rivers, as Jupiter swears in Ovid, *Met.* I, 187. Virgil says *Lacus Ditis opacos* in *Culex* 371. This is a formula of entreaty, like the oath in Ovid, *Trist.* II, 53: « Per mare, per terras, per tertia numina juro ».
- **726 (LL 724)** *Ne rapere hanc* [do not snatch this away]. Ovid has *eripere animam* [to snatch away life], *Met.* VI, 540.
- **727 (LL 725)** *Dona salute* [grant me life / spare me]. After this verse, the following is most ineptly intruded in G. 2: *Et lassata meis nondum satiata remisit*; which perhaps ought to have been placed after verse 718 and explained it; but, as I suspect, it was fashioned by an inept schoolman from this line of Juvenal, VI, 129: « Et lassata viris necdum satiata recessit ».
- **728 (LL 726)** Most read *Concilium Priami*; the Leipzig edition reads *Consilium*, which seems preferable. H. has *Priami pandam*.
- **729 (LL 727)** *Quid Troja pararet* [what Troy was preparing]. Barth, *Adv.* LVIII, 14, p. 2752, thinks that *Troja* was not very elegantly used for the Trojan chiefs. And yet these words were borrowed from Ovid, *Met.* XIII, 244, where Ulysses relates the same affair: « Ausum eadem, quae nos, Phrygia de gente Dolona Interimo; non ante tamen, quam cuncta coegi Prodere, et edidici, quid perfida Troja pararet. Omnia cognoram ».
- **730 (LL 728)** *Fauces mucrone recluso*. So the Basel edition of Torinus, but A. and L. have *mucrone refulso* for *revulso*. Ant. de Rooy, *loc. cit.* p. 98, doubts whether to retain *recluso* or to change it into *reclusas*, since examples of both phrases can be produced. For my part, I would not hesitate to adopt *reclusas*, or rather *revulsas* from the corrupt *refulso*, since Guelf. 1 expressly prefers *reclusas*, and the author's meaning seems to require it. For I think that the phrase *fauces mucrone reclusas* (or *revulsas*) *detrudunt* signifies that Dolon's head, his throat being cut, was severed from his body, in which manner Homer relates that he was beheaded, *Iliad* X, 455 ff. — Thus Juvenal said *jugulos aperire* [to slit the throat], *Sat.* IV. And Horace, *Epod.* XVII, 71: « Modo ense pectus Norico recludere ». Ed. — Therefore the verb *detrudunt*, i.e. *dejiciunt* [they cast down], can be retained, in place of which, as being elsewhere less common, the celebrated de Rooy wished to substitute *detruncant*, and Dussen, p. 37, *pertrudunt*.
- **731 (LL 729)** For *Detrudunt*, which is in A., L., and T., the Helmstedt MS has *Recludunt*. Whence perhaps one might read: *fauces mucrone retruso* (hidden, plunged in) *Recludunt*. — And the verb *Detrudunt* seems repeated from Virg. *Aen.* IX, 496: « tuo-

### Notes

- **(cont.)** (cont.) -que Invisum hoc detrude caput sub Tartara telo ». Ed. — For *juveni*, which is in H. and T., G. 1, A., and L. have *juvenis*, which I think is more correct.
- **733 (LL 731)** *Per herbas* G. 2.
- **734 (LL 732)** *Exanimant*, for they slaughter with the sword and kill, in the active form, is found rather rarely, I think, among the better writers. — The vulgate editions have *Tunc tristi*; I preferred *tum*, which is in G. 2.
- **735 (LL 733)** *Candore nitentes* [shining with whiteness]. Similarly Virg. *Aen.* XII, 84: « Qui candore nives anteirent, cursibus auras ».
- **736 (LL —)** *Rhesi ventigenas*. This verse is manifestly spurious, because it disturbs both the sequence of words and the construction, and says superfluous things. It is read only in the editions of Torinus and Spondanus; in all my manuscripts, and indeed even in the ancient Leipzig edition, it is omitted; nor is it found in the Leiden manuscript, as the celebrated Dorp testifies, *loc. cit.* p. 124.
- **737 (LL 734)** The vulgate editions have *Thraces equos*. Valckenaer, in *Diatr. in Eurip. perd. dram. reliq.* p. 701, wished for *Thracis*, as cited by Dorp *loc. cit.*, who himself however prefers *Thracas equos*, from this passage of Ovid, *Met.* IX, 194: « Quid? quum Thracas equos humano sanguine pingues, Plenaque corporibus laceris praesepia vidi ». Dussen, p. 37, is in doubt whether it should be emended to *Thracis* or *Thracas*. I preferred *Thracis*, because I see it written healthy and well in the manuscript G. 1, which is truly excellent. For the rest, Heinsius on the cited passage of Ovid testifies that the form *Thraces* occurs quite often in the manuscripts of other poets. Statius, *Theb.* VI, 486: « sed Thraces equi ut videre jacentem Hippodamum; redit illa fames ». — The edition of Torinus and Spondanus has *Quos nec praecedere ventus*. Dorp as well as Dussen emend to *quos nec praecederet Eurus*, because poets are accustomed to compare swift running especially with the East Wind (*Eurus*). See Virg. *Aen.* VIII, 223, and Horat. *Carm.* II, 16, 24; and learned men in Virg. I, 317 wish to substitute *Eurus* for *Hebrus*. In this place there is no need even of conjecture, since all my manuscripts, and with them the old Leipzig edition, preserve *nec praecederet Eurus*, and Dorp testifies that the same is in the Leiden manuscript.
- **738 (LL 735)** *Non posset volucris* G. 2. Similarly Virgil, *Aen.* V, 242: « illa Noto citius volucrique sagitta Ad terram fugit ».
- **739 (LL 736)** *Tum quidam Argolicas* G. 2.
- **740 (LL 737)** *Nestoris accipit aetas* [the age of Nestor receives them], that is, the aged Nestor is the first to perceive them arriving, and gladly receives them, as can be understood from Homer, X, 532. In another place, v. 131, our author uses the abstract *prudentia Nestoris* [the wisdom of Nestor], where there is talk of giving counsel; here he uses the expression *aetas Nestoris* [the age of Nestor], regarding only his old age, because it especially befits old men to observe and praise the virtues and noble

| 745 | 748 | Inter se strident mucronibus, instat utriroque |  |
| 746 | 749 | Densa acies, mixtusque fluit cum sanguine sudor. |  |
| 747 | 750 | Tandem ferventi Danaum rex concitus ira | 750 |
| 748 | 751 | Antiphonem iagenti prostratum vulnere fundit, |  |
| 749 | 752 | Pisandrumque simul , fratremque ad bella ruentem |  |

### Notes

- **(cont.)** (cont.) deeds of young men.
- **745 (LL 742)** *Instauratque* G. 1.
- **746 (LL 743)** The editions read *Dardanium Danaumque*. Dussen, p. 38, thinks it should be changed to *Dardanidum*. But Virgil also is accustomed to call them *Dardanii*: — *Nubem telorum* [a cloud of missiles], if I remember correctly, Maro did not say, except from an antecedent comparison, *Aen.* XI [recte X], 808: « sic obrutus undique telis Aeneas nubem belli, dum detonet, omnem Sustinet ». — But Marius Victor says *densam telorum nubem* in *carm. ad Salmon.* v. 16. See vol. II of this work, p. 163. Ed.
- **747 (LL 744)** For *mixtis* I would urge reading *strictis* [drawn], because with only a single verse intervening there follows *mixtusque*.
- **749 (LL 746)** In G. 2, verses 753 and 754 are incorrectly inserted after this line.
- **751 (LL 748)** *Antiphonem*. In Homer, *Il.* XI, 101, I read that Antiphus and Isus, sons of Priam, were slain by Agamemnon. Whether our Homerist intended to name him or another, I still doubt. A. has *Amphionem*.
- **752 (LL 749)** The editions read *Tessandrumque simul*, and in the following verse *Hippolytum*; in place of which it is apparent that *Pisandrum* and *Hippolochum* must be put, from Homer, *Iliad* XI, v. 122, as the celebrated Bondam has already pointed out.
- **753 (LL 750)** Commonly is read *petit Amphimodonta*. G. 1 has *Aphidamantem*, which approaches closer to the true reading. For Bondam rightly showed, p. 169, that *Iphidamanta*, son of Antenor, should be restored here from Homer's *Iliad* XI, 221.
- **754 (LL 751)** In MS G. 1 this verse was omitted in the sequence and written below by a later hand in this manner: *Hic regis dextram gladio ferit, ille dolore*. For this reason it also seems to have been corrected by another hand. For the vulgate books have *Is fratris dextram gladio ferit*; Guelf. 2 has *Hinc fratris*, etc., and the Leiden manuscript is said to have *Hic fratris*. These words are either corrupt, or placed too concisely and obscurely by the author himself. From Homer's narrative, *Iliad* XI, 231 ff., the matter

|---|---|---|---|
| 752 | 755 | Acrior acoepto fugientem Antenore natum | 755 |
| 753 | 756 | Persequitur, traxitque ferox cum vulnere pccnas. |  |
| 754 | 757 | Hector tunc pugnae subit acri concitus ira |  |
| 755 | 758 | Priamides, mox perculsos agit undique Graios, |  |
| 756 | 759 | Nec Paris hostiles cessat prosternere turmas, |  |
| 757 | 760 | Eurypylique femur contento vulnerat arcu. | 760 |
| 758 | 761 | XII. Incumbunt Troes, fugiunt in castra Pelasgi |  |
| 759 | 762 | Yiribus exhaustis, et vastis undique firmant |  |
| 760 | 763 | Obicibus muros ; tum saxo Martius Hector |  |
| 761 | 764 | Perfringit portas, ferrataque robora laxat. |  |
| 762 | 765 | Irrumpunt aditus Phryges, atque in limine primo | 765 |

### Notes

- **(cont.)** (cont.) stood. After Agamemnon had slain Iphidamas, his elder brother Coon, deeply grieving at his brother's death and chancing to stand near Agamemnon, strikes the middle of his arm with a spear; but as he was dragging away his brother's body, Agamemnon soon slays him. Hence the celebrated Bondam emends: *Vis fratris dextram gladio ferit*; which he nevertheless thinks is even so expressed too concisely and obscurely. Dorp, however, prefers *Hic frater* (for *tum* or *tunc*), and supports his reading by the authority of the Leiden manuscript. Following the guidance of the manuscripts, I would prefer it thus: *Hinc frater regis dextram ferit*; for the addition *gladio* [with the sword] is superfluous and contrary to Homer, who writes that he struck him with a spear.
- **755 (LL 752)** *Antenore natum* [the son of Antenor], namely Coon, brother of Iphidamas.
- **756 (LL 753)** *Traxitque ferox cum vulnere poenas*, that is, Agamemnon, although wounded, fiercely continued vengeance, or proceeded to exact it, upon Coon. The Helmstedt MS has *Traxit ... graves cum sanguine poenas*. He seems to have taken the phrase from this passage of Virgil, *Aen.* V, 785: « Non media de gente Phrygum exedisse nefandis Urbem odiis satis est, poenam traxisse per omnes Relliquias ».
- **758 (LL 755)** *Mox perculsos agit*: so the editions of Torinus and Spondanus, and MS H. G. 1 and 2, A., and the Leipzig edition have *percussos*; Burmann the younger on *Anthol. Lat.* vol. I, p. 81, testifies that the same is in both manuscripts of the Leiden library, where he also proves by several examples that this confusion is customary. See Bentley on Hor. *Epod.* XI, v. 3. In this place I prefer *perculsos*.
- **759 (LL 756)** G. 2 writes *Turmas et turbas*.
- **760 (LL 757)** *Verberat arcu* H.
- **761 (LL 758)** *Fugiunt per castra* H.
- **762 (LL 759)** *Firmant Obicibus* [they fasten with bars]. As above, verse 683, « portas objecto robore firmant ».
- **763 (LL 760)** The vulgate editions read *Saxo Mavortius Hector*. I chose to write *tum saxo Martius*, because thus the connection is smoother, and my manuscripts lead the way: H. and G. 1 have *cum saxo Martius Hector*; G. 2 has *tum saxo Martius* or *maximus*. Barth, on the first verse of the *Thebaid*, observes on this and the following verse that the author, by frequently combining the letters *a* and *r*, sought harshness of sound for a terrible event.
- **764 (LL 761)** *Laxat et frangit* G. 2.
- **765 (LL 762)** *Limine et lumine* G. 2.

### Notes

- **766 (LL 763)** G. 2 has *Perstantes sternunt*.
- **767 (LL 764)** The printed editions have *Disturbant*; H. and G. 1 have *deturbant*; G. 2 has *proturbant*.
- **768 (LL 765)** *Geminat victoria vires* [victory redoubles strength], which the printed editions have, occurred above in v. 494; here I preferred, with H. and G. [1 and 2], to write *praebet victoria vires* [victory affords strength].
- **769 (LL 766)** For *turres*, the Helmstedt MS incorrectly has *puppesque*.
- **770 (LL 767)** The printed editions along with MS H. have *Facta testudine*, and G. 2 has *fracta testudine*; but *acta testudine* [advancing under a tortoise-formation] is more correct, which G. 1 exhibits, and which the celebrated Dorpius saw by conjecture, p. 126. Indeed, an imitation of Virgil also suggests this, *Aen.* II, 441: « obsessumque acta testudine limen »; and IX, 505: « Accelerant acta pariter testudine Volsci ».
- **771 (LL 768)** For *Ascendunt*, A. exhibits *Absconduntque aditus*, not badly; and H. has *et portas viribus instant*, while G. 1 and 2 have *et portis viribus instant*, which is perhaps the truer reading, because the author is accustomed to use *viribus* simply and without an adjective. See vs. 512.
- **772 (LL 769)** A. has *Fugiunt linquentes castra*. — This reading seems truer than *fugiunt omnes in castra*, which Wernsdorf has, and it is confirmed by what follows; therefore I have received it into the text. Ed.
- **773 (LL 770)** I preferred *Urget Trojana*, which H. and G. 1 maintain, to the vulgate reading *instat*, which just occurred.
- **776 (LL 773)** G. 1 has *Istic hostis et illic*.
- **777 (LL 774)** *Idomenei dextra*: thus I have placed from G. 1 and 2, in place of what the vulgates have, *Dextraque Idomenei*. For *cadit Asius*, G. 1 writes *cadit Iffirus*, in which monstrous word I do not know whether perhaps *Imbrius* lurks, whom Homer relates was the first slain in this clash by Teucer, *Iliad* XIII, 171; and thus instead of the name of Idomeneus, *Teucer* may have stood in the ancient copies in this manner: *Jam Teucri dextra cadit Imbrius*. Certainly, this Asius who is named first here, and before Amphimachus is slain by Hector, is said by Homer to have been killed long after Amphimachus, *Iliad* XIII, 384. Nor does our poet pass over the deed of Idomeneus in this battle, and he mentions next that Alcathous, the son-in-law of Anchises, was slain by him—a slaughter that was more memorable than that of Asius. Whence I scarcely doubt that this verse, as I conjectured,

### Notes

- **(cont.)** (cont.) [as I conjectured,] ought to be read, and that the names of Teucer and Imbrius ought to be restored to it.
- **778 (LL 775)** *Nec non incumbit in armis*. Thus the vulgates erroneously read. We must write *occumbit*, as I read in H., and just as the celebrated Dorpius testifies, page 127, is present in the Leiden MS and was conjectured without a manuscript by Schrader. G. 1 exhibits *Procumbit*.
- **779 (LL 776)** *Gener Achatius* is an unknown and erroneous name in the printed editions, for which Dorpius and Dussen correctly restore *Alcathous* from Homer; and my three manuscripts openly disclose the same name.
- **780 (LL 777)** *Ductor Rhodius* [the Rhodian leader] is in all the printed editions, and yet Idomeneus, who slew Alcathous, is called the leader of the Cretans by Homer, *Il.* XIII, 221, 259, 274, and in other places. Hence Dorpius, *loc. cit.*, correctly emends *Magnanimus ductor Cretum* [the magnanimous leader of the Cretans]; and he supports this not only by the reading of the Leiden University Library manuscript, which has *Rhaeteis* for *Rhodius*, but I can also confirm it from the two Wolfenbüttel manuscripts, both of which read *Retheus*. The Helmstedt MS has a similar but more obscure word. — For *Fervidus hasta*, *Fervidus ira* is read. This reading of the vulgate editions can indeed be defended, as the celebrated Dorpius notes, p. 128, by a passage of Virgil, *Aen.* VIII, 230: « ter totum fervidus ira Lustrat Aventini montem »; yet the true reading is *tunc fervidus hasta*, which he read added by a second hand in the Leiden MS, and which I likewise found in H. and G. 2.
- **781 (LL 778)** G. 1 has *Ascelaphum*. — *Mergitque sub undas* [and plunges beneath the waves] is an incongruous reading of the vulgates, because the Stygian waters certainly cannot be understood in this way. More correctly, with G. 1 and 2, I write *sub umbras* [beneath the shades]. — Moreover, these words are often interchanged by scribes and copyists.
- **782 (LL 779)** *Ubique ferus* is in the printed editions, but *ferox* in three manuscripts.
- **784 (LL 781)** G. 2 has *Fundit*.
- **786 (LL 783)** Instead of the vulgate reading *fluentis*, we should apparently read *fluento*, which H. and G. 2 have; L. has *fluente*. In G. 1, three verses are missing here because a page was trimmed by a careless hand.
- **788 (LL 785)** H. has *Immanat tellus*.
- **789 (LL 786)** I present these four verses as I received them emended through the care of the celebrated Bondam, p. 171 ff., from Homer's narrative, XIV,

| 792 | 795 | Pulsa metu, vallumque et muros aggere septos | 795 |

| 794 | 797 | Advolat interea Danaum metus, impiger Hector : |  |
| 795 | 798 | Confugiunt iterum ad classes Agamemnonis alae, |  |
| 796 | 799 | Atque inde adversis propellunt viribus hosiem. |  |

### Notes

- **(cont.)** (cont.) [XIV,] 462–488, so that I need not dwell on citing and correcting every single scribal aberration and monstrous form of word here. *Protenora* or *Prothenora*, which is in the vulgates, he thinks was written by the author himself, although according to Homer it should be written *Prothoenora*, because the author expressed the same name similarly above, v. 167: *Clonius dux atque Protenor*. But that the arrangement of names in that passage was corrupt, I demonstrated there from the manuscripts. Although Muncker, cited by Bondam, on Hyginus *Fab.* 86 and 97, affirms that synaeresis is frequently admitted by Latin writers in writing Greek names, how that can be recognized by us when the letters often remain the same, I myself find difficult to understand. We must rather recognize the custom of scribes copying ancient books, who usually shun diphthongs, and whose writing, where contraction seems to have occurred, must accordingly be restored to Greek grammatical usage.
- **790 (LL 787)** *Telamonius Ajax*. After this verse, v. 794 *Instaurantque* was rashly inserted in G. 2.
- **791 (LL 788)** *Sternit atrocem*. Here I would prefer: *quem sternit atroci Peneleus dextra*.
- **792 (LL 789)** H. has *Cecidit Priameia*; G. 2 has *dextra ruit hinc Priameia*.
- **793 (LL 790)** *Acrius insurgunt* [They rise up more fiercely]. For this G. 1 has *assurgunt*; G. 2 has *ad Dorica bella*. Through the author's excessive brevity and barren meagerness in narration, it happens that in this passage it cannot be perceived whence so sudden a reversal of affairs occurred, that the Trojans, who a little while before were slain and routed, now rise up more fiercely, and even drive the Greeks to their ships after inflicting great slaughter. Truly, at least briefly, the causes should have been touched upon which Homer invents: that Jove awakened restored courage to the Trojans, dissuaded Neptune from helping the Greeks any further, and that Hector was revived by Apollo and furnished with new strength to renew the fight. If the author had related these inventions of Homer more attentively than mere battles and slaughters, charges and retreats, he would have preserved more of the charm of poetry, and would not have narrated bare chronicle.
- **794 (LL 791)** *Instaurantque*. This verse is missing in G. 1. A. has *Pelopeia juventus*, which I would almost have preferred.
- **796 (LL 793)** G. 1 has *Fossa volvuntur in ipsa*.
- **799 (LL 796)** G. 2 has *Atque etiam adversis*; G. 1 has *hostes*.

### Notes

- **802 (LL 799)** G. 1 has *Obsistere viribus*.
- **803 (LL 800)** The printed editions have *Stans celsa in puppi* [standing high on the stern], taken from Virgil, *Aeneid* III, 527; but G. 1 and 2 have *Stans prima in puppi* [standing foremost on the stern], which seems more suitable to this passage. — *Incendia saeva Sustinet* [withstands the savage flames]. So Ovid, introducing Ajax speaking, *Met.* XIII, 7: « non Hectoreis dubitavit cedere flammis Quas ego sustinui ». Ed.
- **804 (LL 801)** *Defendit mille carinas* [Defends a thousand ships]. In Ovid, in the passage cited: « Nempe ego mille meo protexi pectore puppes ».
- **809 (LL 806)** G. 2 has *Armis protectus*.
- **810 (LL 807)** The vulgates along with G. 1 have *Advolat*; H. has *Provolat*; G. 2 offers both: and H. and G. 1 have *et falsa conterret*, instead of the vulgate *convertit*. G. 2 has *Una terrentur et conterret*.
- **812 (LL 809)** G. 2 has *Trepidi et timidi*. L. has *Fugientibus eminet*.
- **813 (LL 810)** Here the vulgates read *Perturbat velox acies*. The celebrated Bondam, p. 161, prefers *Proturbat*, that is, drives far in confusion and dislodges the battle-lines or the enemy from the ships. Yet to me it seems that *Perturbat* can stand in this passage, which the manuscripts also confirm. H., G. 2, and A. have *Perturbatque ferox aciem*, which is the true reading and suitable to the construction, from which the vulgate was corrupted. In G. 1 three verses are missing here.
- **814 (LL 811)** H. has *Pergit et ingenti*.
- **815 (LL 812)** The vulgates have it thus: *Et nunc hos gladio, nunc illos proterit arcu*. Here the use of the verb *proterit* [tramples down / crushes] seems entirely unusual regarding those slain by arrows, and was transferred here from verse 677, where however *proterit* must refer to the rock that Hector was hurling. Better, therefore, in this passage is the reading of codices H. and G. 2: « Et nunc hos cursu, nunc illos praeterit ardens » [And now in his course he passes by these, now those, glowing with zeal]. In this verse he seems to have looked to Virgil, *Aen.* IV, 157: « jamque hos cursu, jam praeterit illos ».

### Notes

- **816 (LL 813)** G. 1 has *Horrenti*; G. 2 has *Bellaque horrenda*.
- **818 (LL 815)** I gave *Conspexit fervidus* from H.; the vulgates have *respexit*.
- **819 (LL 816)** H. has *Vastisque horrendus*; G. 2 has *immanis* or *animosus*, and also transposes this verse with the following one.
- **820 (LL 817)** H. has *Vastoque hunc increpat*, but I retained *magnoque*, which is in A. and the vulgates, because *vastis* was already present in the preceding verse.
- **821 (LL 818)** G. 1 has *Huc age huc*; H. and G. 1 have *converte gradum*; others have *gradus*.
- **822 (LL 819)** G. 1 and L. have *Quid Troica dextera*, to which is added H., which has *Troa dextera*. Therefore I preferred that to the vulgate *Trojae dextera*.
- **823 (LL 820)** H. has *Et quantum bello valeat*; G. 2 has *in bello valeat*. I preferred this to what is in the printed editions, *quantum in bello possit*.
- **824 (LL 821)** *Nam licet ipse*. Barth proclaims these verses very beautiful, *Adv.* LIX, 15, p. 2808. But in truth the author owes them not to his own talent, but to Ovid's, whom he copied almost word for word, *Metam.* VIII, 394: « Ipsa suis licet hunc Latonia protegat armis, Hunc tamen invita perimet mea dextra Diana ».
- **825 (LL 822)** G. 1 has *Invito perimet tamen haec*, with the order of the words changed.
- **827 (LL 824)** A. and L. have *Ut qui mentitur*.
- **828 (LL 825)** G. 1 has *Tunc prius*.
- **829 (LL 826)** I gave *Dardanides* from G. 1 and 2. A. along with the printed editions has *Priamides*.
- **830 (LL 827)** *Et mutua dona* [And mutual gifts]. On these words Barth, *Advers.* LVIII, 14, p. 2752, notes that it is an idiosyncrasy of the author that he calls a blow a gift. But in fact Virgil also spoke thus, *Aen.* X, v. 881: « Desine, jam venio moriturus, et haec tibi porto Dona prius ». — So Catullus in the epigram to Calvus [c. 14, 20]: « Ac te his suppliciis remunerabor »; and Valerius Flaccus, V, 550: « Qui gemitus, irasque pares, et mutua Graiis dona ferant ». Jo. Schrader already pointed this out, *Observ.* I, chap. 3, page 35.

| 841 | 845 | XVIII. Tunc ut Pelidae rumor deverberataures, | 845 |

### Notes

- **(cont.)** (cont.) So also *gratiam referre* [to return thanks / repay a favor] is said in an unfavorable sense for revenge or retaliation. Terence, *Eun.* V, 3, 2: « qui referam illi sacrilego gratiam ». Ed.
- **831 (LL —)** *Objicit*. This verse is missing in G. 1, and in H. it is placed written in the margin. G. 2 has *Multo et magno cum pondere*.
- **833 (LL 829)** H. has *Cominus armis Inter se pugnant*; G. 1 has *annis*.
- **835 (LL 831)** A. and L. have *Simulantis pandit*.
- **838 (LL 834)** *Nudato pectore* [With bared breast]. Barth, *Adv.* p. 2808, thinks *pectora* should be read. But I judge *pectore* to be genuine, and *nudato pectore* to be ablative absolute. Namely, he pierces the youth with his sword after his breast had been bared by Apollo, as he stated in verse 836. For Homer relates that the armor was stripped from Patroclus by Apollo; the Latin Homerist, departing from him, says that it was stripped by Hector.
- **839 (LL 835)** The printed editions have *Trajicit, et victor*; G. 1 has *et victo*. H. has *Transfigit, victor*. G. 1 has *detulit arma*, but with *detrahit* written above.
- **840 (LL 836)** L. has *Vendicat exstinctum*, and in it this verse is subjoined: *Ingentes lacrymas gemitusque in corpore fudit*.
- **842 (LL 838)** H. and G. 2 have *Danai sua funera*. The printed editions have *Danai dum funera*.
- **844 (LL 840)** All the vulgate texts read *Testorides*. But learned men have long since conjectured *Nestorides*, that is, Antilochus, and Dorpius, *loc. cit.*, page 129, says this is confirmed by the Vossian codex; I can affirm the same concerning the three manuscripts that I use. — *In castra gerit* seems to be read incorrectly in the printed editions. G. 1, as also the Leiden MS, has *in castra refert*. The Helmstedt MS has *in castra trahit*. Moreover, here too the author narrates differently from Homer. For according to Homer, Menelaus and Meriones carried the body of Patroclus back to the camp, while Antilochus only carried the message of his death to Achilles.
- **845 (LL 841)** *Tunc ut Pelidae*. I have retained the vulgate reading; only I prefer *deverberat* [beats down / strikes upon] to *diverberat*. The Helmstedt MS has *Pelidis aures deverberat impius hor-*

## p. 587

PDF page 597.

| Verse | Wernsdorf | Text | Printed |
|---|---|---|---|
| 842 | 846 | Palluit infelix juvenis, caior ossa reliquit; |  |
| 843 | 847 | Membra siimil lacrymans materno tersit amictu |  |
| 844 | 848 | Deflens Aeacides tristi de caede sodatis, |  |
| 845 | 849 | Unguibns ora seeat, comptos in pulvere crines |  |
| 846 | 850 | Deformat, scindit filrmas de pectore vestes, | 850 |

### Notes

- **(cont.)** (cont.) [-ror]. G. 1 has *Tunc ut Pelide hic aures diverberat horror*. G. 2 has *Pelidis aures diverberat horror*. Barth, *Advers.* p. 2752, regards *diverberare aures rumorem* [that a rumor strikes the ears] as an idiom of our author. Yet other writers use similar expressions. In Plautus, *Amphitr.* I, 1, 177: « vox aures verberat »; and Lucan, VII, 25: « tuba verberat aures ». — In a fragment of Petronius, which we cited in vol. II, p. 128 of this work: « subitis rumoribus oppida pulsat ». Ed. — The same author, *Satyr.* ch. 68: « nullus sonus unquam acidior percussit aures meas ».
- **847 (LL 843)** The vulgate editions offer *Tristia membra simul materno nectit amictu*; where the words *tristia* and *nectit* seem inept. Helmstedt, G. 1, and 2 read: *Membra simul lacrymans*, which I have adopted, and the other part from G. 1, *materno tersit amictu*, unless perhaps *texit* is better. But as to *materno amictu* [with his mother's robe], that is, received from his mother, which our poet says, he has this without doubt from passages of other poets who say that mothers wove garments for their sons going into war, as Virgil of Lausus, *Aen.* XI, 818; Statius of Atys, *Theb.* VIII, 566, and of Parthenopaeus, the son of Atalanta, the same author, IX, 691; and perhaps the same was said somewhere of Achilles. In this passage, however, Homer makes no mention of a maternal garment.
- **848 (LL 844)** *Deflens Aeacides tristi de caede* [The Aeacid weeping over the sad slaughter]. I do not know whether we should entirely agree with Barth *loc. cit.*, who regards *deflere de caede* as an idiom of the author, and indeed as an unusual phrase. *Deflere* is to be taken in the sense of weeping abundantly, and fulfilling one's grief by weeping, just as *declamare*, *defatigare*, *detonare*, and similar words are used; while *de caede* is put for *ob* or *propter caedem* [on account of the slaughter]. Indeed, the verse itself appears so poorly composed and lifeless that I would prefer to eject it entirely, especially since almost the same thing is said in verse 852. The fact that G. 1 reads *tristis de caede* makes the verse not a whit better.
- **849 (LL 845)** *Comptos in pulvere crines Deformat* [He disfigures in the dust his well-groomed hair], I have written from G. 1, without a connective before *comptos*; the printed editions also omit it. The custom of sprinkling oneself with dust in mourning, not only among the Jews (as is well known from Sacred Scripture) but also in use among the Greeks and Romans, although examples of it are rare, is noted by Bernarts on Stat. *Theb.* III, 50; Statius touches upon it rather frequently, especially *Theb.* VI, 30: « sed et ipse exsutus honore Vittarum nexu genitor, squallentiaque ora Sparsus, et incultam ferali pulvere barbam ». — And compare Catullus in *Epithal. Pel.* p. 183, ed. Voss.: « Canitiem terra atque infuso pulvere foedans ». Ed. — Our poet also uses the verb *deformat* according to the custom of the ancients, who are especially accustomed to call those thus sprinkled with dust *informes* [disfigured]. Statius *loc. cit.*: « haustaque informis arena Questibus implet agros ». And Cornelius Severus, on the death of Cicero, v. 16: « Informes vultus sparsamque cruore nefando Canitiem ».
- **850 (LL 846)** *Scindit de pectore vestes* [He tears the garments from his breast]. This custom of all nations of tear-

### Notes

- **(cont.)** (cont.) -ing garments in mourning was illustrated by Barth in many places in his commentaries, and especially on this passage of Statius, *Theb.* IX, 353: « Exsiliit furibunda comis, ac verbere crebro Oraque, pectoraque, et viridem scidit horrida vestem ». Compare his notes on Stat. *Silv.* II, 1, 171. One may add Sil. Ital. XIII, 389: « Pulsato lacerat violenter pectore amictus ».
- **853 (LL 849)** *Ubi deponit questum* H.; *ubi depulsi questus* G. 1; *ubi depositi gemitus* G. 2. It seems preferable to read *gemitus*, because *questus* occurred just before.
- **854 (LL —)** *Tristis ait* [Sadly he says]. This verse is without doubt an interpolation and spurious. For it is absent from the Helmstedt codex and G. 1, and in G. 2 it is placed after verse 858 equally ineptly, an obvious sign of interpolation. And the word *ait*, repeated in verse 856, demonstrates that it was falsely inserted in this place. Therefore Barth (*Adv.* p. 2753) should not have taken offense at the word *cruciabere*, which occurs in this verse, as though it were put for *dolebis* and ran into a barbarism; nor is it necessary that we attempt, with the distinguished Anton de Rooy, *Spic. crit.* p. 99, to correct it to *obtruncabere*: for rather the whole verse should be rejected, and the word *cruciabere* in this sense seems to me to smack of something vulgar and barbarous.
- **856 (LL 851)** *Magnasque meo, violente, dolori Persolves poenas* [And great penalties shalt thou pay, violent man, to my grief]. Thus excellently Helmstedt, with which G. 1 and 2 agree (*magnoque ..... dolore*). How much worse are the vulgate editions! *magnoque meo, violento, labore Persolves poenas*. For the rest, Barth, *Adv.* p. 2753, thinks that the author intended *violentum* to be taken as an insult and reproach, and therefore seems to have written in an age in which such terms were customarily used less appropriately than was proper. I, however, consider that an insult and reproach are sought here in vain; rather he is reproached as *violentus* [violent] who is bold and reckless, and misuses his strength. Certainly Ovid, whom the writer also seems to have followed here, speaks no differently, *Met.* IX, 121: « Quo te fiducia, clamat, Vana pedum, violente, rapit ». — Nor does Tibullus speak otherwise of Mars himself, IV, 2, 3: « at tu, violente, caveto Ne tibi miranti turpiter arma cadant ». And Ovid, *in Ibin*, v. 20: « At tibi, calcasti qui me, violente, jacentem ». Ed.
- **857 (LL 852)** L. has *Persolvas poenas*. For *victor* I wonder whether *victus* might have been better; but the manuscripts agree.
- **859 (LL 854)** G. 1 and 2 have *Post haec*. The vulgate editions have *Post hoc*. *Accensus furiis* [inflamed with rage], Virgil, *Aen.* XII, 946.

### Notes

- **861 (LL 856)** G. 2 faulty: *Auxilii fulgari*.
- **862 (LL 857)** The Homerist here mentions *Aetnaeos ignes* [Aetnaean fires], imitating Virgil, *Aen.* VIII, vv. 419 ff., not Homer, who knows nothing of a burning Aetna and Vulcan's workshop within it. Thus in Reposianus, *De Concub. Martis et Veneris*, v. 163, Vulcan, in order to forge chains to bind the adulterers, « Antra furens Aetnaea petit ».
- **864 (LL 859)** *Mox effecta refert*: thus H., G. 1, and 2. The vulgate editions with A. have *Mox effecta ferens*.
- **865 (LL 860)** The vulgate editions have *Evolat ad Thetidem* [He flies off to Thetis], which is least of all suited to Vulcan, who is described by Homer as lame. — And for that reason he is called the slow-footed god (*tardipes Deus*) by Catullus, *Carm.* 31. Ed. — Hence G. 1 has *Advolat et Thetis*, which violates the meter; and G. 2 exhibits a double reading in its usual manner: *Et donat Thetidi*, while above it has *Et volat ad Thetidem*, as does also Helmstedt. The former is most fitting and has been adopted by me.
- **867 (LL 862)** *Illic Ignipotens*, that is, Vulcan. See what we said on verse 105. For *axem* I would prefer *axes*, which is in G. 2. H. writes *Arcem*. He seems to have in view Ovid, *Metam.* XIII, 110: « Nec clypeus vasti caelatus imagine mundi ». The words of the following verses which we have enclosed in brackets are to be deleted as spurious, which we demonstrated in the second Excursus.
- **870 (LL 864)** *Terras, et cinctum Nerea circum* [The lands, and Nereus girded round]: so all the printed editions with MS G. 2, which however also adds *tinctum*, absurdly. Guelf. 1, indeed, with A. and L., has *terris et cinctum*, which nevertheless cannot be admitted, since it is necessary here to name *terras* [the lands] as a distinct part of the universe, and Nereus, that is, the sea, cannot be said to be 'girded by lands', when on the contrary it girds the lands. So Ovid, *Met.* II, 6, whom our author without doubt had in view: « Aequora caelarat medias cingentia terras ». Priscian, at the beginning of the *Periegesis*: « Oceanum, tellus quo cingitur aequore tota ». And compare what we said on Lucilius' *Aetna*, v. 93. For this reason our author's phrasing *cinctum Nerea circum* may also appear suspect. Barth *loc. cit.* wants to read *et euntem Nerea*, which I certainly approve, and because I know nothing better, I have adopted it into the text. Yet *cinctus circum* might have been used by the author with an altered sense for *circumductus* [led around] or *circumfusus* [poured around], and so for *cinctum*

|---|---|---|---|
| 865 | 871 | Annorumque vices, dimensaque tempora noclis, |  |
| 866 | 872 | Quattuor et mundi partes, quanturo Arctos ab Austro, |  |
| 867 | 873 | Et quantum Occasus roseo distaret ab Ortu: |  |
| 868 | 874 | Lucifer unde suis, unde Hesperus, unus uterque, |  |
| 869 | 875 | Exoreretur equis ; quantus Sol orbe mearet , | 875 |

### Notes

- **(cont.)** (cont.) perhaps *ductum* or *fusum* might be substituted. Ovid, *Met.* I, 12: « Et circumfuso pendebat in aere tellus ». — The verb *cingere* seems occasionally to be used by poets for *circumducere* [to lead around], *circum adponere* [to place around], or *adjungere* [to attach around]. Thus Silius, VIII, 617: « Non totidem Ilva viros, sed lectos cingere ferrum »; although there they prefer to read *gignere ferrum*, or *stringere*. But Mela seems to have used *cingere* in precisely the same way as our author, Book III, ch. 1: « Restat ille circuitus, quem, ut initio diximus, cingit Oceanus », that is, leads around (*circumducit*). But here too Barth wishes to emend to *gignit*. See our note on Avienus' *Descriptio Orbis*, v. 78, which will be inserted in the following volume. Ed.
- **871 (LL 865)** We have written *Annorumque vices* [and the changes of the years] with the vulgate editions (*Astrorumque* G. 1), and *dimensaque* with G. 1 and 2. The vulgate editions have *diversaque*. G. 2 exhibits both readings.
- **872 (LL 866)** The printed editions have *Arcton ab Austro*: I corrected to *Arctos*.
- **874 (LL 868)** G. 2 has *Unde suis et Hesperus*. — A. and L. have *Unus et alter*, but what the rest have is correct, *unus uterque* [one and both]. For poets indeed distinguish Lucifer and Hesperus by name, but recognize them as one star. — See what we said in the Excursus to the *Elegia in obitum Maecenatis*, vv. 129–132, vol. II, p. 232 ff. of this work. Ed. — Seneca indicates this clearly in *Hippol.* 750: « Qualis est primas referens tenebras Nuntius noctis, modo lotus undis Hesperus, pulsis iterum tenebris Lucifer idem ». Because they mean that it is the same star, on that account they say that it uses a changing horse (or mounts another horse like a trick rider) when it comes forth as another. Statius expressly, *Theb.* VI, 237: « Roscida jam novies caelo dimiserat astra Lucifer, et totidem Lunae praevenerat ignes Mutato nocturnus equo; nec conscia fallit Sidera, et alterno deprenditur unus in ortu ». Since, therefore, Lucifer and Hesperus are distinguished by changed horses, hence our poet says that each arises with his own horses (*suis equis*), although others, including the Leipzig edition and G. 2, write *aquis* [from the waters], and the distinguished Anton de Rooy, p. 99, wishes to prefer that. Moreover, I observed that in Lucilius' *Aetna* verses are read to which our author seems to allude here. For that poet says in v. 168: « Hinc furtim Borea atque Noto, nunc unus uterque »; and v. 239: « Lucifer unde micet, quave Hesperus, unde Bootes ». Another similar passage of Lucilius we cite on the following verse. From this you might easily draw an argument that Lucilius was read by our author.
- **875 (LL 869)** The printed editions have *Exoriatur equis*, but in the judgment of Dorp, p. 129, it is better to read *Exoreretur*, which he says is extant in the Leiden codex, and I find in Helmstedt and G. 1; the nearby words *distaret*, *mearet*, *lustraret* also require this. Furthermore, H. and G. 1 read *et quantus in orbe mearet*; and A. and L. have *et quantum in orbe*; nor can it be seen to what *quantus* refers, since it cannot be understood of the preceding Hesperus, and there follows

## p. 591

PDF page 601.

| Verse | Wernsdorf | Text | Printed |
|---|---|---|---|
| 870 | 876 | Quantum et Luna cava lustraret lampade terras. |  |
| 871 | 877 | Addideratque freto sua numina, Nerea magnum, |  |
| 872 | 878 | Oceanumque senem , nec eumdem Protea semper, |  |
| 873 | 879 | Tritonesque feros, et amantem Dorida fluctus. |  |
| 875 | 880 | Terra gerit sihras, horrendaque monstra ferarum, | 880 |
| 876 | 881 | Fluniinaque et montes, cumque aitis oppida muris, |  |
| 877 | 882 | In quibus exercent leges animosaque jura |  |
| 878 | 883 | Certantes populi : sedet illic sequus utrique |  |

### Notes

- **(cont.)** (cont.) Luna [the Moon]. The vulgate reading *quantum vel in orbe mearet* can be valid only to the extent that *vel* is put for *et*, according to the custom of later Latinity (see *Pervig. Ven.* v. 53), and that it be corrected to *quanto vel in orbe mearet Luna*, that is, what great circuit the Moon completes in her course; as Lucilius in *Aetna*, vv. 228 ff.: « Solis scire modum et quanto minor orbita Lunae est, Haec brevior cur bissenos cita pervolet orbes, Annuus ille meet ». But a still deeper wound lies hidden in the verse. For when Lucifer and Hesperus, and then the Moon, have been named, why is he silent about the Sun, which ought especially to have been named and joined with the Moon, as was done in the cited verses of Lucilius and in Homer himself, *Il.* XVIII, 484, whom the author is expressing here? I strongly suspect, therefore, that that *vel*, which stands in the vulgate editions, arose from the word *Sol*, and that, with the help of the manuscripts that have *quantus*, we should read: *quantus Sol orbe mearet, Quantum et Luna cava lustraret lampade terras*; so that to the orb of the Sun, which is always full, the hollow lamp of the Moon may be opposed. For in the following verse the vulgate editions have *Luna cava et nitida lustraret*; and G. 1 *Quantum et cornuta lustraret*. Moreover, G. 2 has *caelum* for *terras*.
- **877 (LL 871)** H. has *Addideratque fretis*; G. 2 has *fortis*, most corruptly.
- **878 (LL 872)** The printed copies incorrectly read *Vertentem Protea semper*. Barth indeed, p. 2808, says that this is an elegant archaism (ἀρχαϊσμὸς) for *verso* or *vertibili*, but all the manuscripts which I use read far better, *nec eumdem Protea semper* [nor Proteus ever the same]. The Basel edition of Torinus also notes this in the margin.
- **879 (LL 873)** *Tritonesque feros* [And wild Tritons]. They are called *feri* [wild/beast-like] because in part they resemble a monster and have fish instead of feet. Thus Claudian calls Triton *ferus* and *semifer*, *De Nupt. Hon.* 138 and 145. After this verse G. 1 and 2 repeat, as I said in the Excursus, verse 869: « Fecerat et liquidas mira Nereidas arte ». It is omitted here, however, in the Helmstedt codex and the Leipzig edition.
- **880 (LL 875)** *Terra gerit silvas* [The earth bears forests]. In this and the following verse he seems to have had in view and almost expressed these lines of Ovid, *Met.* II, 15: « Terra viros, urbesque gerit, silvasque, ferasque, Fluminaque, et Nymphas, et caetera numina ruris ».
- **881 (LL 876)** *Cumque altis oppida muris* [And towns with lofty walls]. Above these words G. 2 superscribes another reading differing only in wording: *cumque altis moenibus urbes*.
- **882 (LL 877)** *Animosaque jura* [And spirited laws]. Barth, *Adv.* p. 2809, considers this to have been said most shrewdly, because they oppose themselves to vices far more powerful.
- **883 (LL 878)** H., G. 1, and 2 have *Aequus utrisque* [Fair to both sides]. In the printed editions it is *utrique*, namely to the plaintiff and defendant in each case.

### Notes

- **(cont.)** (cont.) [He seems to have alluded to] the hemistich of Virgil, *Aen.* III, 64: « Caeruleis maestae vittis ». — And of the Fates, Catullus, in *Epithal. Pel.* p. 186 Voss.: « At roseae niveo residebant vertice vittae ». Ed.
- **898 (LL 893)** *Immani turbine* [with an immense whirlwind]. The Helmstedt MS reads *cum magno turbine*. In G. 2 this verse is erroneously placed after verse 901.
- **899 (LL 894)** G. 1 and 2 read *Cum casta Pallade*. G. 2 has *Praebent*.
- **900 (LL 895)** G. 1 reads *Dant animos*. The Helmstedt MS has *Hunc contra Cythereius*.
- **901 (LL 896)** The Helmstedt MS reads *Sed enim non viribus aequus*. Thus in Maro, *Aen.* V, 809, Neptune says: « Pelidae forti Congressum Aeneam, nec Dis, nec viribus aequis ».
- **902 (LL 897)** Here four manuscripts, and likewise the old Leipzig edition, incorrectly show *corpus erat*. Helmstedt alone has *aequus* written above as an emendation or variant reading, and G. 2 has *compar* written above, which the editions of Torinus and Spondanus rightly preserved. Spondanus, however, transposed the words: *Aeacidae nec erat compar*, perhaps because he believed that in *compar erat* an error against the meter had been committed by the scribes. But Barth observes on this passage, *Adv.* p. 2809, that the author shortened the syllable in *compar*, according to the custom of later Latinity, just as Prudentius does in *Romanus*: « Meatus unus impar ad laudes Dei ». And Avianus similarly shortened it in two passages, *Fab.* XI, 5: « Dispar erat fragili et solidae concordia motus »; and *Fab.* XVIII, 10: « Tantorum solus viribus impar erat ».
- **904 (LL 899)** *Rector aquarum* [the ruler of the waters], Neptune, by whom Aeneas, having engaged with Achilles, was saved and snatched from the combat, as Homer relates, *Il.* XX, 325 ff., and Neptune himself boasts in Virgil, *Aen.* V, 804 ff.
- **905 (LL 900)** *Ut profugus laetis Trojam repararet in armis* [that as a fugitive he might restore Troy in joyful arms]. All the printed editions and most manuscripts read thus. But Barth already (*loc. cit.*) thought it should be emended to *in arvis* [in the fields], which I also find written in the margin of the Helmstedt codex. Moreover, the celebrated Bondam, p. 173, brilliantly conjectured *Latiis* for *laetis*, which indeed must be substituted if *arvis* is to be preferred to the vulgate *armis*. The scribe of the Helmstedt codex seems to have inclined toward this, writing the letter *a* above the word *letis*. Virgil speaks thus and quite frequently, when he says that Troy was brought to Latium, *Aeneid* I, 6; that Troy was to be restored in Italy, *Aen.* III, 504; that Ilium was carried into Italy, *Aen.* I, 68. And Ovid, exactly like our author, *Fast.* IV, 251: « Quum Trojam Aeneas Italos portaret in agros ». — See our edition of Ovid, vol. VI, p. 251. Ed.

## p. 594

| Verse | Wernsdorf | Text | Printed |
|---|---|---|---|
| 901 | 906 | Augustumque genus cseli submitteret astris , ' |  |
| 902 | 907 | Non clarae gentis nobis mansisset origo. |  |
| 903 | 908 | Inde agit Aeacides infesta cuspide Teucros, |  |
| 904 | 909 | Ingentemque manum prosternit caede virorum, |  |
| 905 | 910 | Sanguinis Hectorei sitiens : at Dardana pubes | 910 |
| 906 | 911 | XXI. Confugit ad Xanthi rapidos perterrita fluctus , |  |
| 907 | 912 | Auxiliumque petit divini fluminis : ille |  |
| 908 | 913 | Instat, et in mediis pugnatur gurgitis undis; |  |
| 909 | 914 | Ira dabat vires, stringuntur sanguine ripae, |  |

### Notes

- **906 (LL 901)** The Helmstedt MS reads *Claris committeret astris*, which is worse. *Submitteret astris* can be explained from this as 'send into the stars' or 'introduce to the stars through fame and glory'; yet I would prefer to interpret it as meaning that he brought the Augustan race into life and beneath the heavens, just as Virgil speaks of the Augustan line in *Aeneid* VI, 790: « Hic Caesar et omnis Iuli Progenies, magnum caeli ventura sub axem ». On the model of this passage, I thought our verse should read *caeli submitteret astris*. For *submittere* sometimes means to bring forth and shoot upward. Hence *submissae manus* or *palmae*, that is, uplifted hands, often occur in the poets, e.g., Sil. Ital. XII, 640. And Propertius, I, 2, 9, of flowers: « Adspice quot submittat humus formosa colores ». Furthermore, Barth *loc. cit.* deduces, not without reason, from this verse that this poem was written by a Roman, and while Rome was still flourishing under the ruling Augusti. — Wernsdorf reported this opinion of Barth in the *Prooemium* on the *Epitome Iliados*, at the beginning. Ed.
- **907 (LL 902)** G. 2 reads *Nec clarae*, incorrectly; and there this inept verse is appended: « Ni se proriperet curru quoque invisus abiret », which is a gloss contrary to Homer. Barth *loc. cit.* does not approve of that *clarae*, and thinks something else lies hidden in it: *Non Latiae*, or *Non Iulae gentis*. But there was no need to designate the Julian clan by any name other than the appellative adjective *clarae* [illustrious], since it could already be sufficiently understood from the preceding *Augustumque genus*. Moreover, the Julian clan was called *clara* preeminently (κατ᾽ ἐξοχὴν), because it was considered to have been especially proclaimed by the appearance of the Julian star, or the Dionean star, as Horace and Virgil call it. — Our author calls Aeneas the origin of the Julian race after the manner of Virgil, who says in *Aeneid* XII, v. 166: « Hinc pater Aeneas Romanae stirpis origo »; and *Aen.* I, 286: « Nascetur pulchra Trojanus origine Caesar Julius, a magno demissum nomen Iulo ».
- **909 (LL 904)** All my manuscripts read *Ingentemque modum*, which I think should not be rejected; and *modus* is repeatedly used for any magnitude or measure, as by Horace, *Sat.* II, 2, 36: « Scilicet illis Majorem natura modum dedit »; and II, 6, 1: « modus agri non ita magnus ».
- **913 (LL 908)** The Helmstedt MS reads *In mediis bellatur*.
- **914 (LL 909)** The Helmstedt MS reads *Stringuntur sanguine*, and Burmann the Younger, on *Anthol.* vol. I, p. 73, testifies that it is so read in two codices of the Leiden library. Others have *Tinguntur*, which I have abandoned.

- **919 (LL 914)** For *obruat*, Guelf. 2 puts *obpluat*, a verb that would not be unsuitable to this passage, if it were attested by the usage of another writer.
- **920 (LL 915)** *Impediatur aquis, sed vasto*: thus most vulgates and manuscripts. Leipzig only has *Impeditur*, and Helmstedt *Expediatur aquis et vasto*, which reading, being contrary to the other, betrays a hidden corruption. I read: *Impetuosus aquis, et vasto*, etc. I set forth the reasons for the emendation in Excursus IV at the end of this poem.
- **921 (LL 916)** G. 1 reads *Solvitur atque*.
- **922 (LL 917)** *Praetardatque gradus* is the reading of three manuscripts. The Annaberg edition has *Retardatque*. The vulgate editions have *Praetenditque*, less correctly. Statius, *Thebaid* II, 671: « Tardatique gradus ». But I see that the verb *praetardare* is absent from the lexicons.
- **924 (LL 919)** The printed editions, together with codex A., have *disjectis humeris*. I wrote *disjectos*, because three of my manuscripts show *dejectos*.
- **925 (LL 920)** G. 2 reads *Perpellit fluctus*.
- **926 (LL 921)** In the vulgate editions is read *Admonuit, rapidae quo cederet*. Helmstedt has *Adseruit, rapidae*, with *admonuit* written above. But more correctly G. 1 has *Adseruit, rapidae ne cederet*, that is, sustained, encouraged him. G. 2 has *Imbribus*, *ignibus*, *fluctibus undae*. Juno, moreover, assisted Achilles by rousing Vulcan to his aid to set fire to the banks of the river and the plain.
- **927 (LL 922)** *Sanctaque pugnarunt*, for which G. 1 has *Sacraque*. In this verse the author indeed touches too briefly upon the quarrels and battles in which the gods and goddesses fight over the Trojans—Mars with Minerva, Neptune with Apollo, Juno with Diana—in Homer, *Iliad* XXI, vv. 385–515.
- **931 (LL 926)** G. 2 reads *Vis ulla movet*.

### Notes

- **932 (LL 927)** G. 2 writes *Bellando vires succensus adauget*, but the vulgate editions are better. *Vires successus adauget* [success increases his strength] is the same as what he said above in vv. 494 and 768: *geminat victoria vires* [victory doubles strength].
- **933 (LL 928)** Indeed, all my books, except the editions of Torinus and Spondanus, express *Percussi*; yet I judged *perculsi* [stricken / dismayed] more suitable to this passage.
- **934 (LL 929)** I wrote *intra muros* with G. 1 and 2; the rest retain *inter muros*. — *Exhausta paene salute* [with safety almost exhausted], that is, when they had almost perished, and nearly all hope of safety had vanished. In Virgil it is called « absumpta salus », *Aen.* I, 555.
- **935 (LL 930)** See verse 683. Ed.
- **937 (LL 932)** *Quem non duri timor undique Martis* [whom fear of harsh Mars on every side (does not hold back)]: thus the printed editions with G. 1. G. 2 has *Quem nec duri*. Dussen, p. 40, prefers *duri furor undique Martis* [the fury of harsh Mars], because our author similarly said above concerning Achilles' valor: « Non illum vis ulla tenet ». On the other hand, the distinguished Dorp, p. 130, judges that it should be corrected to *durae mortis* [of harsh death], because it is so written in the Leiden codex, and Lucan says, I, 459: « Felices errore suo, quos ille timorum Maximus haud urget leti metus ». But I have found the same in the Helmstedt codex, and that reading is in a certain measure confirmed by the Virgilian verse, *Aeneid* II, 369: « ubique pavor, et plurima mortis imago ».
- **940 (LL 936)** *Ante oculos subito* [suddenly before his eyes]. Barth, *Advers.* p. 2809, considers this verse interpolated by a clumsy hand, and brought here from its proper place at v. 952. And that what he asserts is true is easily understood upon inspecting Homer, who records no other apparition of Pallas than that related by our author in verse 952. That this verse is suspect is also indicated by the fact that in three manuscripts which I use, it is placed in another location, namely after the following verse.
- **941 (LL 935)** All the manuscripts and printed editions read *Quem procul ut vidit tectum* [When from afar he saw him clad...]. The celebrated Ant. de Rooy, p. 100, wishes it emended to *Quam procul ut vidit tectam*, because he understood it of Pallas, who is named in the preceding verse. Now since we declare that verse spurious, the vulgate reading must be retained, which refers to Achilles. Hence *in caelestibus armis* [in heavenly armor] is to be understood of Achilles' armor forged by Vulcan, just as Maro, *Aen.* XII, 167, says of Aeneas: « Sidereo flagrans clypeo et caelestibus armis ». And Hector

### Notes

- **(cont.)** (cont.) was afraid at the sight of Achilles gleaming in his arms, as Homer relates, *Il.* XXII, 136.
- **943 (LL 938)** The Helmstedt MS and G. 2 read *Sequitur Nereius heros*. The printed editions have *Thetideius*. Below, in v. 980, he is likewise called *Nereius*; and Saleius, *Carm. in Pis.* v. 164, uses that name. In G. 1 this verse has been erased and deleted, for no apparent reason.
- **944 (LL 939)** G. 2 notes *Insomnis* and *insolita*. This verse—which should be punctuated differently from what the editions have and connected with the following lines—contains a simile employed by Homer, *Iliad* XXII, 199 ff., and Virgil, *Aen.* XII, 908, with dreamers who seem to themselves in running either to overtake or to flee another, yet, oppressed with sleep and languid, accomplish nothing. Our author seems to have had an eye to the passage of each, and to have wished partly to imitate them, but did not succeed.
- **945 (LL 940)** The editions read *Hic rursus super insequitur*. Barth *loc. cit.* rightly saw that we should read *Hic cursu*; but *super insequitur* still seems corrupt. Therefore I read with Helmstedt: *Hic cursu superum sequitur*, or *insequitur*, and I understand *superum* of him who is ahead and superior in the race, and who seems to flee.
- **946 (LL 941)** *Gressum labor ipse moratur* [toil itself delays their pace]. Barth *loc. cit.* teaches that it should be written thus, and codices H. and G. 1 show it, whereas the vulgates have *labor ille*, and G. 2 incorrectly *gressu labor ille movetur*. The author seems to have wished to express the thought of Maro in the same comparison, *Aeneid* XII, 909: « nequidquam avidos extendere cursus Velle videmur, et in mediis conatibus aegri Succidimus ».
- **947 (LL 942)** *Alternis poterant insistere coepta*. Thus it is written in the editions and G. 1. The others vary slightly: H. and G. 2 have *Alterius*; A. and L. have *Alternis poterant ut sistere*. *Insistere coepta* here means to pursue flight, to press on in the race, as Virgil said *viam insistere*, *Georg.* III, 164. But then the word *poterant*, and in the following verse *aderat*, by no means suit the passage, and, if I see anything, we should read: *Alternis properant insistere coepta periclis*, that is, both hasten to pursue the course they have begun, with alternating perils, now this one, now that one being endangered, or hoping for the danger of the other.
- **948 (LL 943)** *Nec requies aderat, furor undique concitat iras*. Thus the edition of Torinus reads. But H., G. 1, A., and L. have *timor undique*, which is more correct. For while both heroes fear lest one be overcome by the other, this fear sharpens anger in them and increases the contest. Guelf. 2 offers *concipit iras*, which I do not value highly. But in the first hemistich I think we should read: « Nec requies datur, et timor undique concitat iras ». And perhaps our author had in mind the flight of Daphne pursued by Apollo, described by Ovid, *Metam.* I, 539: « Sic Deus et virgo

- **951 (LL 946)** *Summa dies suprema luce*: thus the printed editions. But this is said tautologically (ταυτολόγως) and with little elegance, especially with the word *suprema* placed twice; manuscript A., however, has *extrema luce*, which I prefer; or, if you will, read: *Quem jam summa dies fatorum lege premebat*.

- **954 (LL 949)** The editions have *Nam nunc*. The Helmstedt MS has *Nam dum*, which I have preferred to the others. G. 1 has *Jam tum*. Leipzig has *Nam tunc*.

- **957 (LL 952)** *Intonat armis* [thunders in arms] is a Virgilian expression, *Aen.* XII, 700.
- **958 (LL 953)** *Nequidquam umbone repellit* [in vain drives back with the shield-boss], as Virgil, *Aeneid* II, 545: « rauco quod protinus aere repulsum, Et summo clypei nequidquam umbone pependit ».
- **959 (LL 954)** *Mutat congressibus ictus* [varies blows in the clashes], that is, engaging he delivers blows now in one way, now in another. These are alternating clashes, which Naso signifies in *Metam.* IX, 42: « Digredimur paullum, rursumque ad bella coimus ». In the margin of the Helmstedt codex this verse is added: « Indignatur eum sibi posse resistere Achilles ».
- **960 (LL 955)** *Sudor agit rivos* [sweat forms streams]. Virgil, *Aen.* V, 200: « sudor fluit undique rivis », and see v. 807.
- **961 (LL 956)** *Collatusque haeret* [and brought close he clings]. Virgil, *Aen.* X, 361: « Concurrunt, haeret pede pes, densusque viro vir ». Yet he had more in mind Ovid, *Metam.* IX, 44: « eratque Cum pede pes junctus: totoque ego pectore pronus Et digitos digitis, et frontem fronte premebam ». In the Leipzig edition, this verse is subjoined, which has already appeared: « Alternis poterant insistere coepta periclis ».
- **962 (LL —)** *Interea validam* [Meanwhile a mighty...]. This verse is omitted in H. and G. 1, but it cannot be spared.

### Notes

- **963 (LL 958)** *Inque virum* [And against the hero]. MSS Helmst. and G. 1 contract this verse with the preceding one (which they had omitted) in this manner: *Inque vicem magnis emisit viribus hastam*. The Leipzig edition, however, inserts after *viribus egit* this line: *Hastam, quam manibus saevis vibravit Achilles*, which is a manifest gloss.
- **964 (LL 959)** I have followed the reading of MSS H., G. 1, and the Leipzig edition. The rest read *Quam post elapsam*.
- **967 (LL 962)** *Inflectitur auro* [is bent by the gold]. The distinguished De Rooy, p. 100, substituted *aere* [by bronze], because Virgil, *Aen.* II, 545: « quod protinus aere repulsum »; and Homer, *Iliad* III, 348, attributes bronze (χαλκόν) to the shield. But on that account *auro* should not be altered. For the discussion is of the Vulcanian armor, which our author stated above at v. 863 to have been crafted from gold. Nor is it uncommon for ancient heroes to bear shields embossed with gold. Ovid, *Metam.* VIII, v. 26: « Seu sumpserat auro Fulgentem clypeum, clypeum sumpsisse decebat ».
- **968 (LL 963)** *Dissiluit mucro* [The blade shattered]. This seems somewhat abrupt, so that I almost suspect some verse has fallen out. For earlier there was mention of the hurled javelin, now of the *mucro*, that is, the sword. And in *Dissiluit* the author indeed seems to express what happened to Turnus in Virgil, *Aen.* XII, 739: « postquam arma Dei ad Vulcania ventum est, Mortalis mucro, glacies ceu futilis, ictu Dissiluit ». But here a blade that is bent by the gold—that is, blunted or deflected—cannot properly be said to have shattered (namely into pieces), but to have fallen down. Therefore I am almost inclined to think we should defend the reading displayed by the old Leipzig edition alone, *Desiliit mucro* [the blade leaped down], or, what would be better for the connection, *Desilit*, or *Decidit et mucro*. But content to have observed this, since other manuscripts do not support it, I attempt nothing further.
- **969 (LL 964)** For *collatis*, H. has *collectis*. G. 2 has both *collatis* and *collectis*.
- **970 (LL 965)** The editions of Torinus and Spondanus read *Inque vicem strictos commutant*. But Barth, *Adv.* LVIII, 14, p. 2753, considers *commutare enses*, for striking each other with mutual sword-blows, to be a mere Germanism. This also seemed suspect to Dussen, who prefers *committunt* in the cited work, p. 40. But my manuscripts G. 1 and 2 read *Inque vicem duros evitant cominus enses*. H., A., and L. have *duros commutant*. Therefore I retain *duros evitant*, and interpret it to mean: charging together with their swords, they fence for a long time in such a way that they avoid each other's blows in turn and receive no wounds. For afterwards Hector again yields and flees. You could also, following the model of verse 959, re-

Hon. et Mar.* v. 86: namely, to brandish and return mutual and crisscrossing blows. Swords and rays equally graze and are grazed in turn, flash and strike back, and are thus "exchanged." Ed.
- **972 (LL 967)** Tor. and Sp. and MS G. 2 read *Horruit instantem*; but G. 1, A., and L. have *Instantem Aeaciden*; H. has *Instantemque Aeacidem*. This seems to have crept in from a gloss, although that *Horruit* is not entirely satisfactory either. Perhaps you might reconcile both readings thus: « sortemque supremam Horret in Aeacide ». Next, for *defectis viribus*, Burmann on *Anthol. Lat.* I, p. 89, emends to *defectus*.
- **973 (LL 968)** *Fraternaque fessus in armis* [and weary in his brother's armor]. This reading of the common editions offended Dussen and Dorp alike. The former wants *pressus in armis*, taking *pressus* here to denote someone who, driven into straits, is pursued by the enemy with swift step and pressed to battle; and he supports this with a passage of Virgil, *Aen.* I, 471. But what he wished to express by introducing the word *pressus*, the distinguished Dorp provided much better, guided by the Leiden manuscript, writing *rebus in arctis* [in dire straits]: which certainly common formula is proven by him from passages of Claudian, *de IV Cons. Hon.* 407, and *in Eutrop.* II, 354. In addition, there are three of my manuscripts, which individually display *rebus in artis*; A. has *fraternaque in artis*, *rebus* having been omitted through error, and the Leipzig edition corruptly has *rebus in armis*.
- **975 (LL 970)** *Sentit adesse* G. 2. — On the phrase *quid agat?* [what is he to do?], one should compare what was noted in volume II of this work, Eleg. XII, v. 37, p. 301. And just as it is said here, *quae numina supplex Invocet?* [what deities should he as a suppliant invoke?], so above Reposianus, v. 145: « Quod numen poscat? » Ed.
- **976 (LL 971)** The editions of Tor. and Sp., along with G. 2, write *In toto languescunt*; but I thought that what the other ancient books, H., G. 1, A., and L., preserve, *et toto*, should be preferred.
- **979 (LL 974)** The editions of Tor. and Sp. present *Defenso auxilium*; but learned men easily detected a flaw in that reading. Dussen, p. 42, emends to *deprenso*, saying that *deprensus* is used of one who is so enclosed and oppressed that every way of escape is barred, and adds passages from Virgil, *Aen.* VIII, 247; Catullus XXV, 15; Propertius II, 25. A more certain emendation, and one necessary to the author's intention, was given by Dorp, p. 131: *Defesso auxilium*, confirmed by the Leiden manuscript and, as I may add, by four

The distinguished Dorp, p. 132, thinks this scarcely Latin expression should be emended thus: *avidosque canes tua viscera pascent* [and your entrails will feed greedy dogs], just as Ovid, *in Ib.* 194: « Hic inconsumpto viscere pascet avem ». And indeed *pascere* in an active sense for *vorare* [to devour] scarcely occurs among good authors, though the compound *depascere* occurs in Columella VII, 5; and in a writer of this sort, not all words can easily be held to the standard of the best usage. Therefore I judge that this matter should be left open.

- **1002 (LL 997)** H. has *Hunc animi*; G. 2 has *et animus ... Achillis*. At the words *nondum satiatus* [not yet satisfied], I am reminded of the epigram which is extant in *Anthol. Lat.* book I, 94, and entitled *On the Dragging of Hector*: « Funere turbat equos necdum satiatus Achilles, Hector et exanimis funere turbat equos ». In this epigram I am bothered by the word *turbat*, which anyone will deem wrongly said if he compares our epitomator. For our author says in v. 1005 that Achilles' horses were not troubled or terrified, but rather pranced more proudly and haughtily because of Hector's corpse. Hence perhaps instead of *turbat* one should read *tardat* [slows], after the example of Statius, *Achill.* I, 88: « modo crassa exire vetabit (Achilles) Flamina, et Hectoreo tardabit funere currus ».
- **1003 (LL 998)** H. and G. 1 have *Deligat ad currum*, which I preferred to the vulgate *Adligat*.
- **1004 (LL 999)** *Ter circum muros victor trahit* [Thrice around the walls the victor drags him]. Here our author once again rendered Homer not from Homer, but from Virgil, who in *Aen.* I, 483, says: « Ter circum Iliacos raptaverat Hectora muros »; but Homer himself writes that, bound to the chariot, he was dragged by Achilles thrice around the mound of Patroclus, not the walls of Troy. Other ancient Greek and Latin poets report that he was dragged around the walls, but they are silent concerning the number three. See Ovid, *Metam.* XII, 591, and *in Ib.* 336. - **(cont.)** (cont.) *Achille*, note H, observes that the author of the *Ilias Latina* is the only one of the poets after Virgil who reports this; and he adds that Ausonius did the same in the *Periocha* of Book XXII of Homer's *Iliad*, and was for that reason criticized by Mariangelus. This agreement between Ausonius and our Homerist on such a singular point is such that we might suppose the author of the Epitome, if not Ausonius himself, was at least the same as the author of the *Periochae*, since the *Periochae* are not attributed to Ausonius with complete certainty.

For poets make the horses of heroes into a kind of diviners of future events. The same scholar noted similar things on Stat. *Theb.* I, 275, and on the last verse of Book I of Claudian's *de Raptu Proserp.* There is a similar expression in this verse of Claudian, *de Cons. These I indeed judge should be followed, because it is probable that later poets, following Virgil, turned the threefold dragging of Hector around Patroclus' tumulus (which Homer relates) into as many rounds about the walls of Troy. Since our author has already indicated these above, he does not seem to have meant them in this place.

The subject is the chariot race, in which Diomedes defeated Meriones, who is here absurdly called *pedibus ferox* [fierce of foot], or *velox* [swift], as is read in the Leipzig edition. I determine that it should be read thus: « Tydides circi cursu pedibusque ferorum Merionem superat » [Tydides surpasses Meriones in the run of the circus and by the feet of his steeds]. — Schrader, *Emend.* ch. 8, p. 160, notes that *circus* is used by the poets of hippodromes. Ed. — He said *ferorum* [of beasts] for *equorum* [of horses], following the example of Virgil, *Aen.* II, 51; V, 818; Manilius Book V, 76, where of the circus charioteer himself: « Aut quum laxato fregerunt cardine claustra, Exagitare feros, pronumque anteire volantes »; add Petronius ch. 89, in the *Trojae halosis*, v. 12, and Ausonius, who speaks of Diomedes' *feros*, that is, horses, *Epist.* 24, 17. Having thus emended the verse, though from conjecture alone, I placed it in the text so that it might be understood.
- **1016 (LL 1011)** *Superavit Epeus*: thus Dorp and Dussen emended, and so Homer informs us, *Iliad* XXIII, 665. In the vulgate editions and manuscripts we find *Ephoebus, Ephebus, Ephemus*.

This reading seems to stem from a correction by a scribe who, believing *adflicti genibus* to be unusual, thought it should be exchanged with the frequent formula *adflictis rebus*; for after *adflictis* there is a lacuna or blank space in the manuscript. Dussen, p. 45, offended by the same thing, prefers *adfusi genibus*, which is in H., or *Et patris adflicti gemitus miserere*. I think nothing should be changed, and with Barth, commentary on Statius, vol. III, p. 394, I explain *adflicti genibus* as driven to your knees, affixed as if by some force. Thus in Suetonius, *Jul.* 20, we read: « ad genua accidere ».
